# Detailed curriculum: 2.0a Numerical methods, floating point and Monte Carlo, and 2.0b Optimisation

Blueprint work file, Stage 2 Phase 1. Draft 1, 7 October 2026.

**Source of truth.** The owner adopted both items at the Blueprint review of 7 October 2026 (`docs/PLAN.md` 12, decision 2): **2.0a Numerical methods, floating point and Monte Carlo** (120 h, canon-gap proposal P3, the first item of Stage 2, all tracks) and **2.0b Optimisation** (80 h, canon-gap proposal P4, after 2.0a and before 2.1, all tracks). Their scope, hours split, canonical resources and exit lines come from `docs/blueprint/work/canon-gap.md` sections G1, G2 and 6. This file expands them into courses, levels, lessons, homework, topic exams, the rebuilt exits, reading and verification, and writes one lesson in full. `docs/curriculum.md` does not yet list either item; `docs/CURRICULUM.md` must add them (section 0.5, request 6).

> **Owner decisions this file follows (7 October 2026).** (1) Engine and every P0 widget batch before content; content depth-first from 0.1. (2) P3 and P4 adopted at 120 h and 80 h; P8 (differential equations) **not** adopted, so 2.0a teaches the differential-equation basics its PDE course needs (decision 6). P5 to P16 not adopted, so nothing here assumes P6's portfolio course or P12's execution course. (5) **Artifact publishing rule:** the Artifact publishes only files built from this repository's own sources and has **no Python**; every Python drill in this file hands off to the web app and returns by result code. Lessons are unaffected: every number on a lesson screen comes from our own JavaScript models, and authored code output is recorded at build time. Maths renders as MathML in the Artifact. The web app keeps full Pyodide with every load group.

**Built on.** `docs/blueprint/work/canon-gap.md` (G1, G2, sections 5 and 6, the resource canon and the solutions table); `docs/CURRICULUM.md` (index, conventions, the review's skill-granularity and Python-floor notes); `docs/curriculum/stage1-probability.md` (the model for structure and depth); the sibling files whose lessons this file builds on and must not repeat: `stage0-number-algebra.md` (0.1 lesson 42, binary floating point), `stage1-python-tools.md` (1.1 lessons 4, 5, 66, 67, 69 to 75; 1.2 lesson 21), `stage1-calculus.md` (1.3 lessons 27, 33, 50 to 53, 75, 84, 86, 111, 117, 124 to 128), `stage1-linear-algebra.md` (1.4 lessons 12, 16, 48, 50 to 52, 67, 77 to 84, 88, 96, 98), `stage1-probability.md` (1.5 levels V5 to V7, J3, J4, J6, Z3, lesson 140 and the P2 extension), `stage1-finance-projects.md` (1.7 levels OP1 to OP3; 2.3 levels OA2, OA5, OA6), `stage1-dsa-cpp.md` (1.9 level CL4), `stage2-ml-papers.md` (2.1 levels LD4, LS4, CP5, TN6); `docs/WIDGETS.md` (widget ids); `docs/PLAN.md` 3.2 (scenes) and 5.7 (Python load groups); `docs/CONTENT-FORMAT.md` (ids, screen types, checks); `docs/ASSESSMENT.md` (the `topic` paper kind as revised, exit parts, homework rules as revised); `docs/AI-TUTOR.md` (Bertie's availability); the owner's lesson-creator, maths-teaching, code-craft and project-forge skills.

**Tags.** [check] marks a resource detail (a chapter, section, lecture or problem-set number or title, a URL path) that I recall but could not confirm from this environment; confirm it before content cites it. [recalled] marks a fact from memory that I am confident of. [unverified] marks something not yet tested (cvxpy importing and solving inside Pyodide). [verified] marks a number recomputed on 7 October 2026 with Python 3.11.15, NumPy 2.4.6, SciPy 1.17.1, SymPy 1.14.0 and mpmath 1.3.0; **every numeric answer in sections 2 (hooks), 3, 4 and 7 is [verified]** and the tag is not repeated on each. The CI interpreter is CPython 3.12 with `tools/py/uv.lock` (`docs/CURRICULUM.md` review note 4); these runs stand as a record and are rerun at `docs/PLAN.md` stage 26. [mode to add] marks a widget mode `docs/WIDGETS.md` does not list; section 8.2 collects them. [our choice] marks a decision without outside evidence.

2.0a exit (`2.0a.exit`): the topic exam at 70% with its cancellation slot at 70%; the four-pricer error-against-work drill and the implied-volatility grid drill, all hidden tests passing (section 4.2). 2.0b exit (`2.0b.exit`): the topic exam at 70% with its duality slot at 70%; three convexity proofs at 7 of 10; the duality, convergence and portfolio drills, with the portfolio's dual values explained at 7 of 10 (section 4.4).

## 0. Scope, decisions and conventions

### 0.1 Decisions

| # | Decision | Chosen | Rejected | Reason |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | Unit of lesson time | **15 minutes per lesson, quiz included**, as every sibling file | 10 or 12 minutes | One unit across the path keeps hours tables comparable (`docs/CURRICULUM.md`, writing conventions) |
| 2 | One file for two items | **Both items here**, numbered separately (2.0a lessons 1 to 91, 2.0b lessons 1 to 58) | Two files | 2.0b builds directly on 2.0a: conjugate gradients (2.0a lesson 46) is an optimiser, Newton's method for systems (2.0a lesson 19) is Newton's method for minimisation, and conditioning (2.0a level NL1) sets every rate in 2.0b level DA1. One file keeps the cross-references checkable, as `stage2-ml-papers.md` does for 2.1 and 3.1 |
| 3 | 2.0a course structure | **Four courses on canon-gap's four hour blocks**: EA Error, Equations and Integrals (floating point and error 12 h, equations, interpolation and quadrature 18 h), NL Numerical Linear Algebra (30 h), PD Finite Differences and the Pricing Equation (25 h), MO Monte Carlo Methods (35 h) | Five courses with floating point alone (13 lessons, too short to carry a homework rhythm); one course per Süli and Mayers chapter | The blocks are canon-gap's (G1, "Hours"); each course has one spine (decision 5). EA puts floating point first because every later level measures its errors in the units EA defines |
| 4 | 2.0b course structure | **Four courses on canon-gap's four blocks**: LP Linear Programming and Duality (20 h), CX Convexity and Optimality (25 h), DA Descent Algorithms (20 h), AP Optimisation at Work: Portfolios and Learning (15 h). **LP before convexity** | Boyd and Vandenberghe's order (convex sets first, LP as a special case); three courses merging DA and AP | LP is concrete, two-dimensional and drawable, and its duality has a picture (a bound built from constraints) before the abstract Lagrangian arrives in CX3. The four courses are short (11 to 18 lessons) because 80 hours hold four distinct stories; `docs/CURRICULUM.md` 1 notes design research 8.2's 20 to 45 is a guide, not a rule |
| 5 | Spines | **One spine per course** (canon-gap decision 1). EA: Süli and Mayers, *An Introduction to Numerical Analysis* (CUP 2003). NL: Trefethen and Bau, *Numerical Linear Algebra* (SIAM 1997). PD: Wilmott, Howison and Dewynne, *The Mathematics of Financial Derivatives* (CUP 1995). MO: Glasserman, *Monte Carlo Methods in Financial Engineering* (Springer 2003). 2.0b: Boyd and Vandenberghe, *Convex Optimization* (CUP 2004, free PDF), except course LP, whose spine is **Vanderbei, *Linear Programming: Foundations and Extensions*, 5th ed** (Springer 2020), because Boyd and Vandenberghe do not teach the simplex method | Boyd and Vandenberghe for course LP; Nocedal and Wright as the 2.0b spine | Canon-gap names these works; it lists Vanderbei as stretch, so this file asks canon-gap to change its role to `basis` for course LP (section 0.5, request 1). Nocedal and Wright stays the algorithms reference (stretch) |
| 6 | Differential equations without P8 | **2.0a teaches what its PDE course needs**: the heat equation as the limit of a random walk, Fourier modes and separation of variables (needed for von Neumann stability), the Black-Scholes equation derived from a binomial tree by Taylor expansion (no Itô calculus), the change of variables to the heat equation, boundary and terminal conditions (level PD1), and stiff ordinary differential equations with stability functions (lesson 54). **Not taught**: second-order linear equations, phase planes for systems, Runge-Kutta beyond one edge screen | Assuming P8's 45 hours; deriving Black-Scholes by Itô's formula | Canon-gap G1 listed P8 as a prerequisite; the owner did not adopt P8. The tree derivation needs only Taylor's theorem (1.3 level X3) and the risk-neutral tree (1.7 level OP1), so every learner can follow it; 3.2 (optional) derives the same equation by hedging. The omitted topics are not used by any 2.0a lesson or the exit |
| 7 | Boundary with Stage 1, 2.1 and 2.3 | **2.0a and 2.0b do not re-teach** the first contacts listed in section 0.2; they prove what those lessons measured and go where they stopped. **2.3 levels OA2, OA5 and OA6 become applications** of 2.0a on real option chains (request 2); **2.1 levels LD4, LS4, CP5 and TN6** recap 2.0b instead of introducing it (request 3) | Teaching the solvers again in 2.3 and 2.1 | 2.0a and 2.0b now precede 2.1 and 2.3 on every track. Canon-gap 8.2 already counted these single preview lessons as inside the sibling files' hours, so nothing here double-counts them |
| 8 | Code and runtimes | **Every code drill runs in Pyodide on the web app only.** In the Artifact a drill shows "This drill runs in the web app" with a handoff link and returns by result code (`ASSESSMENT.md` 14.3; owner decision 5). Drills declare `needs`: 2.0a uses `numpy-pandas` and `scipy-statsmodels`; 2.0b also uses a new **`optimisation`** group (cvxpy-base with Clarabel, and highspy; present in Pyodide 314.0.7's lock file [measured by canon-gap 5.3], import and solve in a browser [unverified]). **No code item in lesson quizzes**: lessons use `predict-output` with output recorded at build time. CI runs every drill on CPython 3.12 and Pyodide | Code items in quizzes; MicroPython | The Artifact has no Python under the publishing rule. A quiz item that hands off would make a 15-minute lesson depend on a second app. Lesson screens compute in JavaScript (`@zq/models/numerics`, `@zq/models/optim`, section 8.3), with Python parity tests |
| 9 | What a floating-point item may ask | **Only results every faithful implementation agrees on.** Basic operations ($+$, $-$, $\times$, $\div$, $\sqrt{}$) are correctly rounded by IEEE 754 and identical in CPython, NumPy and JavaScript; an item may ask for their exact output. An item that depends on the last bit of `exp`, `log`, `cos` and the like is forbidden unless every library must agree (as when $\cos 10^{-8}$ rounds to 1.0); CI evaluates each such answer in CPython and in the app's `dmath` and fails the item if they differ | Grading any printed double | Transcendental functions are not required to be correctly rounded, so their last digit varies between libraries; an item keyed to one library would fail a correct learner on another |
| 10 | Exactness and simulation | **A simulated estimate is never graded** (widget library 1.3). Monte Carlo screens grade a prediction made before the run, or an exact quantity (a standard error from a formula, a variance ratio from a closed form). Reference values for floating-point questions are computed in exact rational arithmetic or with mpmath at 50 digits | Grading an estimate within a tolerance | A tolerance on a random estimate fails honest learners on unlucky seeds or is too wide to teach anything |
| 11 | Proof standard | **2.0a: Süli and Mayers and Trefethen and Bau.** Derive error terms, orders, stability conditions and bounds; prove backward stability results only in the simplest cases (one operation, a sum, back substitution) and state the rest. **2.0b: Boyd and Vandenberghe.** Prove convexity, derive duals, solve KKT systems, prove the linear rate of gradient descent on a quadratic. One long-form item with a 10-point rubric in every homework set; 32 of 80 marks long-form in each exam | Recipe courses; Higham's full error analysis | Interviews and research ask why a scheme is stable and why a problem is convex. Full backward-error proofs of LU and QR are Higham's and Trefethen and Bau's, listed as stretch |
| 12 | Transfer | **At least one quant or ML-lab screen per level**; each level's table is followed by its transfer screens | Applications left to 2.1 and 2.3 | Numerics and optimisation are where pricing, risk, portfolio construction and training break or work |
| 13 | Notation | 2.0a: $u = 2^{-53}$ (unit roundoff of binary64; machine epsilon $2u$ is named once and not used), $\operatorname{fl}(\cdot)$, $\operatorname{ulp}(x)$, $\kappa$ for condition numbers, $r = \frac{\Delta\tau}{\Delta x^2}$, $g$ for amplification factors, $N$ for paths or grid sizes as stated, $\tau$ for time to expiry. 2.0b: Boyd and Vandenberghe's ($p^\star$, $d^\star$, $\boldsymbol\lambda$ for inequality and $\boldsymbol\nu$ for equality multipliers, $\succeq$ for positive semidefinite, $\mu$ and $L$ for strong convexity and smoothness, $\kappa = \frac L\mu$) | Mixing "epsilon" conventions | Two common meanings of machine epsilon differ by a factor of 2; a fixed symbol avoids the off-by-two error in every bound |
| 14 | Calculators and code in papers | The in-app scientific calculator is allowed on lesson screens, homework and both topic papers; no code runs during a paper (items may ask the learner to read code) | No calculator | Numerical analysis computes; the papers test judgement, orders and derivations, not arithmetic speed |
| 15 | Sample lesson | **2.0a lesson 9, "Catastrophic cancellation"** (`lsn.approx.cancellation`), in full in section 7 | 2.0b lesson 12, "The dual as the best bound"; 2.0a lesson 56, the explicit scheme | Cancellation is the 2.0a exit's third task, the root of every later stability argument (Welford, log-sum-exp, finite-difference Greeks, the normal equations), and the first lesson where a learner's correct formula gives a wrong number. 2.0b lesson 12 is the next to write in full |
| 16 | Display codes | **Two letters**: EA, NL, PD, MO (2.0a); LP, CX, DA, AP (2.0b) | One letter | None appears in `docs/CURRICULUM.md` section 1; ids do not depend on them |
| 17 | Tracks | **Every track takes both items in full** (owner decision 2: all tracks) | Track cuts | Traders price and hedge with these methods, researchers optimise portfolios and fit models, developers implement both, ML-lab work trains with them |
| 18 | Bertie | Built in from the first lesson: every screen carries `tutor.focus`, `tutor.notes` and `tutor.avoid`; away during both topic papers; never states an answer before Check. Numerics guidance: prefer "is the error in the problem or in the algorithm?", "which step rounds?", "what happens to the error when you halve the step?", "what is the condition number here?". Optimisation guidance: prefer "which constraints are binding?", "what would a multiplier of zero mean?", "is the feasible set convex?" | | The `secret()` block carries exact values and solver outputs under the never-reveal rule (`AI-TUTOR.md` 5.5) |
| 19 | New widgets | **Ten new widgets, all scenes, all P2** (section 8): `backward-error`, `interp-lab`, `convergence-plot`, `fd-grid`, `rng-lab`, `mc-variance` for 2.0a; `feasible-region`, `dual-pair`, `convex-set`, `frontier-lab` for 2.0b. **29 modes added to 16 existing widgets** (section 8.2), and 12 existing Stage 2 widgets or modes move their first use earlier (section 8.3) | One bespoke "numerics lab" widget; fewer widgets with more modes on `float-inspector` and `descent-lab` | `docs/PLAN.md` 3.2 rule 3: scene by default, and none needs free text, a clock, its own state-machine service or a new gesture. Five are the ones `docs/PLAN.md` 12 lists for P3 and P4 (`convergence-plot`, `fd-grid`, `mc-variance`, `feasible-region`, `dual-pair`); `frontier-lab` was canon-gap P6's, and P6 was not adopted, so 2.0b is its first user; four are new in this file (`backward-error`, `interp-lab`, `rng-lab`, `convex-set`), because no existing widget draws a backward-error ball, an interpolant with its nodes, a generator's lattice or a set with the segment test |

| `nan` in arrays, summation choice, $\log\sum e^{x_i}$ without overflow, `np.linalg.solve` against an inverse | 1.1 lessons 66 and 67 | Lessons 11 and 33 prove the summation bounds and when a solve is accurate; log-sum-exp returns as a convexity fact (2.0b lesson 26) |

| Lagrange multipliers, the multiplier as a price, minimum variance of uncorrelated assets | 1.3 lessons 124 to 128 | 2.0b lesson 15 extends shadow prices to LP ranges, lessons 30 to 34 add inequality constraints and duality |

| LU and its cost, partial pivoting on a $2 \times 2$ example, normal equations losing digits, Gram-Schmidt, QR and Householder, the power method, positive definiteness and Cholesky, the SVD from $A^\top A$ by hand, norms and $\kappa$, gradient descent and conditioning, the QR algorithm in outline | 1.4 lessons 12, 16, 48, 50 to 52, 67, 77 to 84, 88, 96 and 98 | Course NL starts from them: backward error, growth factors, refinement, structure and fill-in, shifted eigenvalue algorithms, sensitivity, iterative solvers. Nothing in 1.4 is repeated |

| Binomial trees, Black-Scholes, the Greeks, implied volatility by bisection or Newton | 1.7 levels OP1 to OP3 (lesson 79) | Lessons 18, 51, 60 to 65 and 86 to 90 build the numerical methods behind those prices |

### 0.3 Conventions for every lesson

**Ids** follow `CONTENT-FORMAT.md` 4.1. Topics `top.numerical-methods` (2.0a) and `top.optimisation` (2.0b). Library namespaces `numerics` (2.0a) and `optim` (2.0b): `skl.numerics.*`, `mc.numerics.*`, `gen.numerics.*`, `drl.numerics.*`, and the same with `optim`. Courses `crs.approx`, `crs.numlinalg`, `crs.pde`, `crs.montecarlo` (2.0a); `crs.linprog`, `crs.convex`, `crs.descent`, `crs.opt-apps` (2.0b). Levels `lvl.<course>.<slug>`, lessons `lsn.<course>.<slug>`, homework `set.<course>.<level-slug>`, papers `exm.numerics` and `exm.optim`, exit parts `2.0a.exit` and `2.0b.exit` (working labels fixed at first publish; ids are immutable and display numbers come from position). None of these ids exists in any other file [checked by search on 7 October 2026]. The `#` column is the lesson's number within its item.

**Every lesson** runs the design research 8.3 sequence: a hook on a canvas, free manipulation, the name for what the learner just did, a ladder of 2 to 4 screens adding one constraint each, the trap, the rule stated as a description of the learner's own steps with at least one derived line the learner completes, a transfer screen where a true quant or ML context exists, the summary card, then a quiz of 4 items (5 in the sample lesson). From the owner's skills, every lesson also carries the "why this exists" frame, one common trap, at least one edge case, a worked example with a decision point where the naive route fails, and a motivated leap wherever a creative step appears (`derive` screens carry `leap: naive, fails, pointsTo`). Numerics adds one rule [our choice]: **every lesson that reports an error says what it is measured against** (exact value, reference solver, or a finer grid) and in what unit (absolute, relative, ulps, digits). Optimisation adds one: **every lesson that states an optimum also states which constraints bind**. Skills are at level grain, with at most 2 introduced per lesson (`ASSESSMENT.md` 4.1); finer distinctions are step tags.

**Widgets** use `docs/WIDGETS.md` ids: the primitives and inputs (`plot-2d`, `plot-3d`, `marks`, `drag-point`, `slider`, `number-line`, `value-table`, `matrix`, `stepper`, `sim-runner`, `choice`, `numeric-input`, `expression-input`, `card-sort`, `derivation-steps`), the code composites (`code-cell` for drills, `predict-output`), and the composites named per lesson (among them `float-inspector`, `root-finder`, `riemann-sum`, `condition-number`, `elimination-lab`, `svd-stepper`, `eigen-finder`, `linear-dynamics`, `quadratic-form`, `descent-lab`, `convexity-chord`, `lagrange-explorer`, `heat-diffusion`, `slope-field`, `binomial-tree`, `bs-explorer`, `greeks-lab`, `iv-solver`, `pricer-compare`, `path-simulator`, `sde-stepper`, `dist-explorer`, `regression-lab`, `cond-expectation`, `portfolio-lab`, `execution-lab`, `penalty-geometry`, `regularisation-path`, `classifier-plane`, `loss-lab`, `likelihood-lab`, `yield-curve-lab`, `pca-lab`, `covariance-lab`). New widgets carry "(new)" on first mention; [mode to add] marks a new mode of an existing widget.

**Quiz item types** (`CONTENT-FORMAT.md` 3.4), the letters of the sibling files: N numeric, X expression, C single choice, M multi-select, O order the steps, S sort or match, I interval, P point or curve on a graph, R region selection, A matrix or vector entry, and **W**, the short production item the review added to every lesson whose objective begins Prove, Derive or Show (2 to 4 lines or two sentences, 3-point rubric; `ASSESSMENT.md` 2.3). There is no K (code) item in a quiz (decision 8). Long-form items appear in homework and papers only. Single and multiple choice stay at or below 40% of each course's quiz items (section 0.4).

**Level review.** Every level ends with a Level review of 8 items at difficulty 2 to 4, interleaved, with up to 30% from earlier levels of the same course (`ASSESSMENT.md` 2.4). The homework set follows (section 3).

**Columns.** Objective is one measurable sentence. Hook is the problem the lesson opens with, its answer in brackets. Trap names the misconception the trap screen is built around, with its id; Edge is the fringe case the lesson must show.

## 1. Item overview

### 1.1 2.0a: purpose in a quant and ML-lab career

- **Prices are numerical answers.** Implied volatility has no closed form: it is a root, and plain Newton diverges in the wings unless bracketed (lessons 17 and 18). American options, barriers and exotics are priced by PDE grids, trees or Monte Carlo (courses PD and MO), and each method has an error that falls at a known rate with work. A desk that cannot say which error dominates cannot say whether a price is right.
- **Variance reduction is money.** Monte Carlo error falls as $N^{-1/2}$, so a control variate that removes 99.9% of the variance (lesson 78) is the difference between a price overnight and a price during the trading day; importance sampling (lesson 79) is how tail risk and deep out-of-the-money options are estimated at all.
- **Statistics on a computer is numerical linear algebra.** The textbook one-pass variance loses about eight of sixteen digits on prices near 10,000 (lesson 12); a covariance matrix of 500 shares from 250 days is singular (lesson 35); a desk's hand-set correlations are often not a valid correlation matrix (lessons 36 and 37); a regression on price levels can lose nine digits to conditioning (lesson 34).
- **Developers ship these bugs.** Equality tests on prices, float32 timestamps, summation order, an instability that appears only after 480 time steps (lesson 56), a solver that returns garbage without raising an error (lessons 32 and 35): pricing and risk libraries break here.
- **ML labs live on the same ideas.** Mixed-precision training and loss scaling (lesson 4), the stable log-sum-exp in every softmax (lesson 10; 2.0b lesson 26), iterative refinement on GPUs (lesson 33), conjugate gradients and matrix-free methods for second-order optimisation (lessons 46 and 48), and quasi-Monte Carlo for high-dimensional integrals (course MO).

### 1.2 2.0a learning outcomes

At the end of 2.0a the learner can:

| # | Outcome | Measured by |
|---|---|---|
| NM-O1 | Describe binary64, float32, float16 and bfloat16 as grids, compute spacings, unit roundoff and ranges, and predict absorption, overflow and underflow | 85% on difficulty-3 items; exam Q1, Q2 |
| NM-O2 | Separate the conditioning of a problem from the stability of an algorithm, compute condition numbers, and use forward error $\lesssim \kappa \times$ backward error | Long-form at 7 of 10 (homework EA2); exam Q4 |
| NM-O3 | Diagnose catastrophic cancellation, count the digits lost, and remove it by rewriting (identities, `log1p`, `expm1`, Welford, compensated or pairwise summation) | Exam Q3 at 7 of 10 (the exit slot) |
| NM-O4 | Measure orders of convergence, prove Newton's quadratic rate, and build safeguarded root finders, including an implied-volatility solver that never fails | 85% on difficulty-3 items; `drl.numerics.exit-iv-grid` |
| NM-O5 | Interpolate with polynomials, Chebyshev nodes and splines, explain Runge's phenomenon, and choose what to interpolate on a yield curve | 85% on difficulty-3 items; exam Q6 |
| NM-O6 | Derive quadrature errors, extrapolate by Richardson and Romberg, use Gauss rules, split at a payoff kink, and state when quadrature loses to Monte Carlo | Long-form at 7 of 10 (homework EA5); exam Q7 |

| NM-O9 | Derive the heat and Black-Scholes equations from random walks and trees, transform one into the other, and set boundary and terminal conditions | Long-form at 7 of 10 (homework PD1); exam Q11 |
| NM-O10 | Analyse explicit, implicit and Crank-Nicolson schemes by von Neumann stability, measure their orders, and price European, American and barrier options on a grid | Exam Q12, Q13; `drl.numerics.exit-four-pricers` |
| NM-O11 | Generate pseudo-random and quasi-random numbers correctly, with independent streams, and transform them into the distributions and correlated paths a model needs | 85% on difficulty-3 items |
| NM-O12 | Size Monte Carlo runs for a target error, separate discretisation bias from statistical error, and reduce variance by antithetic, control-variate, importance, stratified, conditional and quasi-Monte Carlo methods | Long-form at 7 of 10 (homework MO3); exam Q14, Q15 |
| NM-O13 | Estimate Greeks by bumping with common random numbers, pathwise and likelihood-ratio methods, and price early exercise by Longstaff and Schwartz | 85% on difficulty-3 items; exam Q16 |

- **Stage 1 exit** (rule 3: no exit test, no next stage).

On the developer track, 1.9 level CL4 precedes 2.0a.
- **Not needed:** canon-gap P8 (decision 6).

**Feeds.** 2.0b (conditioning, Newton, conjugate gradients); 2.1 and 2.1b (stable losses, mixed precision, SGD noise); 2.2 (likelihood maximisation, simulated volatility models, Euler-Maruyama); 2.3 (implied volatility on chains, the pricers of project 3, Greeks, risk simulation); 3.2 (Feynman-Kac and the numerical pricing of RN3); 3.3 (Newton, Monte Carlo error and floating-point questions at interview).

Solving time (everything except the 15.5 hours of reading and the non-task screens of lessons, about 4 hours) is about 100 hours, 84%, above the curriculum's 60% floor (rule 2). Homework's share is the highest of any item (1.5's is 36%) because 2.0a has 17 levels in 120 hours and every set carries a code drill; reading is correspondingly lean, and section 5 points to sections, not chapters, wherever it can.

**Pace.** About 11 weeks at the curriculum's 15-hour week (120 h would fill 8 weeks only if every block were used fully). The Main block (9 h) carries lessons, homework, reading and the papers (99.75 h, 11.1 weeks) and binds; the Build block (3 h) carries the exit drills and the Lab (10.5 h, 3.5 weeks), the Review block (2 h) practice and the Redo queue (9.75 h, 4.9 weeks). 2.4 contests share the Build block once 100 problems are solved (`ASSESSMENT.md` 8.3), which leaves room for them, so a realistic forecast is 11 to 12 weeks.

### 1.5 2.0b: purpose in a quant and ML-lab career

- **Portfolios are optimisation problems.** Portfolio construction is $\max_{\mathbf w}\boldsymbol\mu^\top\mathbf w - \frac\gamma2\mathbf w^\top\Sigma\mathbf w$ (lesson 48's form, $\gamma$ the risk aversion) under budget, position, sector and turnover constraints; without constraints a mean-variance optimiser maximises estimation error (Michaud 1989; lesson 50). The 2.3 alpha project turns signals into positions with exactly these tools.
- **Prices are duals.** Shadow prices value a relaxed limit (lesson 15), and the one-period fundamental theorem of asset pricing is LP duality through Farkas' lemma (lesson 17). Execution schedules (Almgren and Chriss) are quadratic programmes (lesson 53).
- **Training is optimisation.** The lasso and the support vector machine are convex programmes, and the SVM's dual is where kernels come from (lesson 56). SGD, momentum and Nesterov acceleration (lessons 43 and 44) are every lab's working tools, and their rates are set by the condition number $\kappa$ (lesson 37).
- **Knowing when a problem is convex is the job.** A convex problem has one answer that a solver finds and certifies with a duality gap; a non-convex one (cardinality limits, minimum lots, neural networks) needs a relaxation, a heuristic or a lot of restarts (lessons 18, 28, 46).
