## 2. Courses

2.0a: four courses, 17 levels, 91 lessons. 2.0b: four courses, 10 levels, 58 lessons. Each level is 4 to 7 lessons, then a Level review (8 items) and a homework set (section 3).

### Course EA: Error, Equations and Integrals (`crs.approx`, 5 levels, 30 lessons)

0.1 and 1.1 showed that $0.1 + 0.2 \ne 0.3$ and told the learner to compare floats with a tolerance; 1.3 ran Newton's method and Simpson's rule and measured their errors. This course supplies the theory those lessons lacked: the floating-point number system and its rounding model, the split between a problem's conditioning and an algorithm's stability, and the error analysis of root finding, interpolation and quadrature, each ending in a tool a desk uses (a safeguarded implied-volatility solver, a yield-curve interpolator, a quadrature pricer). Spine: Süli and Mayers chapters 1, 6, 7, 10 and 11 (chapter 10 "Numerical integration II" and chapter 11 "Piecewise polynomial approximation" confirmed by search, 7 October 2026); for floating point and stability, Trefethen and Bau lectures 12 to 15 and Goldberg (1991).

**Level EA1: Floating point as a number system** (`lvl.approx.floating`, 6 lessons)

| # | Lesson | Objective | Hook | Widgets | Trap and edge case | Quiz |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | `lsn.approx.float-grid` The floating-point grid | Describe a binary64 number as $\pm(1.f)_2 \times 2^e$, count the doubles in a binade, and compute the spacing $\operatorname{ulp}(x) = 2^{e - 52}$ for any $x$ in the normal range | How many doubles lie between 1 and 2? Between 1,024 and 2,048? (both $2^{52} \approx 4.5 \times 10^{15}$; the spacing grows from $2.2 \times 10^{-16}$ to $2.3 \times 10^{-13}$) | `float-inspector` (bits, neighbours), `number-line` (log scale), `numeric-input` | Trap: floats are evenly spaced, like a ruler (`mc.numerics.uniform-spacing`). Edge: $2^{53} + 1 = 9{,}007{,}199{,}254{,}740{,}993$ is the first whole number a double cannot hold; it rounds to $2^{53}$ | N, N, C, S |
| 2 | `lsn.approx.rounding-model` The rounding model | Use $\operatorname{fl}(x \circ y) = (x \circ y)(1 + \delta)$ with $\lvert\delta\rvert \le u = 2^{-53}$ to bound the error of one operation and of a chain, and show by example that floating-point addition is not associative | In double precision $(10^{16} + (-10^{16})) + 1 = 1$ but $10^{16} + (-10^{16} + 1) = 0$. Which operation lost the 1? (the inner sum $-10^{16} + 1$, which rounds back to $-10^{16}$ because the spacing there is 2) | `float-inspector` (rounding-model [mode to add]), `derivation-steps`, `choice` | Trap: every operation is correctly rounded, so the result is correctly rounded (`mc.numerics.local-implies-global`). Edge: the worst-case bound for a sum of $n$ terms grows like $(n - 1)u\sum\lvert x_i\rvert$; typical errors grow like $\sqrt n\,u$, because roundings partly cancel | N, C, O, M |
| 3 | `lsn.approx.range-special` Range, overflow, underflow and special values | Predict overflow above $1.8 \times 10^{308}$, gradual underflow below $2.2 \times 10^{-308}$ down to $4.9 \times 10^{-324}$, and the arithmetic of `inf` and `nan`, and rescale a computation so no intermediate overflows | $\sqrt{x^2 + y^2}$ with $x = y = 10^{200}$: the formula gives `inf`, `math.hypot` gives $1.414 \times 10^{200}$. What does `hypot` do differently? (it scales by the larger value before squaring) | `float-inspector` (range), `predict-output`, `choice` | Trap: an overflow in an intermediate means the answer is too large to represent (`mc.numerics.intermediate-overflow`). Edge: subnormals guarantee $x - y = 0$ exactly when $x = y$; fast-math modes that flush them to zero break it | N, C, M, S |
| 4 | `lsn.approx.reduced-precision` Single, half and bfloat16 | Compute $u$ and the range of float32 ($2^{-24}$), float16 ($2^{-11}$, largest 65,504) and bfloat16 ($2^{-8}$, float32's range), and choose a format for storage, arithmetic and accumulation, including loss scaling | A gradient of $10^{-8}$ is stored in float16. What is stored? (0: it is below half the smallest subnormal, $2^{-25} \approx 3.0 \times 10^{-8}$). Multiplied by 1,024 first? ($1.025 \times 10^{-5}$, kept) | `float-inspector` (formats [mode to add]), `slider`, `numeric-input` | Trap: lower precision only loses digits at the end (`mc.numerics.precision-not-range`); it loses range too. Edge: bfloat16 keeps float32's exponent with an 8-bit significand, which is why training in it needs no loss scaling and training in float16 does (Micikevicius and others 2018) | N, C, S, N |
| 5 | `lsn.approx.absorption` Absorption: when adding does nothing | Explain why $x + y = x$ when $\lvert y\rvert < \frac12\operatorname{ulp}(x)$, predict where a counter or accumulator stalls, and fix it with a wider accumulator, integers or reordering | A float32 counter adds 1 for every trade. After 20 million trades it reads? (16,777,216 $= 2^{24}$: from there $x + 1$ rounds back to $x$) | `float-inspector` (accumulate [mode to add]), `predict-output`, `numeric-input` | Trap: rounding only matters in long sums of tiny errors (`mc.numerics.only-tiny-errors`). Edge: seconds since 1970 held in float32 now have a resolution of 128 seconds, so a float32 timestamp column merges trades two minutes apart | N, N, C, M |
| 6 | `lsn.approx.measuring-error` Measuring error: relative error, ulps and digits | Compute absolute and relative error, error in ulps, and correct significant digits as $-\log_{10}$ of the relative error, and read a library's accuracy claim | `log(1 + x)` at $x = 10^{-10}$ returns $1.000000082690371 \times 10^{-10}$; `log1p(x)` returns $9.9999999995 \times 10^{-11}$. How many digits does the first get right? (about 7: relative error $8.3 \times 10^{-8}$) | `float-inspector` (neighbours), `value-table`, `numeric-input` | Trap: absolute error judges every scale fairly (`mc.numerics.absolute-error-scale`). Edge: near zero relative error is undefined, so tests combine $\lvert a - b\rvert \le \text{atol} + \text{rtol}\,\lvert b\rvert$ (1.2 lesson 21) | N, N, C, S |

Transfer screens: lesson 4 is every lab's mixed-precision training recipe; lesson 5 is why exchanges store prices as integer ticks and cash as integer pence; lesson 6 is how a pricing library states it is "accurate to 1 ulp", and how a reviewer tests the claim.

Then the Level review and homework `set.approx.floating`.

**Level EA2: Conditioning and stability** (`lvl.approx.stability`, 7 lessons)

| # | Lesson | Objective | Hook | Widgets | Trap and edge case | Quiz |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 7 | `lsn.approx.condition-number` The condition number of a problem | Compute the relative condition number $\kappa_f(x) = \left\lvert\frac{xf'(x)}{f(x)}\right\rvert$ and predict how much a relative input error is amplified | $f(x) = x - 1$ at $x = 1.000001$: an input error of $10^{-10}$ (relative) becomes what relative error in $f(x)$? (about $10^{-4}$: $\kappa = \frac x{x - 1} \approx 10^6$) | `backward-error` (new: conditioning mode), `function-mapping`, `numeric-input` | Trap: an ill-conditioned problem means a bad algorithm (`mc.numerics.conditioning-is-algorithm`). Edge: $\sqrt x$ has $\kappa = \frac12$ everywhere, so square roots never amplify relative error; $\ln x$ has $\kappa = \frac1{\lvert\ln x\rvert}$, unbounded near $x = 1$ | N, C, X, N |
| 8 | `lsn.approx.backward-error` Forward and backward error | Define the backward error of a computed answer as the smallest input change that makes it exact, and judge an algorithm by forward error $\lesssim \kappa \times$ backward error | Horner's rule evaluates the expanded $(x - 2)^9$ at $x = 2.01$ and returns $-3.75 \times 10^{-12}$; the true value is $10^{-18}$. Is Horner's rule unstable? (no: it is backward stable; the value of a polynomial near a ninefold root is ill-conditioned, and the noise across $[1.92, 2.08]$ reaches $1.1 \times 10^{-11}$) | `backward-error` (polynomial mode), `plot-2d`, `choice` | Trap: a wrong answer convicts the algorithm (`mc.numerics.blame-algorithm`). Edge: evaluating the factored form $(x - 2)^9$ instead is accurate to 16 digits, because that is a different, well-conditioned problem | C, N, S, W |
| 9 | `lsn.approx.cancellation` Catastrophic cancellation (section 7) | Predict the digits lost when subtracting nearly equal numbers as about $\log_{10}\frac{\lvert a\rvert}{\lvert a - b\rvert}$, locate the rounding the subtraction exposes, and remove the subtraction by an identity | A risk system computes $\frac{1 - \cos x}{x^2}$, which tends to $\frac12$. At $x = 10^{-8}$ it prints 0. Why, and what is the fix? ($\cos 10^{-8}$ rounds to exactly 1.0; rewrite as $\frac{2\sin^2(x/2)}{x^2}$) | `float-inspector` (cancellation), `derivation-steps`, `slider`, `choice` | Trap: the subtraction is where the error is made (`mc.numerics.subtraction-inexact`); by Sterbenz's lemma it is exact, and the damage was done when $\cos x$ was rounded. Edge: subtracting exact inputs is harmless: $1.5 - 1.25$ loses nothing | C, N, N, M, W |
| 10 | `lsn.approx.stable-rewrites` Stable rewrites: `log1p`, `expm1` and compounding | Rewrite formulas so no step subtracts nearly equal rounded numbers, using `log1p`, `expm1` and $\exp\left(n\operatorname{log1p}\frac rn\right)$ | Compound 5% a year $n$ times a year: $\left(1 + \frac{0.05}n\right)^n$ gives 1.05127109498 at $n = 10^6$, 1.05122908433 at $n = 10^{12}$ and exactly 1.0 at $n = 10^{15}$. Where did the money go? ($1 + 5 \times 10^{-17}$ rounds to 1; the limit is $e^{0.05} = 1.0512710964$) | `float-inspector` (cancellation), `value-table`, `expression-input` | Trap: more compounding periods are always closer to the continuous limit (`mc.numerics.more-steps-better`). Edge: $\exp\left(n\operatorname{log1p}\frac rn\right)$ is accurate at every $n$; log returns of tiny moves use `log1p` for the same reason | N, X, C, N |
| 11 | `lsn.approx.summation` Summation algorithms | Compare recursive, sorted, pairwise and Kahan compensated summation by their error bounds (about $(n - 1)u$, $\lceil\log_2n\rceil u$ and $2u$ relative to $\sum\lvert x_i\rvert$) and choose one for a long sum | Sum $\frac1{k^2}$ for $k = 1$ to $10^6$ in float32, largest terms first and then smallest first. Which is closer to the true 1.644933067? (smallest first: error $8 \times 10^{-8}$ against $2.1 \times 10^{-4}$) | `float-inspector` (accumulate), `convergence-plot` (new), `choice` | Trap: the order of a sum cannot matter (`mc.numerics.order-irrelevant`). Edge: NumPy's `sum` is pairwise, already far better than a Python loop; fast-math compilers delete Kahan's compensation (1.9 lesson 64) | C, N, O, M |
| 12 | `lsn.approx.one-pass-variance` One-pass variance: the textbook formula against Welford | Show why $\frac1n\sum x_i^2 - \bar x^2$ cancels catastrophically when the mean is large against the spread, and compute the variance stably by Welford's update or two passes | Prices 10,000, 10,001 and 10,002. The one-pass formula gives 0 in float32 and 0.6666666716 in float64. The true population variance? ($\frac23$; Welford gives 0.6666667 in float32) | `float-inspector` (cancellation), `value-table`, `derivation-steps` | Trap: algebraically equal formulas are numerically equal (`mc.numerics.algebra-equals-numerics`). Edge: subtracting any value near the mean first rescues the one-pass formula; a million prices near 10,000 give a float32 "variance" of 8 against a true 0.999 | N, W, C, O |
| 13 | `lsn.approx.unstable-recurrences` Stable and unstable recurrences | Tell a stable from an unstable recurrence by how it multiplies an initial error, and reverse an unstable recurrence to make it stable | $I_n = \int_0^1x^ne^{x - 1}\,dx$ satisfies $I_n = 1 - nI_{n-1}$, with $I_0 = 1 - e^{-1}$. Run forward, it gives $I_{20} = -30.19$, though $0 < I_n < \frac1{n + 1}$. Why? (the rounding error in $I_0$ is multiplied by $20! \approx 2.4 \times 10^{18}$) | `backward-error` (recurrence mode), `value-table`, `numeric-input` | Trap: a correct formula gives a correct algorithm (`mc.numerics.formula-is-algorithm`). Edge: running backwards from $I_{31} = 0$ divides the error by $n$ at each step and gives $I_{20} = 0.0455448840758181$ to full precision | N, C, N, S |

Transfer screens: lesson 7 is why a hedge ratio between two nearly identical instruments is unreliable whatever the code; lesson 12 is the volatility estimate inside every streaming risk system; lesson 13 is the pattern behind unstable recursive filters and estimators.

Then the Level review and homework `set.approx.stability`.

**Level EA3: Solving equations** (`lvl.approx.roots`, 6 lessons)

| # | Lesson | Objective | Hook | Widgets | Trap and edge case | Quiz |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 14 | `lsn.approx.brackets` Brackets, bisection and stopping in floating point | Bound bisection's steps by $\left\lceil\log_2\frac{b - a}\varepsilon\right\rceil$, choose a stopping rule that terminates in floating point, and estimate a root's attainable accuracy as about $\frac{\varepsilon_f}{\lvert f'(x^\star)\rvert}$ | Bisection for $x^2 - 2$ on $[0, 2]$ with tolerance $10^{-20}$ never stops. Why? (after 53 halvings the bracket is one ulp wide, $2.2 \times 10^{-16}$, and the midpoint equals an endpoint) | `root-finder` (bisection, bracket), `convergence-plot`, `numeric-input` | Trap: a tolerance can be as small as you like (`mc.numerics.tolerance-below-ulp`). Edge: the residual $\lvert f(x)\rvert$ cannot fall below the rounding noise in evaluating $f$, so near a flat root a whole interval of $x$ looks like a root | N, N, C, M |
| 15 | `lsn.approx.order-of-convergence` Fixed-point iteration and the order of convergence | Define order $p$ and rate, and estimate $p$ from three successive errors as $\frac{\ln(e_{n+1}/e_n)}{\ln(e_n/e_{n-1})}$ | Errors $10^{-2}, 10^{-4}, 10^{-8}, 10^{-16}$: order? (2). Errors $10^{-2}, 2 \times 10^{-3}, 4 \times 10^{-4}$: order and rate? (1, rate 0.2) | `convergence-plot` (semilog, log-log), `root-finder` (fixed-point [mode to add]), `numeric-input` | Trap: high order means fast from any start (`mc.numerics.order-is-speed`). Edge: on a semilog plot linear convergence is a straight line and quadratic convergence falls off a cliff; 1.3 lesson 86 proved the contraction condition | N, N, P, C |
| 16 | `lsn.approx.newton-secant` Newton, the secant method and cost per evaluation | Derive $e_{n+1} \approx \frac{f''(x^\star)}{2f'(x^\star)}e_n^2$, state the secant method's order $\varphi \approx 1.618$, and compare methods by order per function evaluation | Newton needs $f$ and $f'$ each step (order 2); the secant method needs only $f$ (order 1.618). If $f'$ costs as much as $f$, which gains more digits per evaluation? (secant: 1.618 against $\sqrt2 \approx 1.414$) | `root-finder` (Newton, secant [mode to add]), `convergence-plot`, `derivation-steps` | Trap: Newton is always fastest (`mc.numerics.newton-always-best`). Edge: at a double root Newton is only linear, with rate $\frac12$; the step $x - 2\frac f{f'}$ restores quadratic convergence (homework EA3) | X, N, C, W |
| 17 | `lsn.approx.safeguarded` Safeguarded Newton and Brent's method | Combine a bracket with Newton or secant steps, accepting a step only if it stays inside the bracket and shrinks it enough, and otherwise bisecting | Newton on $\arctan x = 0$ from $x_0 = 1.5$: $-1.69, 2.32, -5.11, 32.3, -1575$. From 1.3 it converges. Where is the boundary? ($x_0 \approx 1.3917$, where one step lands exactly on $-x_0$ and the iterates cycle) | `root-finder` (Newton, brent [mode to add]), `plot-2d`, `numeric-input` | Trap: a convergence proof near the root says nothing about where you start (`mc.numerics.local-is-global`). Edge: Brent's method (`scipy.optimize.brentq`) is never much slower than bisection and is usually superlinear | N, C, O, M |
| 18 | `lsn.approx.implied-vol` Implied volatility that never fails | Build an implied-volatility solver that rejects prices outside the no-arbitrage bounds, brackets $\sigma$, starts where Newton is safe and safeguards each step | A 3-month call struck at 150 on a share at 100, priced at 30% volatility (0.0192). Newton from 10% jumps to $1.5 \times 10^{11}$. Why, and which start is safe? (vega at 10% is $1.3 \times 10^{-13}$; from the price curve's inflection point, $\sigma_0 = \sqrt{2\lvert\ln(F/K)\rvert/T} = 1.80$, Newton converges monotonically: 0.68, 0.48, 0.39, ..., 0.3000) | `iv-solver` (algorithm, bounds), `root-finder` (brent), `choice` | Trap: a tolerance in price is a tolerance in volatility (`mc.numerics.price-vs-vol-tolerance`); in the wings a price error of $10^{-8}$ can be a large volatility error. Edge: the at-the-money start $\sqrt{2\pi/T}\,\frac CS$ is 20.05% for a true 20.09% (1.7 lesson 79) and fails in the wings; Manaster and Koehler (1982) gave the inflection start | N, C, M, O |
| 19 | `lsn.approx.newton-systems` Newton's method for systems | Solve $F(\mathbf x) = \mathbf 0$ by $J(\mathbf x_k)\boldsymbol\Delta = -F(\mathbf x_k)$, see quadratic convergence in $\mathbb R^n$, and approximate a Jacobian by finite differences | Find $(x, y)$ with $x^2 + y^2 = 4$ and $xy = 1$, starting from $(2, 0)$. Errors after each step? (0.070, 0.0018, $1.5 \times 10^{-6}$, $1.1 \times 10^{-12}$: the digits double; the root is $\left(\frac{\sqrt6 + \sqrt2}2, \frac{\sqrt6 - \sqrt2}2\right)$) | `root-finder` (newton-2d [mode to add]), `matrix`, `convergence-plot` | Trap: form $J^{-1}$ to take a step (`mc.numerics.invert-to-solve`). Edge: a singular Jacobian (starting at $(0, 0)$) stops Newton; calibrating two model parameters to two quotes is this problem | A, N, C, O |

Transfer screens: lesson 18 is the 2.0a exit's second task and the solver 2.3 level OA2 applies to real chains; lesson 19 is how a two-parameter model is calibrated to two quotes.

Then the Level review and homework `set.approx.roots`.

**Level EA4: Interpolation** (`lvl.approx.interpolation`, 5 lessons)

| # | Lesson | Objective | Hook | Widgets | Trap and edge case | Quiz |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 20 | `lsn.approx.interpolating-polynomial` One polynomial through $n + 1$ points | Prove that one polynomial of degree at most $n$ interpolates $n + 1$ points, write it in Lagrange and barycentric form, and explain why solving the Vandermonde system is ill-conditioned | Through $(0, 1)$, $(1, 3)$ and $(2, 7)$: the quadratic? ($x^2 + x + 1$). The Vandermonde matrix for 21 equally spaced points on $[0, 1]$ has condition number? ($9.5 \times 10^{16}$: every digit gone) | `interp-lab` (new: polynomial mode), `matrix`, `expression-input` | Trap: monomial coefficients are a good way to store an interpolant (`mc.numerics.monomial-basis`). Edge: the barycentric formula costs $O(n)$ a point and is stable on Chebyshev nodes | X, N, C, W |
| 21 | `lsn.approx.runge` The interpolation error and Runge's phenomenon | Use $f(x) - p(x) = \frac{f^{(n+1)}(\xi)}{(n + 1)!}\prod_i(x - x_i)$ to explain why equally spaced interpolation of $\frac1{1 + 25x^2}$ diverges near the ends | Interpolate $\frac1{1 + 25x^2}$ at 11 equally spaced points on $[-1, 1]$. Largest error? (1.92, at $x = \pm0.94$). At 21 points? (59.8) | `interp-lab` (runge preset), `plot-2d`, `numeric-input` | Trap: more points always give a better fit (`mc.numerics.more-points-better`). Edge: the node polynomial $\prod(x - x_i)$ is what grows near the ends; the function itself is smooth | N, N, C, P |
| 22 | `lsn.approx.chebyshev` Chebyshev nodes | Place nodes at $x_j = \cos\frac{(2j + 1)\pi}{2(n + 1)}$, show they make $\max\lvert\prod(x - x_j)\rvert = 2^{-n}$, the least possible, and see geometric convergence for smooth functions | The same function at 11 Chebyshev nodes: largest error? (0.109). At 21? (0.0153) | `interp-lab` (chebyshev), `slider`, `numeric-input` | Trap: Chebyshev nodes fix every interpolation problem (`mc.numerics.chebyshev-cures-all`); they need smoothness and the freedom to choose where to sample. Edge: market quotes come at strikes and maturities you do not choose, so desks interpolate locally (lessons 23 and 24) | N, N, C, M |
| 23 | `lsn.approx.splines` Piecewise interpolation and cubic splines | Bound piecewise-linear error by $\frac{h^2}8\max\lvert f''\rvert$, build a natural cubic spline from a tridiagonal system, and compare its overshoot with a single high-degree polynomial | Six points: three at height 0, then three at height 1. The degree-5 interpolant swings between $-0.20$ and $1.20$. The natural cubic spline? (between $-0.11$ and $1.11$) | `interp-lab` (spline), `drag-point`, `choice` | Trap: a smooth interpolant is a faithful one (`mc.numerics.smooth-is-faithful`). Edge: a spline is local in its effect but not in its computation: moving one point changes every piece, with influence falling by a factor of about $2 + \sqrt3 \approx 3.7$ per interval | N, C, M, P |
| 24 | `lsn.approx.yield-curves` Interpolating a yield curve | Choose what to interpolate (zero rates, discount factors or log discount factors) and predict the forward rates each choice implies | Continuously compounded zero rates of 1% at 1 year, 2% at 2 years and 3% at 5 years. Linear interpolation of $\ln P(t)$ gives which forward rates? (3% on $[1, 2]$, $3\tfrac23$% on $[2, 5]$). Linear interpolation of zero rates gives forwards that jump from 4% down to $2\tfrac23$% at 2 years | `interp-lab` (curve preset), `yield-curve-lab`, `numeric-input` | Trap: linear zero rates give smooth forwards (`mc.numerics.linear-zero-smooth-forward`); they give a sawtooth. Edge: monotone convex interpolation (Hagan and West 2006) keeps forwards positive and continuous; a natural spline on zero rates can make them negative | N, N, C, S |

Transfer screens: lesson 24 is how every rates desk builds a discount curve; lesson 23's spline is the smoothing step of a volatility surface (2.3 level OA4).

Then the Level review and homework `set.approx.interpolation`.

**Level EA5: Numerical integration** (`lvl.approx.quadrature`, 6 lessons)

| # | Lesson | Objective | Hook | Widgets | Trap and edge case | Quiz |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 25 | `lsn.approx.quadrature-error` Where quadrature errors come from | Derive the composite trapezoid error $-\frac{(b - a)h^2}{12}f''(\xi)$ and Simpson's $O(h^4)$ error from Taylor expansions, and predict the error ratio when $h$ halves | 1.3 lesson 75 measured that halving $h$ cuts Simpson's error by 16. On $\int_0^1\sqrt x\,dx$ the ratio is 2.83. Why? ($\sqrt x$ has an unbounded derivative at 0, so the error is $O(h^{1.5})$, and $2^{1.5} = 2.83$) | `riemann-sum` (error inset), `convergence-plot`, `derivation-steps` | Trap: a rule's order holds for every integrand (`mc.numerics.order-unconditional`). Edge: for a smooth periodic integrand over a period the trapezoid rule converges exponentially (Trefethen and Weideman 2014): 8 points give $\int_0^{2\pi}e^{\cos x}\,dx$ to $1.3 \times 10^{-6}$, 16 to every digit | N, N, C, W |
| 26 | `lsn.approx.richardson` Richardson extrapolation and Romberg integration | Combine two estimates with a known error order to cancel the leading term, $\frac{4T(h/2) - T(h)}3$, and build a Romberg table | Trapezoid estimates of $\int_0^1e^x\,dx$ with 2 and 4 strips are 1.7539311 and 1.7272219, errors $3.6 \times 10^{-2}$ and $8.9 \times 10^{-3}$. Combine them: error? ($3.7 \times 10^{-5}$; one more Romberg level gives $1.4 \times 10^{-8}$) | `riemann-sum` (romberg [mode to add]), `value-table`, `numeric-input` | Trap: extrapolation always helps (`mc.numerics.extrapolate-always`); it needs the error expansion it assumes. Edge: extrapolating noisy estimates (Monte Carlo) amplifies the noise; on a binomial tree the odd-even wobble must be removed first (2.3 lesson 160) | N, N, C, O |
| 27 | `lsn.approx.gauss` Gaussian quadrature | Choose $n$ nodes and weights that integrate every polynomial of degree up to $2n - 1$ exactly, derive the two-point rule's nodes $\pm\frac1{\sqrt3}$, and compare with Newton-Cotes rules at equal cost | With two evaluations on $[-1, 1]$, integrate $1$, $x$, $x^2$ and $x^3$ exactly. Where must the two points be? ($\pm\frac1{\sqrt3}$, weight 1 each) | `riemann-sum` (gauss [mode to add]: weights drawn as bar widths), `derivation-steps`, `numeric-input` | Trap: equally spaced nodes are best (`mc.numerics.equal-spacing-best`). Edge: Gauss rules need smoothness; a kink inside the interval, such as an option payoff at the strike, costs them their order | X, N, C, W |
| 28 | `lsn.approx.option-quadrature` Pricing by quadrature: the kink at the strike | Price a European option by integrating its payoff against the lognormal density, and restore fast convergence by integrating only where the payoff is smooth | An at-the-money call ($S = K = 100$, $\sigma = 20\%$, $T = 1$, $r = 0$; Black-Scholes 7.9656) by Gauss-Hermite nodes over the whole line: errors 0.29, 0.12 and 0.042 with 10, 20 and 40 nodes. With 20 Gauss-Legendre nodes above the strike only? ($2.3 \times 10^{-9}$) | `riemann-sum` (gauss), `dist-explorer` (lognormal), `numeric-input` | Trap: a high-order rule is accurate for any integrand (`mc.numerics.high-order-any`). Edge: in one dimension quadrature beats Monte Carlo by orders of magnitude; in high dimension the comparison reverses (lesson 30) | N, N, C, M |
| 29 | `lsn.approx.adaptive` Adaptive quadrature, and when it lies | Explain how adaptive rules estimate their error by comparing two rules, handle singular integrands and infinite ranges by substitution and splitting, and construct an integrand that fools them | `scipy.integrate.quad` integrates a Gaussian spike of width $10^{-4}$ at 0.3 over $[0, 1000]$ and returns 0 with error estimate 0. The true value? ($1.77 \times 10^{-4}$: no sample point landed on the spike) | `riemann-sum` (adaptive [mode to add]), `plot-2d`, `choice` | Trap: the reported error estimate is a guarantee (`mc.numerics.error-estimate-guarantee`). Edge: giving `quad` the breakpoints 0.299, 0.3 and 0.301 recovers $1.7725 \times 10^{-4}$; the breakpoint 0.3 alone does not | N, C, M, S |
| 30 | `lsn.approx.curse-of-dimension` The curse of dimension | Show that a product rule with $m$ points per axis costs $m^d$ evaluations, so the trapezoid rule's error against work $W$ is $O(W^{-2/d})$, against Monte Carlo's $O(W^{-1/2})$ in every dimension | A basket option on 10 shares priced on a grid of 10 points per share needs how many evaluations? ($10^{10}$). From which dimension does Monte Carlo match the trapezoid rule's rate? ($d = 4$, where $\frac2d = \frac12$) | `convergence-plot` (work mode), `slider` (dimension), `numeric-input` | Trap: a method that wins in one dimension wins in all (`mc.numerics.dimension-free-ranking`). Edge: Simpson's break-even is $d = 8$; quasi-Monte Carlo (level MO4) moves the boundary but does not remove it | N, W, C, S |

Transfer screens: lesson 28 is how short-dated options and characteristic-function pricers are integrated on a desk; lesson 30 opens course MO.

Then the Level review and homework `set.approx.quadrature`.

### Course NL: Numerical Linear Algebra (`crs.numlinalg`, 4 levels, 18 lessons)

1.4 taught elimination, LU, QR, Cholesky, the SVD and the condition number as linear algebra, with one lesson each on pivoting, the normal equations' lost digits and the QR algorithm. This course treats them as algorithms in floating point: what a computed solution satisfies (backward error, the growth factor), how to improve it (refinement), how structure changes the cost (symmetry, bands, sparsity), how eigenvalues and singular values are computed and how sensitive they are, and how large systems are solved without factorising them. Spine: Trefethen and Bau lectures 17 to 23 and 25 to 40 [check 37]; problems from MIT 18.335J, whose solutions are public.

**Level NL1: Solving $A\mathbf x = \mathbf b$ well** (`lvl.numlinalg.solving`, 5 lessons)

| # | Lesson | Objective | Hook | Widgets | Trap and edge case | Quiz |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 31 | `lsn.numlinalg.residual-vs-error` A small residual is not a small error | Prove $\frac{\lVert\mathbf x - \hat{\mathbf x}\rVert}{\lVert\mathbf x\rVert} \le \kappa(A)\frac{\lVert\mathbf r\rVert}{\lVert\mathbf b\rVert}$ and read the residual as a measure of backward error | Solve $H\mathbf x = \mathbf b$ for the $10 \times 10$ Hilbert matrix with true $\mathbf x$ all ones: the relative residual is $10^{-16}$. The relative error? ($2.3 \times 10^{-5}$; $\kappa = 1.6 \times 10^{13}$) | `condition-number` (residual [mode to add]), `matrix`, `numeric-input` | Trap: check a solution by its residual (`mc.numerics.residual-certifies`). Edge: for the $12 \times 12$ Hilbert matrix the error is 13% while the residual stays at $10^{-16}$ | N, N, C, W |
| 32 | `lsn.numlinalg.growth-factor` Gaussian elimination is backward stable, usually | State that partial pivoting gives $(A + \delta A)\hat{\mathbf x} = \mathbf b$ with $\lVert\delta A\rVert \lesssim n\rho u\lVert A\rVert$, where $\rho$ is the growth factor, and exhibit a matrix with $\rho = 2^{n-1}$ | A $60 \times 60$ matrix with 1 on the diagonal, $-1$ below it and 1 in the last column has $\kappa \approx 27$. LAPACK's partially pivoted solver returns an answer with relative error? (2.5: no correct digit, because the growth factor is $2^{59} \approx 5.8 \times 10^{17}$) | `elimination-lab` (floating, growth [mode to add]), `matrix`, `numeric-input` | Trap: partial pivoting guarantees stability (`mc.numerics.pivoting-guarantees`). Edge: growth like this is almost never met in practice, which is why partial pivoting is the default (Trefethen and Bau, lecture 22); QR or complete pivoting avoids it | N, C, N, M |
| 33 | `lsn.numlinalg.iterative-refinement` Iterative refinement and mixed precision | Improve a solution by solving $A\boldsymbol\delta = \mathbf r$ with the existing factors and a residual computed in higher precision, and predict when a float32 factorisation delivers float64 accuracy ($\kappa u_{32} < 1$) | Factor a matrix with $\kappa = 10^3$ in float32 (error $3.1 \times 10^{-5}$), then refine with float64 residuals. Errors after one and two steps? ($4.3 \times 10^{-10}$, $3.4 \times 10^{-14}$: double precision) | `convergence-plot`, `matrix`, `numeric-input` | Trap: a low-precision factorisation caps the accuracy (`mc.numerics.factor-precision-caps`). Edge: refinement fails once $\kappa \ge \frac1{u_{32}} \approx 1.7 \times 10^7$; GPU solvers use this scheme | N, N, C, O |
| 34 | `lsn.numlinalg.digits-you-keep` How many digits you keep | Use "about $\log_{10}\kappa$ digits are lost" to judge a computed solution, estimate $\kappa$ cheaply, and see how rescaling columns changes $\kappa$ | A regression of returns on the price levels of five bank shares has $\kappa(X^\top X) = 10^9$. How many digits of $\hat{\boldsymbol\beta}$ can you trust in double precision? (about 7) | `condition-number`, `value-table`, `numeric-input` | Trap: $\kappa = 10^9$ means the answer is wrong (`mc.numerics.large-kappa-wrong`). Edge: rescaling a column from pounds to pence changes $\kappa$ by up to $10^4$ without changing the question; what matters is the conditioning of the question asked | N, N, C, M |
| 35 | `lsn.numlinalg.singular-in-disguise` Singular in disguise: covariance from fewer days than assets | Show that a sample covariance of $p$ assets from $n \le p$ observations has rank at most $n - 1$, detect numerical rank with a tolerance, and repair the problem by shrinkage or a factor model | 500 shares and 250 days of returns: the rank of the sample covariance? (249). `np.linalg.solve` returns minimum-variance weights of size $3 \times 10^{21}$ without an error. Why no error? (rounding leaves the zero pivots tiny but not exactly zero) | `svd-stepper`, `value-table`, `choice` | Trap: no exception means the matrix was invertible (`mc.numerics.no-error-no-problem`). Edge: numerical rank depends on the tolerance; NumPy's default is $\sigma_1\max(m, n)\varepsilon_{\text{mach}}$; shrinkage (1.4 lesson 95) restores full rank | W, C, S, N |

Transfer screens: lesson 35 is 1.4 lesson 80's minimum-variance portfolio at desk scale; lesson 33 is how GPUs solve in double precision at single-precision cost.

Then the Level review and homework `set.numlinalg.solving`.

**Level NL2: Structure: symmetric, banded and sparse** (`lvl.numlinalg.structure`, 4 lessons)

| # | Lesson | Objective | Hook | Widgets | Trap and edge case | Quiz |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 36 | `lsn.numlinalg.valid-correlations` Cholesky's limits and valid correlations | Find the range of correlations that keeps a matrix positive semidefinite, use Cholesky as the test, and explain why a matrix on the boundary fails Cholesky in floating point | Two shares each have correlation 0.9 with the market. How low can their correlation with each other be? (0.62: $\rho_{12}\rho_{13} - \sqrt{(1 - \rho_{12}^2)(1 - \rho_{13}^2)}$) | `quadratic-form` (covariance ellipses), `matrix`, `numeric-input` | Trap: a symmetric matrix with unit diagonal and entries in $[-1, 1]$ is a correlation matrix (`mc.numerics.entrywise-valid`). Edge: at exactly 0.62 the matrix is singular; rounding makes its smallest eigenvalue $-1.1 \times 10^{-16}$ and Cholesky fails, so simulations add a small diagonal "jitter" or use $LDL^\top$ | N, C, N, M |
| 37 | `lsn.numlinalg.nearest-correlation` Repairing a correlation matrix | Repair an invalid correlation matrix by eigenvalue clipping with rescaling and by Higham's alternating projections, and compare the two by distance from the input | A desk's estimates are 0.9, 0.9 and 0.5: invalid. The nearest correlation matrix in the Frobenius norm? (0.8708, 0.8708, 0.5167, at distance 0.0629; clipping then rescaling gives 0.8674, 0.8674, 0.5047, at 0.0656) | `matrix`, `quadratic-form`, `numeric-input` | Trap: clip the negative eigenvalues and stop (`mc.numerics.clip-is-enough`); the diagonal no longer holds ones. Edge: the nearest matrix is unique because the valid set is convex (2.0b lesson 20); Higham (2002) | N, C, S, N |
| 38 | `lsn.numlinalg.tridiagonal` Banded and tridiagonal systems | Solve a tridiagonal system by the Thomas algorithm in about $8n$ operations, state when it is stable without pivoting (diagonal dominance), and recognise where tridiagonal systems arise | A tridiagonal system with $n = 10^6$: dense LU costs about $\frac23n^3 \approx 7 \times 10^{17}$ operations. The Thomas algorithm? (about $8 \times 10^6$: milliseconds) | `matrix` (spy [mode to add]), `stepper`, `numeric-input` | Trap: store the matrix and call a dense solver (`mc.numerics.dense-default`). Edge: cubic splines (lesson 23) and every implicit finite-difference step (course PD) are tridiagonal | N, N, O, C |
| 39 | `lsn.numlinalg.sparse-fill` Sparse matrices and fill-in | Store a sparse matrix in compressed form, predict fill-in during elimination, and reduce it by reordering | An arrowhead matrix of size 200 with a dense first row and column has 598 non-zeros. Its LU factors have? (40,200: complete fill). With the dense row and column last? (798) | `matrix` (spy), `elimination-lab`, `choice` | Trap: sparse in, sparse out (`mc.numerics.sparse-stays-sparse`). Edge: sparse direct solvers choose an ordering for you (`scipy.sparse.linalg.splu` uses COLAMD by default); the inverse of a sparse matrix is usually dense, one more reason never to form it | N, N, C, S |

Transfer screens: lesson 36 decides whether a desk's stress-test correlations can be simulated at all; lesson 38 makes each implicit PDE step in course PD cost the same as an explicit one.

Then the Level review and homework `set.numlinalg.structure`.

**Level NL3: Eigenvalues and the SVD in floating point** (`lvl.numlinalg.eigen`, 4 lessons)

| # | Lesson | Objective | Hook | Widgets | Trap and edge case | Quiz |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 40 | `lsn.numlinalg.inverse-iteration` Shifted inverse iteration and the Rayleigh quotient | Find the eigenvalue nearest a shift $\mu$ by iterating with $(A - \mu I)^{-1}$, predict its rate $\frac{\lvert\lambda_1 - \mu\rvert}{\lvert\lambda_2 - \mu\rvert}$, and accelerate it with Rayleigh-quotient shifts | Eigenvalues 1 and 0.99: power iteration needs about 1,375 steps to gain 6 digits. Inverse iteration with shift 1.004? (about 11: the rate is $\frac{0.004}{0.014} = 0.29$) | `linear-dynamics` (inverse-iteration [mode to add]), `convergence-plot`, `numeric-input` | Trap: solving with the nearly singular $A - \mu I$ ruins the answer (`mc.numerics.near-singular-ruins`); the large error points along the wanted eigenvector. Edge: Rayleigh-quotient iteration on a symmetric matrix converges cubically | N, N, C, M |
| 41 | `lsn.numlinalg.qr-shifts` The QR algorithm with shifts and deflation | Explain how shifts make the QR algorithm converge cubically on symmetric matrices, why software first reduces to tridiagonal form, and why `eigh`, not `eig`, is the routine for symmetric matrices | Unshifted QR on eigenvalues 1, 0.99 and 0.5 needs about 1,400 steps to split the first two. With the Wilkinson shift the last off-diagonal entry falls 0.082, 0.035, $8 \times 10^{-4}$, $8 \times 10^{-9}$. Steps to full precision? (one more) | `svd-stepper` (eigen mode), `convergence-plot`, `choice` | Trap: eigenvalues are computed as roots of the characteristic polynomial (`mc.numerics.charpoly-roots`; 1.4 lesson 98). Edge: `eig` on a symmetric matrix can return complex numbers with tiny imaginary parts and non-orthogonal vectors; `eigh` guarantees real eigenvalues and orthonormal vectors | N, C, O, S |
| 42 | `lsn.numlinalg.eigen-sensitivity` How sensitive are eigenvalues and eigenvectors? | Bound a symmetric matrix's eigenvalue changes by $\lVert E\rVert_2$ (Weyl), estimate eigenvector rotation by $\frac{\lVert E\rVert}{\text{gap}}$, and show a non-symmetric eigenvalue moving by $\sqrt\varepsilon$ | Perturb the bottom-left entry of $\begin{pmatrix}1&1\\0&1\end{pmatrix}$ by $10^{-10}$. How far do its eigenvalues move? ($10^{-5}$: they become $1 \pm 10^{-5}$) | `eigen-finder` (perturb [mode to add]), `pca-lab`, `numeric-input` | Trap: a small change to a matrix makes a small change to its eigenvectors (`mc.numerics.eigvec-stable`). Edge: two nearly equal principal components of returns swap or rotate between estimation windows, so a factor defined as "component 2" is unstable while the plane the two span is stable | N, C, M, W |
| 43 | `lsn.numlinalg.svd-not-ata` Computing the SVD without $A^\top A$ | Explain why the SVD is computed by bidiagonalisation rather than from the eigenvalues of $A^\top A$, and show that singular values below $\sqrt u\,\sigma_1$ are lost that way | Läuchli's matrix: rows $(1, 1)$, $(\delta, 0)$, $(0, \delta)$ with $\delta = 10^{-9}$. Smallest singular value from `eigvalsh(A.T @ A)`? (0). From `svd(A)`? ($10^{-9}$) | `svd-stepper`, `float-inspector` (cancellation), `choice` | Trap: the SVD is the eigen-decomposition of $A^\top A$, so compute it that way (`mc.numerics.svd-via-ata`; 1.4 lesson 84 did it by hand). Edge: $1 + \delta^2$ rounds to 1 for $\delta$ below about $10^{-8}$, so $A^\top A$ is exactly singular in floating point | N, C, N, M |

Transfer screens: lesson 42 is why statistical factor models are rotated and why hedges built on small principal components are fragile; lesson 43 is why `lstsq` uses the SVD.

Then the Level review and homework `set.numlinalg.eigen`.

**Level NL4: Iterative methods** (`lvl.numlinalg.iterative`, 5 lessons)

| # | Lesson | Objective | Hook | Widgets | Trap and edge case | Quiz |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 44 | `lsn.numlinalg.splitting` Jacobi and Gauss-Seidel | Write a splitting $A = M - N$ as $\mathbf x_{k+1} = M^{-1}(N\mathbf x_k + \mathbf b)$, prove it converges when the spectral radius of $M^{-1}N$ is below 1, and use diagonal dominance as a sufficient test | Jacobi on $\begin{pmatrix}4&1\\2&5\end{pmatrix}\mathbf x = \begin{pmatrix}1\\2\end{pmatrix}$ converges to $\left(\frac16, \frac13\right)$ (spectral radius 0.32). With the two equations swapped? (it diverges: spectral radius 3.16; after 10 steps $(-16{,}666.5, -33{,}333)$) | `linear-dynamics` (iteration), `matrix`, `numeric-input` | Trap: an iterative method works for any invertible matrix (`mc.numerics.iterative-universal`). Edge: the order of the equations changes convergence while changing nothing about the solution | N, N, C, W |
| 45 | `lsn.numlinalg.sor` Successive over-relaxation and the speed of convergence | Predict iteration counts from the spectral radius, compare Jacobi, Gauss-Seidel and SOR on the model problem, and choose the relaxation factor | The 1-D model problem with 100 unknowns: Jacobi's spectral radius is 0.99952. Iterations to gain 6 digits? (about 28,600; Gauss-Seidel about 14,300; SOR with $\omega = 1.94$ about 220) | `convergence-plot`, `slider` ($\omega$), `numeric-input` | Trap: more sweeps fix everything (`mc.numerics.more-sweeps`). Edge: SOR diverges for $\omega \ge 2$; projected SOR is the American-option solver of lesson 63 | N, N, C, S |
| 46 | `lsn.numlinalg.conjugate-gradient` Conjugate gradients | Derive CG as minimising $\frac12\mathbf x^\top A\mathbf x - \mathbf b^\top\mathbf x$ over growing Krylov spaces along $A$-conjugate directions, and compare its bound $2\left(\frac{\sqrt\kappa - 1}{\sqrt\kappa + 1}\right)^k$ with steepest descent's $\left(\frac{\kappa - 1}{\kappa + 1}\right)^k$ | $\kappa = 10^4$: steepest descent needs about 69,000 iterations to gain 6 digits. CG? (about 725) | `descent-lab` (cg [mode to add]), `convergence-plot`, `numeric-input` | Trap: CG is a direct method that takes exactly $n$ steps (`mc.numerics.cg-direct`). Edge: in exact arithmetic CG finishes in as many steps as $A$ has distinct eigenvalues: 2 for eigenvalues $\{1, 1, 100\}$ (homework NL4) | N, W, C, O |
| 47 | `lsn.numlinalg.preconditioning` Preconditioning | Solve $M^{-1}A\mathbf x = M^{-1}\mathbf b$ with $M \approx A$ cheap to invert, and show a diagonal scaling removes bad units | $A = DBD$ with $B = \begin{pmatrix}2&1\\1&2\end{pmatrix}$ and $D = \operatorname{diag}(10^3, 10^{-3})$, as when one unknown is in pence and one in millions: $\kappa(A) = 1.3 \times 10^{12}$. After scaling rows and columns by the square roots of the diagonal? (3) | `condition-number`, `matrix`, `numeric-input` | Trap: preconditioning changes the solution (`mc.numerics.precond-changes-answer`). Edge: incomplete Cholesky keeps $A$'s sparsity; the best preconditioner is knowledge of the problem, such as a coarser grid | N, N, C, X |
| 48 | `lsn.numlinalg.choosing-solver` Choosing a solver | Match a linear-algebra problem to its method by size, structure, symmetry and the accuracy it needs | Five problems: a $50 \times 50$ dense system; a tridiagonal system of $10^6$; a sparse symmetric positive definite system of $10^7$; an overdetermined fit; a Hessian you can only multiply by. Match each to LU, Thomas, preconditioned CG, QR least squares and matrix-free CG | `card-sort`, `choice` | Trap: `np.linalg.solve` for everything (`mc.numerics.one-solver`). Edge: matrix-free methods need only products $A\mathbf v$, which is how second-order methods run on neural networks (2.1b) | S, C, M, S |

Transfer screens: lesson 46 returns as an optimiser in 2.0b lesson 43; lesson 45's SOR solves the American option in lesson 63.

Then the Level review and homework `set.numlinalg.iterative`.

### Course PD: Finite Differences and the Pricing Equation (`crs.pde`, 3 levels, 17 lessons)

1.7 priced options on trees and by the Black-Scholes formula; 1.3 met a single partial differential equation and checked a solution of it. Canon-gap P8, which would have taught differential equations, was not adopted, so this course builds the equation itself first (level PD1: the heat equation from a random walk, its Fourier modes, the Black-Scholes equation from a binomial tree, the change of variables between them, boundary conditions), then the stability theory of time stepping (level PD2, including stiff ordinary differential equations), then pricing on grids (level PD3: European, American and barrier options, Greeks, and the comparison with trees). Spine: Wilmott, Howison and Dewynne chapters 3 to 5, 8 and 9 (8 and 9 [searched]; 3 to 5 [recalled]); for stability of time stepping, Süli and Mayers chapter 12.

**Level PD1: From trees to the heat equation** (`lvl.pde.heat`, 5 lessons)

| # | Lesson | Objective | Hook | Widgets | Trap and edge case | Quiz |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 49 | `lsn.pde.random-walk-limit` The heat equation from a random walk | Derive $u_t = \frac{\Delta x^2}{2\Delta t}u_{xx}$ by Taylor-expanding the averaging rule $u(x, t + \Delta t) = \frac12[u(x + \Delta x, t) + u(x - \Delta x, t)]$, and read the heat equation as the law of a diffusing particle | A particle steps $\pm\Delta x$ every $\Delta t$. Taylor-expand the averaging rule: which equation appears, and what must $\frac{\Delta x^2}{\Delta t}$ do as both shrink? (the heat equation; the ratio must stay fixed) | `heat-diffusion`, `path-simulator` (scaled walk), `derivation-steps` | Trap: spread grows in proportion to time (`mc.numerics.diffusion-linear`); it grows like $\sqrt t$. Edge: the averaging rule is already a numerical scheme, lesson 56's explicit scheme with $r = \frac12$ | X, C, O, W |
| 50 | `lsn.pde.fourier-modes` Fourier modes and separation of variables | Solve $u_t = u_{xx}$ on an interval with zero boundary values by separating variables, show each mode $\sin kx$ decays like $e^{-k^2t}$, and explain why the equation smooths and why running it backwards is ill-posed | Start with $\sin x + \sin 10x$ on $[0, \pi]$. At $t = 0.1$, how much of each mode is left? ($e^{-0.1} = 0.905$ of the first, $e^{-10} = 4.5 \times 10^{-5}$ of the second) | `heat-diffusion` (modes [mode to add]), `slider`, `numeric-input` | Trap: the heat equation runs backwards in time as easily as forwards (`mc.numerics.backward-heat`). Edge: option prices run backwards in calendar time, which is forwards in time to expiry $\tau = T - t$, so pricing is well posed (lesson 52) | N, N, C, M |
| 51 | `lsn.pde.bs-from-tree` The Black-Scholes equation from a tree | Derive $V_t + \frac12\sigma^2S^2V_{SS} + rSV_S - rV = 0$ by Taylor-expanding the risk-neutral one-step recursion of a binomial tree to order $\Delta t$ | In a tree with log-steps $\pm\sigma\sqrt{\Delta t}$ and risk-neutral probability $q$, expand $V(Se^{\pm\sigma\sqrt{\Delta t}}, t + \Delta t)$ to second order. Which terms survive as $\Delta t \to 0$? (those of order $\Delta t$: the time derivative, $\frac12\sigma^2S^2V_{SS}$ from the squared step, and $rSV_S - rV$ from the risk-neutral drift and the discounting) | `binomial-tree` (as-scheme [mode to add]), `derivation-steps`, `choice` | Trap: the share's real drift $\mu$ appears in the equation (`mc.numerics.real-drift-in-pde`). Edge: the expansion needs a smooth $V$, and at expiry the payoff has a kink, which is where every scheme of this course loses accuracy (lesson 61); 3.2 derives the same equation by hedging with Itô's formula | C, X, O, W |
| 52 | `lsn.pde.to-heat` From Black-Scholes to the heat equation | Transform the Black-Scholes equation by $x = \ln\frac SK$, $\tau = \frac12\sigma^2(T - t)$ and $V = Ke^{\alpha x + \beta\tau}u$ into $u_\tau = u_{xx}$, finding $\alpha = -\frac{k - 1}2$ and $\beta = -\frac{(k + 1)^2}4$ with $k = \frac{2r}{\sigma^2}$ | Which substitution removes the $S^2$ in front of $V_{SS}$? ($x = \ln S$: then $S^2V_{SS} = V_{xx} - V_x$) | `derivation-steps`, `heat-diffusion`, `expression-input` | Trap: forget that $\tau$ runs backwards from expiry (`mc.numerics.time-direction`). Edge: the transformed problem shows the price is the payoff smoothed by a Gaussian kernel, which gives the Black-Scholes formula (Wilmott, Howison and Dewynne, chapter 5 [check]) | X, X, O, W |
| 53 | `lsn.pde.boundaries` Domains, boundaries and terminal conditions | Truncate the share price at $S_{\max}$, set boundary values for calls and puts ($V(0, t) = 0$ and $V(S_{\max}, t) \approx S_{\max} - Ke^{-r(T - t)}$ for a call), and treat a knock-out barrier as a boundary | A grid for the 1-year at-the-money call ($\sigma = 20\%$, $r = 5\%$) stops at $S_{\max} = 200$ instead of 400. How much price does that cost? (less than $10^{-8}$: the risk-neutral chance of passing 200 is 0.0005, and the boundary value is nearly right there) | `fd-grid` (new: domain mode), `bs-explorer`, `numeric-input` | Trap: a far boundary costs nothing (`mc.numerics.free-boundary-distance`); on a uniform grid it costs points. Edge: a down-and-out barrier is a natural boundary, with $V = 0$ exactly | N, C, N, S |

Transfer screens: lesson 51 shows that the tree and the PDE are one model; lesson 53 sets up the barrier options of lesson 64.

Then the Level review and homework `set.pde.heat`.

**Level PD2: Time stepping and stability** (`lvl.pde.stability`, 6 lessons)

| # | Lesson | Objective | Hook | Widgets | Trap and edge case | Quiz |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 54 | `lsn.pde.stiff-odes` Stiff equations and stability regions | Show that explicit Euler on $y' = -\lambda y$ is stable only for $h\lambda \le 2$ and implicit Euler for every $h$, and compare their stability functions $1 + z$ and $\frac1{1 - z}$ with the trapezoidal rule's $\frac{1 + z/2}{1 - z/2}$ | $y' = -1000y$, $y(0) = 1$, Euler with $h = 0.01$: $y$ after 10 steps? ($(-9)^{10} \approx 3.5 \times 10^9$, against the true $e^{-10} = 4.5 \times 10^{-5}$) | `slope-field` (Euler, stiff [mode to add]), `plot-2d`, `numeric-input` | Trap: the step size is set by accuracy alone (`mc.numerics.accuracy-sets-step`). Edge: Runge-Kutta 4 has a larger stability region but is still explicit, so the stiffness limit remains; 1.3 lesson 84 met Euler's accuracy, not its stability | N, W, C, S |
| 55 | `lsn.pde.method-of-lines` The method of lines | Replace $u_{xx}$ by $\frac{u_{j+1} - 2u_j + u_{j-1}}{\Delta x^2}$ to get $\frac{d\mathbf u}{d\tau} = D\mathbf u$, find that $D$'s eigenvalues run from about $-\pi^2$ to $-\frac4{\Delta x^2}$ on $[0, 1]$, and conclude that explicit steps need $\Delta\tau \le \frac{\Delta x^2}2$ | With $\Delta x = 0.01$ the most negative eigenvalue of $D$ is about? ($-40{,}000$). The largest stable explicit $\Delta\tau$? ($5 \times 10^{-5}$) | `fd-grid` (mol), `eigen-finder`, `numeric-input` | Trap: space and time steps can be chosen independently in an explicit scheme (`mc.numerics.independent-steps`). Edge: halving $\Delta x$ quarters the allowed $\Delta\tau$, so refining the grid by 2 costs 8 times the work (2.3 lesson 164's hook) | N, N, C, M |
| 56 | `lsn.pde.explicit-von-neumann` The explicit scheme and von Neumann stability | Write $u_j^{n+1} = u_j^n + r(u_{j+1}^n - 2u_j^n + u_{j-1}^n)$, derive its amplification factor $g = 1 - 4r\sin^2\frac{k\Delta x}2$, and prove stability exactly when $r = \frac{\Delta\tau}{\Delta x^2} \le \frac12$ | The explicit scheme with $r = 0.52$ on a smooth start matches the true solution for 400 steps; by step 600 its values are near 1,000. Why so late? (the highest mode grows by 1.08 a step from rounding noise of about $10^{-16}$, and $1.08^{479} \approx 10^{16}$) | `fd-grid` (explicit, stability), `slider` ($r$), `numeric-input` | Trap: an unstable scheme fails at once (`mc.numerics.instability-immediate`). Edge: the explicit scheme is a trinomial tree with probabilities $r$, $1 - 2r$, $r$, so $r > \frac12$ is a negative probability | X, N, C, W |
| 57 | `lsn.pde.implicit` Implicit Euler in time | Step with $(I - \Delta\tau D)\mathbf u^{n+1} = \mathbf u^n$ by the Thomas algorithm, derive $g = \frac1{1 + 4r\sin^2(k\Delta x/2)}$, and separate stability from accuracy | Implicit Euler with $r = 50$: stable? (yes: $0 < g < 1$ for every mode). Accurate? (first order in time: halving $\Delta\tau$ halves the time error) | `fd-grid` (implicit), `convergence-plot`, `choice` | Trap: unconditionally stable means any step is accurate (`mc.numerics.stable-is-accurate`). Edge: each implicit step costs about as much as an explicit one, $O(n)$, because the system is tridiagonal (lesson 38) | C, N, N, M |
| 58 | `lsn.pde.crank-nicolson` Crank-Nicolson and its oscillations | Average the explicit and implicit steps to reach second order in time, derive $g = \frac{1 - 2rs^2}{1 + 2rs^2}$ with $s = \sin\frac{k\Delta x}2$, and explain the oscillations near a kink when $r$ is large, and Rannacher's fix | With $r = 50$ the highest mode has $g = -\frac{99}{101} = -0.98$. After 10 steps, how much of it is left? ($0.98^{10} = 0.82$, with its sign flipping every step: it barely decays) | `fd-grid` (crank-nicolson, gamma overlay), `slider`, `numeric-input` | Trap: unconditionally stable and second order means free of artefacts (`mc.numerics.cn-artefact-free`). Edge: two to four implicit Euler steps at the start damp the high modes the payoff's kink creates (Rannacher 1984) | N, N, C, W |
| 59 | `lsn.pde.theta-orders` The θ-scheme and measuring order | Write the explicit, implicit and Crank-Nicolson schemes as one θ-scheme, state their local and global errors, and measure the order from three refinements | A Crank-Nicolson grid for the 1-year at-the-money call ($r = 5\%$, $\sigma = 20\%$, $S_{\max} = 400$) with $N$ price steps and $N$ time steps: errors $-0.0400$, $-0.0099$ and $-0.0025$ at $N = 100$, 200 and 400. Order? (2: each halving divides the error by 4) | `convergence-plot` (log-log), `fd-grid`, `numeric-input` | Trap: refine one direction and expect the full order (`mc.numerics.refine-one-direction`); the other direction's error becomes a floor. Edge: implicit Euler with $\Delta\tau \propto \Delta S$ shows order 1, because its time error dominates | N, N, C, P |

Transfer screens: lesson 56 shows that a binomial tree is a stability-limited explicit scheme; lesson 59 is half of the 2.0a exit's error-against-work plot.

Then the Level review and homework `set.pde.stability`.
