| # | Lesson | Objective | Hook | Widgets | Trap and edge case | Quiz |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 60 | `lsn.pde.european-grid` A European option on a grid | Assemble the Crank-Nicolson system for the Black-Scholes equation in $S$, with boundary rows and the terminal payoff, and read the price at $S_0$ by interpolation | A grid with $S_{\max} = 400$, 400 price steps and 400 time steps prices the 1-year at-the-money call ($S = K = 100$, $r = 5\%$, $\sigma = 20\%$). Its error against 10.4506? ($-0.0025$) | `fd-grid` (bs), `bs-explorer`, `numeric-input` | Trap: read the nearest node when $S_0$ falls between nodes (`mc.numerics.nearest-node`). Edge: with 50 price steps of 8, $S_0 = 100$ and the strike fall between nodes and the error jumps to 0.14 | N, C, N, M |
| 61 | `lsn.pde.strike-placement` The kink, the strike and the grid | Keep the strike in the same place relative to the nodes at every refinement (on a node, or cell-averaged), and explain why otherwise the measured order wanders | With the strike on a node, refinements give errors $-0.040$, $-0.0099$, $-0.0025$, $-0.00062$ (ratio 4 each time). With the strike at 0.75, 0.25, 0.5 and 0.25 of a cell: $0.0162$, $0.0039$, $0.0021$, $0.00025$. Ratios? (4.2, 1.8, 8.5: no order to extrapolate) | `fd-grid` (payoff smoothing), `convergence-plot`, `choice` | Trap: more nodes always smooth the error (`mc.numerics.refine-smooths`). Edge: for a digital payoff, set the strike node to $\frac12$ or average the payoff over its cell (Pooley, Vetzal and Forsyth 2003) | C, N, C, M |
| 62 | `lsn.pde.greeks-grid` Greeks from the grid | Compute delta and gamma by central differences of neighbouring grid values, theta from the time step, and vega from a second grid with a bumped $\sigma$ | Call values 9.2151, 10.4506 and 11.7611 at $S = 98$, 100 and 102. Delta and gamma? ($\frac{11.7611 - 9.2151}4 = 0.6365$; $\frac{11.7611 - 2 \times 10.4506 + 9.2151}4 = 0.01875$; exact 0.6368 and 0.01876) | `fd-grid` (greeks), `greeks-lab`, `numeric-input` | Trap: bump and revalue on two grids whose nodes move with $S_0$ (`mc.numerics.moving-grid-bump`). Edge: Crank-Nicolson's gamma oscillates near the strike for large $r$ (lesson 58), and Rannacher steps fix it | N, N, C, S |
| 63 | `lsn.pde.american-psor` American options: the free boundary | Write the American put as a linear complementarity problem ($V \ge$ payoff, the Black-Scholes operator $\le 0$, equality in one of them), solve each step by projected SOR, and read off the exercise boundary | American put, $S = K = 100$, $r = 5\%$, $\sigma = 20\%$, $T = 1$: the European put is 5.5735. The American? (6.090: an early-exercise premium of 0.517) | `fd-grid` (american), `binomial-tree` (american), `numeric-input` | Trap: take $\max(V, \text{payoff})$ after each Crank-Nicolson step and call it exact (`mc.numerics.projection-exact`); it converges, more slowly than PSOR (6.086 on a 400 by 400 grid). Edge: an American call on a share without dividends is never exercised early, and the grid shows it | N, N, C, M |
| 64 | `lsn.pde.barriers` Barrier options on a grid | Price a knock-out option with the barrier on a node as a zero boundary, and price discrete monitoring with a continuous model by shifting the barrier by $0.5826\,\sigma\sqrt{\Delta t}$ | A down-and-out call with barrier 90, monitored daily: worth more or less than with continuous monitoring? (more: fewer chances to knock out). Which continuous barrier prices it? ($90e^{-0.5826 \times 0.2/\sqrt{252}} = 89.34$; Broadie, Glasserman and Kou 1997) | `fd-grid` (barrier), `path-simulator` (barrier), `numeric-input` | Trap: monitoring at a grid's dates is continuous monitoring (`mc.numerics.discrete-is-continuous`). Edge: $0.5826 = -\frac{\zeta(1/2)}{\sqrt{2\pi}}$; a barrier between nodes costs the scheme its order | C, N, N, M |
| 65 | `lsn.pde.trees-vs-grids` Trees, grids and the cost of accuracy | Compare the binomial tree (error $O(N^{-1})$ with an odd-even wobble, $\frac{N^2}2$ nodes) with Crank-Nicolson (error $O(N^{-2})$, $N^2$ nodes) by error against work | To price the at-the-money call to $2.5 \times 10^{-3}$: the tree needs 800 steps ($3.2 \times 10^5$ nodes), Crank-Nicolson a 400 by 400 grid ($1.6 \times 10^5$). To $6 \times 10^{-4}$? (tree about 3,300 steps, $5.4 \times 10^6$ nodes; Crank-Nicolson 800 by 800, $6.4 \times 10^5$) | `pricer-compare` (pde, work [mode to add]), `convergence-plot`, `numeric-input` | Trap: compare methods by error against their own $N$ (`mc.numerics.error-vs-n`). Edge: trees handle discrete dividends and early exercise with no extra machinery; grids give smooth Greeks | N, N, C, S |

Transfer screens: lesson 63 is how listed American options are priced; lesson 65 is the PDE half of the exit.

Then the Level review and homework `set.pde.pricing`.

### Course MO: Monte Carlo Methods (`crs.montecarlo`, 5 levels, 26 lessons)

1.1 simulated with seeds and standard errors; 1.5 proved the laws that make simulation work. This course is the method as a quant practises it: where random numbers come from and how they fail, how to size a run and separate bias from noise, the five standard ways to reduce variance, quasi-Monte Carlo, sensitivities and early exercise, and the exit's comparison of four pricers by error against work. Spine: Glasserman chapters 1 to 8, chapter 6 only for discretisation bias [check section numbers throughout]; Owen's free *Monte Carlo theory, methods and examples* as a second explanation.

**Level MO1: Random numbers** (`lvl.montecarlo.rng`, 5 lessons)

| # | Lesson | Objective | Hook | Widgets | Trap and edge case | Quiz |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 66 | `lsn.montecarlo.lcg` What a pseudo-random generator is | Describe a generator as a state, an update and an output, compute a linear congruential generator's period, and see its lattice structure | IBM's RANDU used $x_{k+1} = 65{,}539\,x_k \bmod 2^{31}$. Plot consecutive triples in 3-D and rotate: what appears? (15 planes, because $x_{k+2} = 6x_{k+1} - 9x_k \bmod 2^{31}$) | `rng-lab` (new: lcg, lattice), `plot-3d`, `choice` | Trap: output that looks random is random enough (`mc.numerics.looks-random`). Edge: Marsaglia (1968) showed every such generator's $d$-tuples lie on parallel planes; the question is how far apart they are | C, N, S, W |
| 67 | `lsn.montecarlo.generators-streams` Modern generators, seeds and streams | Compare the Mersenne Twister, PCG64 (NumPy's default) and Philox (counter-based, the app's own), give parallel workers independent streams, and avoid seed collisions | 1,000 parallel jobs each seeded with a random 32-bit integer: chance that two share a seed? ($1.2 \times 10^{-4}$, the birthday problem). Seeded 1, 2, 3, ... instead? (no collision, but for some generators correlated streams) | `rng-lab` (streams), `sim-runner`, `numeric-input` | Trap: seeds 1, 2, 3, ... give independent streams (`mc.numerics.sequential-seeds`). Edge: a counter-based generator computes the $k$-th draw directly, which is how this app replays any simulation exactly (`ARCHITECTURE.md` decision 14) | N, C, N, S |
| 68 | `lsn.montecarlo.inverse-transform` Inverse transform and its numerical limits | Generate $X = F^{-1}(U)$, guard $\ln U$ against $U = 0$, and show that a uniform with 53 random bits bounds the tail you can reach | A uniform with 53 random bits is never below $2^{-53}$ unless it is 0. The most negative normal $\Phi^{-1}(U)$ can produce? ($-8.21$) | `rng-lab` (inverse), `dist-explorer`, `numeric-input` | Trap: inverse transform reaches any tail you need (`mc.numerics.unbounded-tail`). Edge: a probability of $10^{-20}$ needs a sampler aimed at the tail (lesson 79), not more uniforms | N, C, N, I |
| 69 | `lsn.montecarlo.rejection` Normals, the polar method and acceptance-rejection | Generate normals by Box-Muller and Marsaglia's polar method, and sample by acceptance-rejection with acceptance rate $\frac1c$ | Sample a standard normal conditioned on $Z > 4$ by drawing normals and rejecting those at or below 4. Draws per accepted sample? (about 31,600) | `rng-lab` (rejection), `dist-explorer`, `numeric-input` | Trap: rejection is always cheap (`mc.numerics.rejection-cheap`). Edge: an exponential proposal shifted to the cut-off accepts most draws; the polar method accepts $\frac\pi4 = 78.5\%$ of its pairs | N, N, C, S |
| 70 | `lsn.montecarlo.correlated-paths` Correlated normals and exact paths | Turn independent normals into correlated ones with any $L$ such that $LL^\top = \Sigma$ (Cholesky or eigenvectors), and simulate geometric Brownian motion exactly in logs | Two assets with correlation $-0.99$: do $L$ from Cholesky and $L$ from the eigen-decomposition give the same joint distribution? (yes; the individual sample paths differ) | `path-simulator` (correlated), `covariance-lab`, `choice` | Trap: correlate the simulated prices instead of the shocks (`mc.numerics.correlate-prices`). Edge: for quasi-Monte Carlo the choice of $L$ matters, because it decides which coordinates carry the variance (lesson 85) | C, N, A, X |

Transfer screens: lesson 67 is how a risk system's overnight simulation is reproduced for an audit; lesson 70 is every multi-asset simulation.

Then the Level review and homework `set.montecarlo.rng`.

**Level MO2: Estimators, error and bias** (`lvl.montecarlo.error`, 5 lessons)

| # | Lesson | Objective | Hook | Widgets | Trap and edge case | Quiz |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 71 | `lsn.montecarlo.estimator-error` The estimator, its error and rare events | Attach a standard error $\frac{\hat\sigma}{\sqrt N}$ to an estimate, show that it does not depend on dimension, and size $N$ for a relative error $\varepsilon$ on a probability $p$: $N \approx \frac{1 - p}{p\varepsilon^2}$ | To estimate a default probability of $10^{-4}$ with a 10% relative standard error, how many paths? (about $10^6$) | `mc-variance` (new: single estimator), `sim-runner`, `numeric-input` | Trap: Monte Carlo's accuracy depends on the dimension (`mc.numerics.mc-dimension`). Edge: for rare events the relative error grows like $\frac1{\sqrt{Np}}$, which is why importance sampling exists (lesson 79); 1.1 lesson 70 attached the error, this lesson sizes the run | N, N, C, I |
| 72 | `lsn.montecarlo.efficiency` Efficiency: variance times cost | Compare estimators by variance per unit of computing time, and decide when a costlier estimator wins | Estimator A has variance 4 and costs 1 μs a path; B has variance 0.1 and costs 20 μs. Which is better for a fixed budget, and by how much? (B: $0.1 \times 20 = 2$ against $4 \times 1 = 4$, half the error variance for the same time) | `mc-variance` (efficiency), `value-table`, `numeric-input` | Trap: compare variances per path (`mc.numerics.variance-per-path`). Edge: a variance reduction that doubles the cost must more than halve the variance to pay (Glasserman section 1.1 [check]) | C, N, N, S |
| 73 | `lsn.montecarlo.discretisation-bias` Discretisation bias against statistical error | Split $\text{RMSE}^2 = \text{bias}^2 + \frac{\sigma^2}N$, and choose $\Delta t \propto \varepsilon$ and $N \propto \varepsilon^{-2}$ for a weak first-order scheme, a cost of order $\varepsilon^{-3}$ | Euler-Maruyama call prices on GBM with 1, 2, 4 and 8 steps have biases $-0.247$, $-0.078$, $-0.030$ and $-0.011$. With $2 \times 10^6$ paths (standard error 0.01), from which step count is the bias hidden in the noise? (8) | `sde-stepper`, `convergence-plot`, `numeric-input` | Trap: more paths cure every error (`mc.numerics.paths-cure-bias`). Edge: GBM can be simulated exactly in logs in one step, so its only error is statistical; multilevel Monte Carlo (Giles 2008) cuts the cost to about $\varepsilon^{-2}$ | N, C, N, P |
| 74 | `lsn.montecarlo.strong-weak` Strong and weak order | Measure Euler-Maruyama's strong (pathwise) order $\frac12$ and weak (expectation) order 1, and say which a pricing or a hedging task needs | Euler-Maruyama on GBM with 16, 64 and 256 steps: mean pathwise errors 0.58, 0.30 and 0.15. Strong order? ($\frac12$: quadrupling the steps halves the error) | `sde-stepper` (strong and weak error [mode to add]), `convergence-plot`, `numeric-input` | Trap: one order describes a scheme (`mc.numerics.one-order`). Edge: Milstein's scheme has strong order 1 at the same weak order; a discontinuous payoff spoils the weak order | N, C, N, S |
| 75 | `lsn.montecarlo.path-dependence` Path dependence: monitoring and the Brownian bridge | Correct a barrier simulated on a coarse grid with the Brownian-bridge crossing probability $\exp\left(-\frac{2(x_i - b)(x_{i+1} - b)}{\sigma^2\Delta t}\right)$ between steps, in log price | A down-and-out call simulated with 12 monthly steps misses crossings between the dates. With the bridge probability on each step, how many steps does a continuously monitored price need? (12 suffice: the bridge accounts for the gaps) | `path-simulator` (bridge), `mc-variance`, `choice` | Trap: checking the barrier at the simulation dates is continuous monitoring (`mc.numerics.dates-are-continuous`). Edge: a contract monitored daily is a different contract from a continuous one (lesson 64) | C, N, O, S |

Transfer screens: lesson 73 is the cost model of every Monte Carlo risk engine; lesson 75 is how barrier products are simulated cheaply.

Then the Level review and homework `set.montecarlo.error`.

**Level MO3: Variance reduction** (`lvl.montecarlo.variance-reduction`, 6 lessons)

| # | Lesson | Objective | Hook | Widgets | Trap and edge case | Quiz |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 76 | `lsn.montecarlo.antithetic` Antithetic variates | Pair each $Z$ with $-Z$, show the pair's variance is $\frac{\sigma^2}2(1 + \rho)$ with $\rho = \operatorname{Corr}(f(Z), f(-Z))$, and predict when it helps | For an at-the-money call, an antithetic pair has half the variance of two independent paths. For a straddle? (1.65 times: worse, because the straddle's payoff is symmetric, so $f(Z)$ and $f(-Z)$ are positively correlated) | `mc-variance` (antithetic), `plot-2d`, `numeric-input` | Trap: antithetic variates always reduce variance (`mc.numerics.antithetic-always`). Edge: for a monotone payoff they never hurt; 2.3 lesson 162 applies them to real chains | N, C, N, S |
| 77 | `lsn.montecarlo.control-variates` Control variates | Derive the optimal coefficient $b^\star = \frac{\operatorname{Cov}(Y, X)}{\operatorname{Var}(X)}$ and the variance factor $1 - \rho^2$, and use the discounted terminal price as a control for a call | The discounted payoff of the at-the-money 1-year call has correlation 0.924 with $e^{-rT}S_T$. How many fewer paths does the control need? (variance falls to $1 - 0.924^2 = 0.145$: about 7 times fewer) | `mc-variance` (control), `regression-lab`, `numeric-input` | Trap: any correlated control helps a lot (`mc.numerics.weak-control-helps`); $\rho = 0.5$ saves only 25%. Edge: $b$ estimated from the same paths adds a bias of order $\frac1N$; several controls are a regression | N, N, C, W |
| 78 | `lsn.montecarlo.asian-control` The geometric Asian as a control | Price an arithmetic-average Asian option with the geometric-average Asian, which has a closed form under GBM, as its control | With 12 monthly averaging dates the two discounted payoffs have correlation 0.9996. The plain standard error with $2 \times 10^5$ paths is 0.019. With the control? (0.00053: about 1,300 times fewer paths for the same error) | `mc-variance` (control), `pricer-compare` (mc), `numeric-input` | Trap: estimate the control's mean by simulation too (`mc.numerics.control-mean-simulated`); it must be known exactly. Edge: Kemna and Vorst (1990) | N, N, C, M |
| 79 | `lsn.montecarlo.importance-sampling` Importance sampling | Write $E_p[f] = E_q\left[f\frac pq\right]$, shift the mean of $Z$ to where the payoff lives, and check that the weights keep the variance finite | A call struck at 200 on a share at 100 ($\sigma = 20\%$, $r = 5\%$, $T = 1$; price 0.0048): plain Monte Carlo pays on 0.05% of paths. Shift $Z$'s mean by 3.32: share of paths that pay, and variance ratio? (50%; about 1,900) | `mc-variance` (importance), `dist-explorer`, `numeric-input` | Trap: any proposal works if the weights are right (`mc.numerics.any-proposal`); a proposal with lighter tails than $\lvert f\rvert p$ can make the variance infinite. Edge: the zero-variance proposal, proportional to $\lvert f\rvert p$, is unknowable but says where to put the mass | N, N, C, X |
| 80 | `lsn.montecarlo.stratification` Stratified and Latin hypercube sampling | Split the uniform's range into $N$ strata with one draw each, show the variance falls faster than $\frac1N$ for smooth integrands, and stratify many dimensions with Latin hypercube sampling | Estimate $E[U^2] = \frac13$ with 100 uniforms: standard error 0.030. With one draw in each of 100 strata? (0.00033: 90 times smaller) | `mc-variance` (stratified), `rng-lab` (point sets), `numeric-input` | Trap: stratify all $d$ dimensions on a grid (`mc.numerics.grid-strata`); that needs $m^d$ cells, lesson 30's curse. Edge: stratifying only a path's terminal value and filling in by the Brownian bridge captures most of the gain | N, C, N, S |
| 81 | `lsn.montecarlo.conditional` Conditional Monte Carlo and combining methods | Replace a payoff by its conditional expectation given part of the path, which by Eve's law (1.5) never increases the variance, and judge whether two reductions combine | A digital paying 1 if $S_T > K$: condition on $S_{T - \Delta t}$ and use the exact one-step probability. What happens to the variance and to the payoff's smoothness? (the variance falls; the conditional payoff is smooth, which also fixes its Greeks) | `mc-variance` (conditional), `cond-expectation`, `choice` | Trap: variance reductions multiply (`mc.numerics.reductions-multiply`). Edge: antithetic pairs on top of a good control often add little, because the control already removed the linear part antithetics remove | C, N, M, S |

Transfer screens: lesson 79 is how risk systems estimate tail losses and how deep out-of-the-money options are priced; lesson 78 is the exotic desk's classic trick.

Then the Level review and homework `set.montecarlo.variance-reduction`.

**Level MO4: Quasi-Monte Carlo** (`lvl.montecarlo.qmc`, 4 lessons)

| # | Lesson | Objective | Hook | Widgets | Trap and edge case | Quiz |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 82 | `lsn.montecarlo.low-discrepancy` Low-discrepancy sequences | Generate the van der Corput sequence by reversing binary digits, extend it to Halton and Sobol points, and measure how evenly they fill the cube | The 5th point of the base-2 van der Corput sequence? ($5 = 101_2$, reversed $0.101_2 = 0.625$) | `rng-lab` (qmc), `plot-2d`, `numeric-input` | Trap: quasi-random points are a kind of random number (`mc.numerics.qmc-random`). Edge: Halton points in high bases, such as dimensions 17 and 18, line up in stripes until $N$ is large | N, N, C, S |
| 83 | `lsn.montecarlo.koksma-hlawka` Why quasi-Monte Carlo converges faster | State the Koksma-Hlawka bound $\lvert\text{error}\rvert \le V(f)D_N^\star$ with $D_N^\star = O\left(\frac{(\log N)^d}N\right)$, and see error near $N^{-1}$ for smooth low-dimensional integrands | Estimate $\int_0^1x^2\,dx$ with 1,024 unscrambled Sobol points and with 1,024 random points. Errors? ($4.9 \times 10^{-4}$, against a typical $9.3 \times 10^{-3}$) | `convergence-plot`, `rng-lab` (qmc), `numeric-input` | Trap: quasi-Monte Carlo is better in every dimension (`mc.numerics.qmc-every-dimension`). Edge: one quasi-Monte Carlo run has no error bar, since nothing in it is random (lesson 84) | N, C, M, S |
| 84 | `lsn.montecarlo.randomised-qmc` Randomised quasi-Monte Carlo | Randomise a point set by scrambling or a random shift so each replicate is unbiased, and estimate the error from independent replicates | 16 scrambled Sobol sets of 1,024 points estimate $\int_0^1x^2\,dx$ with a standard error of $1.2 \times 10^{-7}$. Plain Monte Carlo with all 16,384 points? (about $2.3 \times 10^{-3}$) | `mc-variance` (rqmc), `rng-lab`, `numeric-input` | Trap: use any $N$ and keep Sobol's first point at 0 (`mc.numerics.sobol-any-n`); Sobol sets balance at powers of 2. Edge: SciPy's `qmc.Sobol` warns when $N$ is not a power of 2 | N, C, M, O |
| 85 | `lsn.montecarlo.effective-dimension` Effective dimension and the Brownian bridge | Show that a path of $m$ steps is an $m$-dimensional integral, and that building it by the Brownian bridge puts most of the variance in the first few coordinates, where Sobol points are best | An Asian option with 64 dates and 1,024 points per replicate: plain Monte Carlo has standard error 0.28; scrambled Sobol with paths built step by step, 0.044. With the Brownian bridge? (0.008) | `mc-variance` (rqmc), `path-simulator` (bridge), `numeric-input` | Trap: the gain comes from the points alone (`mc.numerics.points-alone`). Edge: the eigenvector construction (lesson 70) does at least as well for Asians (Caflisch, Morokoff and Owen 1997); for barrier options the bridge's advantage shrinks | N, C, W, M |

Transfer screens: lesson 85 is how banks price path-dependent books overnight with quasi-Monte Carlo.

Then the Level review and homework `set.montecarlo.qmc`.

**Level MO5: Sensitivities, early exercise and the exit** (`lvl.montecarlo.greeks`, 6 lessons)

| # | Lesson | Objective | Hook | Widgets | Trap and edge case | Quiz |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 86 | `lsn.montecarlo.bump-crn` Greeks by bumping with common random numbers | Estimate delta by central differences with the same random numbers in both runs, and choose the bump size against bias and noise | Delta of the at-the-money call (exact 0.6368) by a bump of 0.01 with $10^5$ paths: standard error with independent runs? (3.3). With common random numbers? (0.0018) | `mc-variance` (greeks), `greeks-lab`, `numeric-input` | Trap: independent seeds for the bumped runs (`mc.numerics.independent-bumps`). Edge: for a digital, a bump of 0.01 with common random numbers still has a standard error of 0.0030, 34 times the likelihood-ratio estimator's (lesson 88) | N, N, C, M |
| 87 | `lsn.montecarlo.pathwise` Pathwise derivatives | Differentiate the payoff along each path, $\frac\partial{\partial S_0}e^{-rT}(S_T - K)^+ = e^{-rT}\mathbb 1\{S_T > K\}\frac{S_T}{S_0}$, and state when the derivative and the expectation may be exchanged | The pathwise delta of the at-the-money call averages to what? ($N(d_1) = 0.6368$; standard error 0.0018 with $10^5$ paths) | `mc-variance` (greeks), `derivation-steps`, `numeric-input` | Trap: differentiate inside the expectation for any payoff (`mc.numerics.pathwise-always`); for a digital the pathwise derivative is zero almost everywhere. Edge: adjoint (reverse-mode) differentiation gives every pathwise Greek for about the cost of one price (Giles and Glasserman 2006) | X, N, C, W |
| 88 | `lsn.montecarlo.likelihood-ratio` Likelihood-ratio Greeks | Differentiate the density instead of the payoff, giving delta $= E\left[e^{-rT}f(S_T)\frac Z{S_0\sigma\sqrt T}\right]$, valid for discontinuous payoffs | The delta of an at-the-money digital (exact 0.01876) by likelihood ratio with $10^5$ paths: standard error? ($8.8 \times 10^{-5}$) | `mc-variance` (greeks), `dist-explorer`, `numeric-input` | Trap: likelihood-ratio estimates are always worse than pathwise ones (`mc.numerics.lr-worse`). Edge: they are noisier for smooth payoffs and the simple unbiased choice for jumps; mixing the two is standard | N, C, N, M |
| 89 | `lsn.montecarlo.longstaff-schwartz` American options by regression | Price an early-exercise option by Longstaff and Schwartz's method: regress discounted continuation values on basis functions over in-the-money paths, exercise when the payoff beats the fitted value, and price on fresh paths | An American put ($S = K = 100$, $\sigma = 20\%$, $r = 5\%$, $T = 1$) exercisable on 50 dates: $10^5$ paths and a quadratic basis give about 6.06 (standard error 0.02) on fresh paths. The 50-date value from a tree? (6.079; the fully American value is 6.090) | `mc-variance` (lsm), `regression-lab`, `binomial-tree`, `choice` | Trap: price on the same paths used to fit the rule (`mc.numerics.in-sample-rule`). Edge: on fresh paths any fitted rule is suboptimal, so the estimate is biased low; a dual method gives an upper bound (Andersen and Broadie 2004) | N, C, M, S |
| 90 | `lsn.montecarlo.error-vs-work` One option, four pricers: error against work | Plot error against work for the closed form, the tree, a Crank-Nicolson grid and Monte Carlo with a control variate on log-log axes, and read off the slopes | Predict each slope against work before plotting: the tree (work $\propto N^2$, error $\propto N^{-1}$), Crank-Nicolson (work $\propto N^2$, error $\propto N^{-2}$) and Monte Carlo (work $\propto N$, error $\propto N^{-1/2}$). ($-\frac12$, $-1$ and $-\frac12$) | `pricer-compare` (work), `convergence-plot`, `numeric-input` | Trap: read each method's order against its own $N$ and compare them (`mc.numerics.error-vs-n`). Edge: the exit states orders in each method's own parameter ($O(n^{-1})$, $O(\Delta t^2 + \Delta S^2)$, $O(N^{-1/2})$); against work the tree and Monte Carlo share a slope in one dimension, and Monte Carlo wins from about 4 dimensions | N, N, P, C |
| 91 | `lsn.montecarlo.choosing-method` Choosing a numerical method | Choose a pricer by dimension, payoff smoothness, early exercise and the Greeks needed, and use two independent pricers as the strongest test of both | Five products: a vanilla European, an American put, a 5-asset basket, an Asian with 252 dates, a daily-monitored barrier. Match each to a method (closed form or quadrature; grid or tree; Monte Carlo; quasi-Monte Carlo with the bridge; grid with the barrier on a node) | `card-sort`, `choice` | Trap: one pricer for everything (`mc.numerics.one-pricer`). Edge: when two independent pricers agree within their stated errors, both are probably right; when they disagree, the difference is information | S, C, M, S |

Transfer screens: lessons 90 and 91 rehearse the exit (section 4.2) and hand over to 2.3 level OA5, which applies them to real option chains.

Then the Level review and homework `set.montecarlo.greeks`. The Numerics Lab (`set.montecarlo.lab`, 7 drills, section 3.3) runs beside course MO.

### Course LP: Linear Programming and Duality (`crs.linprog`, 3 levels, 17 lessons)

1.3 optimised along constraints with Lagrange multipliers and read the multiplier as a price; 0.2 shaded inequality regions. This course turns both into linear programming: formulation and geometry, the simplex method as a walk between vertices, integer constraints, and duality (bounds, certificates, complementary slackness, shadow prices with their ranges, Farkas' lemma), ending in the theorem that no arbitrage means positive state prices. One running example, a risk-budgeted allocation, carries the course so that every new idea has a picture the learner has already drawn. Spine: Vanderbei chapters 1 to 7 (titles of chapters 4, 5 and 7 [searched], the rest [recalled]); Boyd and Vandenberghe §4.3 and §5.8; finance models from Cornuéjols, Peña and Tütüncü.

**Level LP1: Linear programmes and their geometry** (`lvl.linprog.geometry`, 6 lessons)

| # | Lesson | Objective | Hook | Widgets | Trap and edge case | Quiz |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | `lsn.linprog.modelling` From a decision to a linear programme | Write a decision as variables, a linear objective and linear constraints, and solve a two-variable programme by drawing it | Allocate £100m between two strategies with expected returns 8% and 12%, a risk budget $0.1x + 0.3y \le 18$ and a liquidity limit $y \le 50$. Best allocation? ($x = 60$, $y = 40$: £9.6m a year) | `feasible-region` (new: graphical mode), `drag-point`, `numeric-input` | Trap: fill the highest-return strategy first (`mc.optim.greedy-allocation`): $y = 50$ breaks no rule but leaves the risk budget room for only $x = 30$, £8.4m. Edge: a constraint that never binds ($y \le 50$ here) could be deleted without changing the answer, but you only know that afterwards | N, C, A, P |
| 2 | `lsn.linprog.feasible-regions` Feasible regions, level lines and vertices | Draw a feasible region as an intersection of half-planes, slide the objective's level line to the optimum, and recognise infeasible, unbounded and tied problems | Change the objective to $0.1x + 0.3y$, the risk used. Which allocations maximise it? (every point on the edge from $(30, 50)$ to $(60, 40)$: value 18) | `feasible-region` (level-line), `slider`, `choice` | Trap: a linear programme has exactly one optimal point (`mc.optim.unique-optimum`). Edge: an objective parallel to a binding constraint makes a whole edge optimal; a solver returns one of its vertices | C, R, N, M |
| 3 | `lsn.linprog.standard-form` Standard form, slacks and basic solutions | Convert inequalities to equalities with slack variables, and identify vertices with basic feasible solutions: choose $m$ basic variables and set the rest to zero | The allocation problem has 5 variables in standard form ($x$, $y$ and three slacks) and 3 equations. How many ways to choose 3 basic variables, and how many give feasible vertices? (10 choices; one is singular, leaving 9 basic solutions, 5 of them feasible: the 5 corners) | `feasible-region` (basis), `matrix`, `numeric-input` | Trap: every crossing of two constraint lines is a vertex (`mc.optim.every-crossing-vertex`); four of them are infeasible. Edge: when more than $n$ constraints pass through one corner the vertex is degenerate and several bases name it | N, N, C, S |
| 4 | `lsn.linprog.vertex-theorem` Why an optimum sits at a vertex | Prove that a linear objective on a bounded polyhedron attains its maximum at a vertex, from "every point is a convex combination of the vertices" | A linear objective on a triangle: can its maximum be strictly inside? (no: from an inside point, moving along the objective's gradient improves it) | `feasible-region` (convex-combination), `derivation-steps`, `choice` | Trap: the optimum is unique (`mc.optim.unique-optimum`). Edge: an unbounded polyhedron also needs its directions; the theorem becomes "at a vertex, or unbounded" | O, C, W, M |
| 5 | `lsn.linprog.finance-lps` Linear programmes in finance | Formulate cash-flow matching, arbitrage detection and $L_1$ index tracking as linear programmes, linearising absolute values with two inequalities | Bond A pays 1 at date 1 (price 0.95), bond B pays 1 at date 2 (price 0.90), bond C pays 1 at both dates (price 1.80). Is there a portfolio that pays you today and never costs you later? (yes: buy C, sell A and B, receive 0.05 now; the later payments cancel) | `feasible-region`, `matrix`, `choice` | Trap: $\lvert x\rvert$ cannot appear in a linear programme (`mc.optim.abs-not-linear`); minimising $\sum\lvert e_i\rvert$ is one, with $u_i \ge e_i$ and $u_i \ge -e_i$. Edge: the largest deviation, $\min\max_i\lvert e_i\rvert$, is a linear programme too | S, C, M, X |
| 6 | `lsn.linprog.solvers` Solving linear programmes in code | Read a solver's status, solution and dual values (`scipy.optimize.linprog` with HiGHS), and interpret its tolerances | An interior-point run reports status 0 and $x = (59.99999999, 40.00000001)$. Wrong? (no: its tolerances are about $10^{-9}$; round only for reporting, or ask for a simplex crossover) | `predict-output`, `value-table`, `choice` | Trap: "optimal" means the model is right (`mc.optim.solver-validates-model`). Edge: a badly scaled model, with pounds and millions in one row, can make a solver report a feasible problem infeasible; rescale first | C, S, O, N |

Transfer screens: lesson 5 is how a bond desk tests quotes for arbitrage; lesson 1 is a risk-budgeted allocation, the shape of every capital decision.

Then the Level review and homework `set.linprog.geometry`.

**Level LP2: The simplex method** (`lvl.linprog.simplex`, 5 lessons)

| # | Lesson | Objective | Hook | Widgets | Trap and edge case | Quiz |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 7 | `lsn.linprog.simplex-walk` The simplex method: walking to better vertices | Explain the simplex method as moving along an edge to an adjacent vertex with a better objective until no edge improves, and trace it on the allocation problem | Start at $(0, 0)$ and let the variable with the largest objective coefficient enter. Which vertices do you visit? ($(0, 0) \to (0, 50) \to (30, 50) \to (60, 40)$) | `feasible-region` (simplex), `stepper`, `choice` | Trap: the steepest edge in the picture is the one the method takes (`mc.optim.picture-steepest`); Dantzig's rule compares reduced costs per unit of the entering variable, which depends on scaling. Edge: letting $x$ enter first visits $(100, 0)$ and reaches $(60, 40)$ in two moves | O, C, N, M |
| 8 | `lsn.linprog.tableau` One pivot in a tableau | Perform a pivot: choose the entering variable by its reduced cost, the leaving variable by the minimum ratio test, and update the tableau by row operations | In the starting tableau $y$ enters. Ratios $\frac{100}1$, $\frac{18}{0.3}$ and $\frac{50}1$: which variable leaves? (the liquidity slack, ratio 50) | `matrix` (row gestures), `elimination-lab`, `numeric-input` | Trap: include negative or zero entries in the ratio test (`mc.optim.ratio-negative`). Edge: a tie in the ratio test leaves a basic variable at zero: degeneracy | A, N, C, O |
| 9 | `lsn.linprog.phase-one` Degeneracy, cycling and a first vertex | Find a starting vertex by Phase I (minimising artificial variables), detect infeasibility when Phase I's optimum is positive, and prevent cycling with Bland's rule | Add a minimum deployment rule $x + y \ge 10$. Is $(0, 0)$ still a starting vertex? (no: it is infeasible; Phase I finds a feasible vertex first) | `feasible-region` (phase-one), `stepper`, `choice` | Trap: the origin is always feasible (`mc.optim.origin-feasible`). Edge: with Bland's rule (the lowest-index candidate enters and leaves) the simplex method never cycles | C, N, R, O |
| 10 | `lsn.linprog.complexity` How fast is the simplex method? | State that the simplex method can visit every vertex (Klee and Minty 1972) yet usually takes a small multiple of $m$ pivots, and that interior-point methods run in polynomial time | On the 3-dimensional Klee-Minty cube, Dantzig's rule visits how many of the 8 vertices? (all 8: 7 pivots) | `feasible-region` (klee-minty), `plot-3d`, `numeric-input` | Trap: an exponential worst case means slow in practice (`mc.optim.worst-case-typical`). Edge: smoothed analysis (Spielman and Teng 2004) explains why: small random perturbations make the expected number of pivots polynomial | N, C, M, S |
| 11 | `lsn.linprog.integer` Integer constraints and branch and bound | Show that rounding an LP solution can be infeasible or poor, and solve a small integer programme by branch and bound with the LP relaxation as a bound | Maximise $y$ subject to $-x + y \le 1$, $3x + 2y \le 12$, $2x + 3y \le 12$, with $x, y \ge 0$ whole numbers. The LP optimum is $(1.8, 2.8)$. Round to $(2, 3)$? (infeasible). The integer optimum? ($y = 2$, at $(1, 2)$ or $(2, 2)$) | `feasible-region` (lattice), `stepper`, `numeric-input` | Trap: round the LP solution (`mc.optim.round-lp`). Edge: the relaxation's 2.8 bounds the integer optimum from above; lot sizes and "at most 20 names" make portfolio problems integer | N, W, C, P |

Transfer screens: lesson 11 is why a minimum lot size makes rebalancing hard; lesson 10 is why practitioners trust LP solvers on millions of variables.

Then the Level review and homework `set.linprog.simplex`.

**Level LP3: Duality** (`lvl.linprog.duality`, 6 lessons)

| # | Lesson | Objective | Hook | Widgets | Trap and edge case | Quiz |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 12 | `lsn.linprog.dual-as-bound` The dual as the best bound | Combine constraints with non-negative multipliers to bound the objective, and find the best such bound as a new linear programme, the dual | Multiply the capital constraint by 0.06 and the risk constraint by 0.2 and add them. What bound on $0.08x + 0.12y$ results? ($0.06 \times 100 + 0.2 \times 18 = 9.6$, the optimum itself) | `dual-pair` (new: bound mode), `slider`, `numeric-input` | Trap: multipliers can have any sign (`mc.optim.multiplier-sign`); on the $\le$ constraints of a maximisation they must be non-negative, or the inequality flips. Edge: the dual of the dual is the primal | N, N, C, X |
| 13 | `lsn.linprog.weak-strong` Weak and strong duality | Prove weak duality in one line ($\mathbf c^\top\mathbf x \le \mathbf y^\top A\mathbf x \le \mathbf y^\top\mathbf b$), state strong duality, and use a primal-dual pair as a certificate of optimality | A colleague claims an allocation earning £9.7m. Refute it without searching. (the multipliers $(0.06, 0.2, 0)$ prove that no feasible allocation exceeds £9.6m) | `dual-pair` (gap), `derivation-steps`, `choice` | Trap: proving optimality means checking every vertex (`mc.optim.search-to-prove`). Edge: if the primal is unbounded the dual is infeasible; both can be infeasible | W, C, O, M |
| 14 | `lsn.linprog.complementary-slackness` Complementary slackness | Use "a positive multiplier means a binding constraint, and a slack constraint has a zero multiplier" to read the dual from the primal solution | At $(60, 40)$ the liquidity limit $y \le 50$ is slack. Its multiplier? (0). Solve for the other two from the binding constraints. ($0.06$ and $0.2$) | `dual-pair` (slackness), `matrix`, `numeric-input` | Trap: every constraint has a positive price (`mc.optim.every-constraint-priced`). Edge: at a degenerate vertex the dual is not unique (a risk budget of exactly 20, lesson 15) | N, A, C, S |
| 15 | `lsn.linprog.shadow-prices` Shadow prices and their ranges | Read a dual value as the change in the optimum per unit change in a constraint's right-hand side, and find the range over which it holds | Raise the risk budget from 18 to 19. New optimum? (£9.8m: up by the multiplier 0.2). Over which budgets does 0.2 hold? (from 10, where the capital constraint stops mattering, to 20, where the liquidity limit starts to bind) | `dual-pair` (sensitivity), `feasible-region`, `numeric-input` | Trap: a shadow price holds for any size of change (`mc.optim.shadow-unbounded`). Edge: at a budget of exactly 20 the vertex is degenerate and the risk multiplier jumps from 0.2 to 0; 1.3 lesson 126 met the multiplier as a price in smooth problems | N, I, C, N |
| 16 | `lsn.linprog.farkas` Farkas' lemma | State Farkas' lemma (exactly one of $\{A\mathbf x = \mathbf b, \mathbf x \ge \mathbf 0\}$ and $\{A^\top\mathbf y \ge \mathbf 0, \mathbf b^\top\mathbf y < 0\}$ has a solution) and draw it as a hyperplane separating $\mathbf b$ from the cone of $A$'s columns | Columns $(1, 0)$ and $(1, 1)$, and $\mathbf b = (0, 1)$. Is $\mathbf b$ a non-negative combination of the columns? (no). Find a certificate $\mathbf y$. (for example $(1, -1)$: $A^\top\mathbf y = (1, 0) \ge \mathbf 0$ and $\mathbf b^\top\mathbf y = -1 < 0$) | `dual-pair` (cone), `drag-point`, `choice` | Trap: when there is no solution there is no short proof of it (`mc.optim.no-certificate`). Edge: Farkas' lemma is LP duality in disguise, and the next lesson's no-arbitrage theorem is Farkas' lemma in finance | C, P, A, W |
| 17 | `lsn.linprog.state-prices` No arbitrage is duality | Prove for a one-period market that there is no arbitrage exactly when positive state prices $\boldsymbol\psi$ with $D^\top\boldsymbol\psi = \mathbf p$ exist, and build the arbitrage when they do not | A bond pays 1 in both states (price 0.95), a share pays 120 or 90 (price 100), a call struck at 100 pays 20 or 0 (price 10). State prices from the bond and share, and the call's implied price? ($\boldsymbol\psi = (0.4833, 0.4667)$; 9.67, so sell the call and buy $\frac23$ of a share with 57 borrowed, locking in 0.33) | `dual-pair` (state-prices), `matrix`, `numeric-input` | Trap: state prices are real-world probabilities (`mc.optim.state-price-probability`); divided by the bond price they are risk-neutral probabilities. Edge: with more states than assets, state prices are not unique (an incomplete market); 1.4 lesson 18 solved for them, this lesson proves when they exist | A, N, C, W |

Transfer screens: lesson 15 is how a risk manager prices relaxing a limit; lesson 17 is the one-period fundamental theorem of asset pricing (3.2).

Then the Level review and homework `set.linprog.duality`.

### Course CX: Convexity and Optimality (`crs.convex`, 3 levels, 18 lessons)

Convexity is what makes an optimisation problem solvable and its solution certifiable. This course teaches it in the order a practitioner needs: recognise convex sets (especially portfolio constraints), prove functions convex without fuss, then state optimality through the Lagrangian, duality and the KKT conditions, with every multiplier read as a price. Spine: Boyd and Vandenberghe chapters 2 to 5 [check section numbers]; Stanford EE364A lectures as the parallel.

**Level CX1: Convex sets** (`lvl.convex.sets`, 5 lessons)

| # | Lesson | Objective | Hook | Widgets | Trap and edge case | Quiz |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 18 | `lsn.convex.convex-sets` Convex sets | Test convexity by segments, and classify the standard sets (half-spaces, polyhedra, balls, ellipsoids, the simplex) and common portfolio constraints | Which sets of portfolios are convex: fully invested and long-only; gross exposure $\sum\lvert w_i\rvert \le 2$; at most 10 non-zero positions; every position either 0 or at least 1%? (the first two) | `convex-set` (new: segment test), `card-sort`, `choice` | Trap: a set described by tidy formulas is convex (`mc.optim.formula-implies-convex`). Edge: the cardinality and minimum-holding constraints are the non-convex ones every production optimiser struggles with | M, S, C, W |
| 19 | `lsn.convex.operations` Operations that preserve convexity | Show that intersections, affine images and preimages, and sums of convex sets are convex, and use them to prove a feasible set convex | Is the set of long-only portfolios with volatility at most 15% and beta between 0.9 and 1.1 convex? (yes: an ellipsoid, a slab and the simplex intersected) | `convex-set` (intersection), `quadratic-form`, `choice` | Trap: a union of convex sets is convex (`mc.optim.union-convex`). Edge: the portfolios with volatility exactly 15% do not form a convex set, though those with at most 15% do | C, R, W, S |
| 20 | `lsn.convex.psd-cone` The positive semidefinite cone | Prove that positive semidefinite matrices form a convex cone and valid correlation matrices a convex set, so the nearest valid correlation matrix is unique | The average of two valid correlation matrices: valid? (yes: $\mathbf v^\top\left(\frac12A + \frac12B\right)\mathbf v \ge 0$). The average of two invalid ones? (it may or may not be valid) | `quadratic-form`, `matrix`, `choice` | Trap: the matrices with a negative eigenvalue form a convex set too (`mc.optim.complement-convex`). Edge: 2.0a lesson 37's projection is onto this convex set, which is why Higham's method converges to one answer | C, W, N, A |
| 21 | `lsn.convex.separation` Separating and supporting hyperplanes | State the separating hyperplane theorem, find a separating line for two point clouds, and see a supporting hyperplane at a boundary point | Two clouds of points: drag a line that separates them. When is it impossible? (when their convex hulls overlap) | `convex-set` (separation), `classifier-plane`, `drag-point` | Trap: two disjoint sets can always be separated by a line (`mc.optim.disjoint-separable`); both must be convex. Edge: the separating line with the widest gap is the support vector machine of lesson 56 | P, C, O, S |
| 22 | `lsn.convex.projection` Projection onto convex sets | Project onto a box (clip), a ball (rescale) and the simplex (shift and clip), and use the uniqueness of the projection | Project the weights $(0.5, 0.4, 0.3, -0.2)$ onto the long-only, fully invested simplex. Result? ($(0.4333, 0.3333, 0.2333, 0)$: subtract $\tau = \frac1{15}$ and clip at 0) | `convex-set` (projection), `value-table`, `numeric-input` | Trap: clip negatives to 0 and renormalise (`mc.optim.clip-renormalise`); that gives $(0.4167, 0.3333, 0.25, 0)$, not the nearest point. Edge: projected gradient descent (lesson 42) is a gradient step followed by this projection | A, N, C, X |

Transfer screens: lesson 18 explains why production portfolio optimisers are mixed-integer; lesson 22 is how a weight vector from a signal is made fully invested and long-only.

Then the Level review and homework `set.convex.sets`.

**Level CX2: Convex functions** (`lvl.convex.functions`, 6 lessons)

| # | Lesson | Objective | Hook | Widgets | Trap and edge case | Quiz |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 23 | `lsn.convex.functions-nd` Convex functions in several variables | Define convexity by Jensen's inequality and the epigraph, test it on lines, and use $f(\mathbf y) \ge f(\mathbf x) + \nabla f(\mathbf x)^\top(\mathbf y - \mathbf x)$ to show every local minimum is global | Is $f(x, y) = x^2 + xy + y^2$ convex? Restrict it to the line through $(1, -1)$ and $(-1, 1)$. (along $(t, -t)$, $f = t^2$; on every line it is a convex parabola) | `convexity-chord` (surface [mode to add]), `plot-3d`, `choice` | Trap: convex in each variable separately means convex (`mc.optim.separate-convexity`); $xy$ is linear in each variable and not convex. Edge: 1.3 lesson 50 proved the one-variable tests; restricting to lines reduces every case to them | C, N, X, W |
| 24 | `lsn.convex.hessian-test` The second-order test | Prove convexity from $\nabla^2f \succeq 0$ on a convex domain, using 1.4 level Z1's tests | Is $f(x, y) = \frac{x^2}y$ convex on $y > 0$? (yes: $\nabla^2f = \frac2{y^3}\begin{pmatrix}y^2 & -xy\\-xy & x^2\end{pmatrix}$, positive semidefinite) | `quadratic-form`, `matrix`, `expression-input` | Trap: a positive Hessian determinant means convex (`mc.optim.det-positive`); negative definite $2 \times 2$ matrices have positive determinants too. Edge: the domain must be convex: $\frac1x$ has a positive second derivative on $x \ne 0$ but is not convex there | C, A, X, W |
| 25 | `lsn.convex.calculus` Building convex functions | Prove convexity without derivatives from non-negative sums, affine composition, pointwise maxima and the composition rules | Prove that $f(\mathbf w) = \max_i\left(\mathbf a_i^\top\mathbf w + b_i\right)$ is convex, and hence the hinge loss $\max(0, 1 - y\,\mathbf w^\top\mathbf x)$ | `derivation-steps`, `convexity-chord`, `card-sort` | Trap: a composition of convex functions is convex (`mc.optim.composition-convex`); $e^{-x^2}$ composes the convex $e^{-u}$ with the convex $x^2$ and is not convex. Edge: $\sqrt{\mathbf w^\top\Sigma\mathbf w} = \lVert L^\top\mathbf w\rVert_2$ is a norm of a linear map, hence convex, though $\sqrt{\cdot}$ is concave | W, S, C, M |
| 26 | `lsn.convex.log-sum-exp` Log-sum-exp, softmax and the log-determinant | Prove $\log\sum_ie^{x_i}$ convex from its Hessian $\operatorname{diag}(\mathbf p) - \mathbf p\mathbf p^\top \succeq 0$ with $\mathbf p = \operatorname{softmax}(\mathbf x)$, and use the convexity of $-\log\det X$ and of the logistic loss | The Hessian of $\log(e^x + e^y)$ at $(0, 0)$? ($\begin{pmatrix}\frac14 & -\frac14\\-\frac14 & \frac14\end{pmatrix}$: positive semidefinite and singular along $(1, 1)$) | `quadratic-form`, `matrix`, `derivation-steps` | Trap: positive semidefinite means positive definite (`mc.optim.psd-is-pd`); log-sum-exp is linear along $(1, 1, \dots)$, because adding $c$ to every logit adds $c$ to the result. Edge: 2.0a lesson 10 evaluated the same function stably; 2.1 lesson 21 differentiates it | A, C, N, W |
| 27 | `lsn.convex.strong-convexity` Strong convexity, smoothness and the condition number | Define $\mu$-strong convexity and $L$-smoothness by $\mu I \preceq \nabla^2f \preceq LI$, compute $\kappa = \frac L\mu$, and state the quadratic bounds they give | For $f = \frac12(x^2 + 100y^2)$: $\mu$, $L$ and $\kappa$? (1, 100, 100) | `quadratic-form`, `descent-lab`, `numeric-input` | Trap: strictly convex means strongly convex (`mc.optim.strict-is-strong`); $x^4$ is strictly convex with zero curvature at 0. Edge: the logistic loss is not strongly convex far from the data; a ridge term $\frac\lambda2\lVert\mathbf w\rVert^2$ makes it $\lambda$-strongly convex | N, C, X, S |
| 28 | `lsn.convex.beyond-convexity` Recognising non-convex problems, and rescuing some | Recognise quasi-convex and non-convex objectives, and turn maximising a Sharpe ratio into a convex problem by rescaling | Maximise $\frac{\boldsymbol\mu^\top\mathbf w}{\sqrt{\mathbf w^\top\Sigma\mathbf w}}$ over fully invested portfolios: convex? (no; but minimising $\mathbf w^\top\Sigma\mathbf w$ subject to $\boldsymbol\mu^\top\mathbf w = 1$ and rescaling to a unit budget gives the same portfolio when the best Sharpe ratio is positive) | `frontier-lab` (new: tangency), `derivation-steps`, `choice` | Trap: a ratio of a linear and a convex function can be maximised by gradient ascent like any other objective (`mc.optim.ratio-easy`). Edge: cardinality limits are rescued only approximately, by an $L_1$ penalty or a mixed-integer solver | C, W, M, S |

Transfer screens: lesson 28 computes the tangency portfolio; lesson 26 is why logistic regression and softmax classifiers have a single optimum.

Then the Level review and homework `set.convex.functions`.

**Level CX3: Duality and the KKT conditions** (`lvl.convex.kkt`, 7 lessons)

| # | Lesson | Objective | Hook | Widgets | Trap and edge case | Quiz |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 29 | `lsn.convex.problems` Convex optimisation problems | Write a problem in standard form, decide whether it is convex, and place it in the hierarchy LP, QP, SOCP, SDP | Four portfolio problems: minimum variance with a return target; maximum return with volatility at most 15%; maximum return with volatility exactly 15%; minimum variance with at most 20 names. Which are convex? (the first two) | `card-sort`, `convex-set`, `choice` | Trap: a convex objective makes a convex problem (`mc.optim.objective-only`); the feasible set must be convex too. Edge: "volatility at most 15%" is a second-order cone constraint, $\lVert L^\top\mathbf w\rVert_2 \le 0.15$ | S, C, M, W |
| 30 | `lsn.convex.lagrangian` The Lagrangian and the dual function | Form $L(\mathbf x, \boldsymbol\lambda, \boldsymbol\nu)$, compute the dual function $g = \inf_{\mathbf x}L$, prove $g \le p^\star$ for $\boldsymbol\lambda \ge \mathbf 0$, and show $g$ is concave | Minimise $x^2$ subject to $x \ge 1$. The dual function? ($g(\lambda) = \lambda - \frac{\lambda^2}4$). Its maximum? (1, at $\lambda = 2$, equal to $p^\star = 1$) | `dual-pair` (nonlinear), `plot-2d`, `expression-input` | Trap: the dual function is convex because the primal is (`mc.optim.dual-convex`); it is always concave, even for a non-convex primal. Edge: LP duality (lesson 13) is this construction with a linear objective | X, N, C, W |
| 31 | `lsn.convex.slater` Strong duality and Slater's condition | State that a convex problem with a strictly feasible point has zero duality gap, and recognise when strong duality fails | Minimise $e^{-x}$ subject to $\frac{x^2}y \le 0$ on $y > 0$. Optimal value? (1: feasibility forces $x = 0$). Dual optimal value? (0: no point is strictly feasible; after Boyd and Vandenberghe's exercise 5.21 [recalled]) | `dual-pair` (gap), `plot-2d`, `numeric-input` | Trap: convexity alone guarantees a zero gap (`mc.optim.convex-zero-gap`). Edge: non-convex problems usually have a gap; a cardinality-constrained portfolio's dual gives only a bound | N, N, C, S |
| 32 | `lsn.convex.kkt` The KKT conditions | State stationarity, primal and dual feasibility and complementary slackness, read them as "$-\nabla f$ lies in the cone of the active constraints' gradients", and solve a small problem with them | Minimise $(x - 2)^2 + (y - 1)^2$ subject to $x + y \le 2$. Solution and multiplier? ($(1.5, 0.5)$, $\lambda = 1$) | `lagrange-explorer` (kkt [mode to add]), `derivation-steps`, `numeric-input` | Trap: set the gradient to zero (`mc.optim.zero-gradient-constrained`); the unconstrained minimum $(2, 1)$ is infeasible. Edge: for a convex problem with Slater's condition the KKT conditions are necessary and sufficient; without convexity they are only necessary | A, N, C, W |
| 33 | `lsn.convex.long-only-kkt` KKT for a long-only portfolio | Find the long-only minimum-variance portfolio by guessing the active set and checking the KKT conditions, and interpret the multiplier on a zero weight | Three assets with volatilities 10%, 20% and 30%, asset 3 being 0.9 correlated with asset 1: the unconstrained minimum-variance portfolio holds $-36\%$ of asset 3. Long-only? ($(0.895, 0.105, 0)$, volatility 9.8% against 6.0%) | `lagrange-explorer` (kkt), `frontier-lab`, `numeric-input` | Trap: set the negative weight to zero and renormalise the rest (`mc.optim.clip-weights`), which gives $(0.958, 0.042, 0)$. Edge: the multiplier on $w_3 \ge 0$, 0.033, measures how much asset 3 "wants" to be shorted: its marginal variance exceeds the held assets' by that amount | A, N, C, M |
| 34 | `lsn.convex.multipliers-as-prices` Multipliers as prices | Read $\lambda^\star = -\frac{\partial p^\star}{\partial u}$ for a perturbed constraint, and use it to price a relaxation in a nonlinear problem | On a minimum-variance frontier the variance at an 8% return target is 0.01931 and the target's multiplier is 0.80 per unit of return. Predict the variance at 8.1%. (0.02011; the true value is 0.02013) | `frontier-lab`, `dual-pair` (sensitivity), `numeric-input` | Trap: the multiplier predicts large changes too (`mc.optim.shadow-unbounded`). Edge: the prediction is a tangent; the frontier curves, so the error grows with the square of the change | N, N, C, S |
| 35 | `lsn.convex.modelling-cvxpy` Modelling with cvxpy | Write portfolio and regression problems in cvxpy under the disciplined convex programming rules, and read each constraint's `dual_value` | Which does cvxpy accept as convex: `quad_form(w, S)`, `sqrt(quad_form(w, S))`, `norm(L.T @ w, 2)`, `power(quad_form(w, S), 0.5)`? (the first and the third) | `card-sort`, `predict-output`, `choice` | Trap: cvxpy rejects only non-convex expressions (`mc.optim.dcp-rejects-nonconvex`); it also rejects convex ones it cannot prove. Edge: write $\sqrt{\mathbf w^\top\Sigma\mathbf w}$ as $\lVert L^\top\mathbf w\rVert_2$ with $\Sigma = LL^\top$ (Diamond and Boyd 2016) | S, C, M, S |

Transfer screens: lesson 33 is the path's first constrained portfolio; lesson 35 is the tool of the 2.0b exit's portfolio task.

Then the Level review and homework `set.convex.kkt`.

### Course DA: Descent Algorithms (`crs.descent`, 2 levels, 12 lessons)

1.3 and 1.4 ran gradient descent on quadratics and found that the eigenvalues set its speed. This course proves the rates, adds the methods practitioners use (line search, Newton, BFGS, proximal steps, projection, acceleration, SGD, barriers), and ends with how to choose one and when to stop. Spine: Boyd and Vandenberghe chapter 9 and §11.1 to 11.3 [check]; Nocedal and Wright chapters 3 and 6; Bottou, Curtis and Nocedal (2018) for stochastic methods.

**Level DA1: Unconstrained methods and their rates** (`lvl.descent.rates`, 6 lessons)

| # | Lesson | Objective | Hook | Widgets | Trap and edge case | Quiz |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 36 | `lsn.descent.line-search` Choosing steps by line search | Apply exact line search on a quadratic and Armijo backtracking (sufficient decrease, halving), and state the Wolfe conditions | Backtracking from $\eta = 1$ with $c = 10^{-4}$ on $f(x) = x^4$ at $x = 1$: which step is accepted? ($\eta = 0.25$, which lands exactly on 0) | `descent-lab` (line-search [mode to add]), `plot-2d`, `numeric-input` | Trap: one good fixed step works everywhere (`mc.optim.fixed-step-universal`). Edge: at $\eta = 0.5$ the trial point $x = -1$ has the same value as $x = 1$, so the Armijo test refuses it | N, C, O, X |
| 37 | `lsn.descent.gd-rates` Convergence rates of gradient descent | Prove linear convergence at rate $1 - \frac\mu L$ for a strongly convex $L$-smooth quadratic with step $\frac1L$, and state the sublinear $O\left(\frac1k\right)$ rate in function value for convex functions | $\kappa = 100$ with step $\frac1L$: iterations to shrink the error a millionfold? (about 1,375). On $f = x^4$, which is not strongly convex? (sublinear: the distance to 0 falls like $k^{-1/2}$) | `descent-lab`, `convergence-plot`, `numeric-input` | Trap: gradient descent converges linearly on every convex function (`mc.optim.linear-always`). Edge: 1.4 lesson 96 found the factor $1 - \eta\lambda_i$ on a quadratic; 2.1 lesson 17 uses it for losses | N, N, C, W |
| 38 | `lsn.descent.newton-min` Newton's method for minimisation | Derive the Newton step $-\nabla^2f^{-1}\nabla f$ from the quadratic model, show quadratic convergence near a minimiser and invariance to linear changes of variable, and damp it with a line search | On $f = \frac12(x^2 + 100y^2)$ gradient descent needs about 1,375 iterations. Newton's method? (1: the quadratic model is exact) | `descent-lab` (newton), `quadratic-form`, `choice` | Trap: Newton's method is always better (`mc.optim.newton-better`); with $10^6$ parameters the Hessian has $10^{12}$ entries. Edge: on a non-convex function a Newton step can head for a saddle or a maximum, where the Hessian is indefinite; on $\log\cosh x$ it diverges from $\lvert x_0\rvert > 1.089$ (homework DA1) | N, C, X, W |
| 39 | `lsn.descent.measure-rates` Linear against quadratic convergence, measured | Tell linear from quadratic convergence from an error sequence (constant $\frac{e_{k+1}}{e_k}$ against constant $\frac{e_{k+1}}{e_k^2}$) and on semilog plots | Logistic regression on 200 points. Newton's errors: 0.50, 0.089, 0.0032, $4.3 \times 10^{-6}$, $7.8 \times 10^{-12}$. Gradient descent with step $\frac1L$ shrinks its error by 0.70 a step. How many gradient steps match Newton's fifth? (72) | `convergence-plot` (semilog), `descent-lab`, `numeric-input` | Trap: a straight line on a log-log plot is linear convergence (`mc.optim.loglog-linear`); linear convergence is straight on a semilog plot. Edge: the 2.0b exit asks for this comparison on a problem of the learner's own | N, P, C, S |
| 40 | `lsn.descent.quasi-newton` Quasi-Newton methods: BFGS | Build curvature from gradients through the secant condition $B_{k+1}\mathbf s_k = \mathbf y_k$, state BFGS's superlinear convergence, and use L-BFGS when $n$ is large | On the same 2-D quadratic, BFGS with exact line search reaches the minimum in how many steps? (2, the dimension). On Rosenbrock's valley from $(-1.2, 1)$, gradient descent with backtracking takes 13,756 iterations and damped Newton 21. BFGS? (33) | `descent-lab` (bfgs [mode to add]), `convergence-plot`, `numeric-input` | Trap: quasi-Newton methods need the Hessian (`mc.optim.quasi-needs-hessian`). Edge: L-BFGS keeps about 10 vector pairs, so its memory is $O(n)$; it is SciPy's choice for bound-constrained problems | N, N, C, S |
| 41 | `lsn.descent.nonsmooth` Non-smooth objectives: subgradients and proximal steps | Explain why gradient descent with a fixed step oscillates on $\lvert x\rvert$, define a subgradient, and take a proximal step for an $L_1$ penalty (soft-thresholding) | Gradient descent on $f(x) = \lvert x\rvert$ from 1 with step 0.3: iterates? (0.7, 0.4, 0.1, $-0.2$, 0.1, $-0.2$, ...: it never settles) | `descent-lab` (proximal [mode to add]), `loss-lab`, `numeric-input` | Trap: the gradient of $\lvert x\rvert$ at 0 is 0, so descent stops there (`mc.optim.kink-gradient-zero`). Edge: the proximal step $\operatorname{sign}(z)\max(\lvert z\rvert - \eta\lambda, 0)$ lands exactly on 0, which is how the lasso zeroes coefficients (lesson 54; 2.1 lesson 51) | N, C, X, P |

Transfer screens: lesson 39 is the 2.0b exit's convergence task; lesson 40 is what `scipy.optimize.minimize` does by default for smooth unconstrained problems.

Then the Level review and homework `set.descent.rates`.

**Level DA2: Constraints, momentum and noise** (`lvl.descent.scale`, 6 lessons)

| # | Lesson | Objective | Hook | Widgets | Trap and edge case | Quiz |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 42 | `lsn.descent.projected-gradient` Projected gradient descent | Take a gradient step and project back onto a convex feasible set, and recognise that its fixed points are the KKT points | Minimum variance over the long-only simplex for lesson 33's three assets, from equal weights: where does projected gradient descent end? (at $(0.895, 0.105, 0)$, the KKT point) | `descent-lab` (projected [mode to add]), `convex-set` (projection), `numeric-input` | Trap: step, then clip the negative weights and renormalise (`mc.optim.clip-renormalise`). Edge: the projection onto the simplex costs only a sort (lesson 22), so projected gradient scales to thousands of assets | A, C, N, M |
| 43 | `lsn.descent.momentum` Momentum and acceleration | Write heavy-ball and Nesterov momentum, state the accelerated rate $\left(1 - \frac1{\sqrt\kappa}\right)^k$, and compare iteration counts with gradient descent | $\kappa = 10^4$: gradient descent needs about 138,000 iterations to shrink the error a millionfold. Nesterov's method? (about 1,375: $\sqrt\kappa$ replaces $\kappa$) | `descent-lab` (nesterov [mode to add], momentum), `convergence-plot`, `numeric-input` | Trap: momentum always helps (`mc.optim.momentum-always`); badly tuned it overshoots, and its guarantees are for convex problems. Edge: conjugate gradients (2.0a lesson 46) reach the $\sqrt\kappa$ rate on quadratics with nothing to tune; 2.1 lesson 112 races Adam against both | N, N, C, M |
| 44 | `lsn.descent.sgd` Stochastic gradient descent | Show that constant-step SGD hovers in a noise ball whose size grows with the step, state the Robbins-Monro conditions $\sum\eta_k = \infty$, $\sum\eta_k^2 < \infty$, and use iterate averaging | SGD on $\frac12E[(x - \xi)^2]$ with $\xi \sim \mathcal N(0, 1)$ and constant step 0.1: where does $x$ hover, and with what variance? (around 0, with variance $\frac\eta{2 - \eta} = 0.053$) | `descent-lab` (stochastic), `sim-runner`, `numeric-input` | Trap: SGD with a constant step converges to the minimum (`mc.optim.sgd-constant-converges`). Edge: averaging the iterates (Polyak and Juditsky 1992) recovers the optimal $O\left(\frac1k\right)$ rate without tiny steps; 2.1 lesson 19 measures minibatch noise | N, W, C, S |
| 45 | `lsn.descent.barrier` Interior-point methods in one picture | Replace inequality constraints by a logarithmic barrier, follow the central path as the barrier weight $t$ grows, and read the duality gap $\frac mt$ | Minimise $x$ subject to $x \ge 1$ through the barrier problem $\min\,tx - \log(x - 1)$. Its minimiser? ($x = 1 + \frac1t$, so the gap is $\frac1t$) | `feasible-region` (central-path), `plot-2d`, `expression-input` | Trap: a barrier method solves the constrained problem exactly at each $t$ (`mc.optim.barrier-exact`). Edge: 20 to 50 Newton steps solve LPs and QPs of almost any size, which is what Clarabel and HiGHS's interior-point solver do | X, N, C, P |
| 46 | `lsn.descent.nonconvex` Non-convex landscapes | Distinguish minima, maxima and saddles by the Hessian, explain why critical points in high dimension are mostly saddles, and say what guarantees remain without convexity | Gradient descent with step 0.1 on $x^2 - y^2 + \frac14y^4$ from $(1, 10^{-8})$: by step 35 the gradient is below $10^{-3}$ near $(0, 0)$. A minimum? (no, a saddle: by step 103 the iterate has escaped, and it ends at $(0, \sqrt2)$) | `descent-lab` (saddle preset [mode to add]), `quadratic-form`, `choice` | Trap: a zero gradient means a minimum (`mc.optim.zero-gradient-minimum`). Edge: noise, as in SGD, pushes iterates off saddles (Dauphin and others 2014) | C, N, M, S |
| 47 | `lsn.descent.choosing` Choosing an algorithm and a stopping rule | Choose among gradient methods, Newton, L-BFGS, interior-point solvers and SGD by size, smoothness, constraints and convexity, and stop on a KKT residual or duality gap | Five problems: a 3,000-asset long-only QP; a smooth 10-parameter likelihood; a lasso with $10^5$ features; a neural network on $10^7$ examples; an LP with $10^6$ variables. Match each (interior-point QP solver; Newton or BFGS; proximal gradient or coordinate descent; SGD with momentum; HiGHS) | `card-sort`, `choice` | Trap: stop when the objective stops changing (`mc.optim.stop-on-flat`); a slow method can stall far from the optimum. Edge: a duality gap below $10^{-8}$ is a certificate; a small gradient alone is not, on a constrained problem | S, C, M, S |

Transfer screens: lesson 44 is the training loop of every model in 2.1 and 2.1b; lesson 45 is what happens inside cvxpy when it solves.

Then the Level review and homework `set.descent.scale`.

### Course AP: Optimisation at Work: Portfolios and Learning (`crs.opt-apps`, 2 levels, 11 lessons)

The course spends the tools on the two places a quant meets them most: building portfolios (mean-variance with real constraints, estimation error, turnover, every dual value read, and execution as a QP) and fitting models (regression and the lasso, logistic regression, the support vector machine and its dual, maximum likelihood), ending with how to check a solver's answer. P6 (portfolio theory) and P12 (execution) were not adopted, so this is the path's treatment of constrained portfolios and of Almgren and Chriss before 2.3 and 3.1 use them. Readings: Cornuéjols, Peña and Tütüncü, 2nd ed, chapters 3, 4 and 6 [searched]; Boyd and Vandenberghe chapter 6; the papers named per lesson.

**Level AP1: Portfolio construction** (`lvl.opt-apps.portfolios`, 6 lessons)

| # | Lesson | Objective | Hook | Widgets | Trap and edge case | Quiz |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 48 | `lsn.opt-apps.mean-variance` Mean-variance as a quadratic programme | Write $\max_{\mathbf w}\boldsymbol\mu^\top\mathbf w - \frac\gamma2\mathbf w^\top\Sigma\mathbf w$ subject to $\mathbf 1^\top\mathbf w = 1$, solve it from the KKT system, and trace the frontier as $\gamma$ varies | Two assets with expected returns 8% and 12%, volatilities 15% and 25%, correlation 0.3, risk aversion $\gamma = 4$. Optimal weights? ($(0.66, 0.34)$: expected return 9.36%, volatility 14.9%) | `frontier-lab`, `portfolio-lab`, `numeric-input` | Trap: doubling risk aversion halves the risky position (`mc.optim.risk-aversion-linear`); the weights move from $(0.5, 0.5)$ at $\gamma = 2$ to $(0.66, 0.34)$ at 4 and $(0.74, 0.26)$ at 8. Edge: as $\gamma \to \infty$ they tend to the minimum-variance portfolio $(0.82, 0.18)$ | A, N, C, P |
| 49 | `lsn.opt-apps.constrained-frontier` Constraints bend the frontier | Add long-only, position-cap and sector constraints, see the frontier kink where an asset enters or leaves, and identify corner portfolios | Unconstrained, lesson 33's minimum-variance portfolio shorts asset 3. With long-only weights, what happens to the left end of the frontier? (it moves right, from 6.0% to 9.8% volatility, and the frontier kinks where asset 3 enters) | `frontier-lab` (constraints), `portfolio-lab`, `choice` | Trap: constraints only cut off part of the unconstrained frontier (`mc.optim.constraints-truncate`); they bend it. Edge: between kinks the constrained frontier is a piece of the unconstrained frontier of the assets held | C, N, M, S |
| 50 | `lsn.opt-apps.error-maximisation` The error maximiser | Show that mean-variance weights swing with small changes in expected returns when assets are correlated, and compare remedies (shrinking $\boldsymbol\mu$, constraints, penalties) | Three assets with 20% volatility, correlations 0.8 to 0.9 and expected returns of 8% each: weights $(0.39, 0.17, 0.43)$ at $\gamma = 4$. Raise asset 1's expected return to 8.5%. New weights? ($(0.55, 0.04, 0.41)$: half a point of return moves 16 points of weight) | `frontier-lab` (sensitivity), `slider`, `numeric-input` | Trap: the weights are as reliable as the inputs look precise (`mc.optim.precise-inputs`). Edge: long-only constraints act like shrinkage of the covariance matrix (Jagannathan and Ma 2003); Michaud (1989) named the problem | A, C, N, W |
| 51 | `lsn.opt-apps.turnover` Turnover limits and transaction costs | Model a turnover limit $\sum\lvert w_i - w_i^0\rvert \le \tau$ and proportional costs with auxiliary variables $u_i \ge \pm(w_i - w_i^0)$, and predict which trades survive | Current weights $(0.2, 0.3, 0.5)$; the unconstrained optimum $(0.81, 0.19, 0)$ needs 122.5% turnover. With a 30% limit, which trades does the optimiser keep? (buy 15% of asset 1 and sell 15% of asset 3, leaving asset 2: the trades with the largest gain per unit of turnover) | `frontier-lab` (turnover), `value-table`, `choice` | Trap: scale every trade towards the target by the same factor (`mc.optim.scale-trades`). Edge: proportional costs in the objective create a no-trade band around the current weights | A, C, O, S |
| 52 | `lsn.opt-apps.reading-duals` Reading every dual value | Interpret the multipliers of the budget, long-only, cap and turnover constraints, and check each by re-solving with the limit moved | In lesson 51's solution the marginal utilities $\mu_i - \gamma(\Sigma\mathbf w)_i$ are $-0.0540$, $-0.0660$ and $-0.0816$. The turnover multiplier? ($\frac{-0.0540 - (-0.0816)}2 = 0.0138$: one unit of turnover moves half a unit from the sold asset to the bought one). Check: re-solve at 30.1% (utility rises by $1.38 \times 10^{-5}$) | `dual-pair` (portfolio), `value-table`, `numeric-input` | Trap: a constraint with a zero multiplier can be dropped in every scenario (`mc.optim.zero-dual-removable`). Edge: the untouched asset's marginal utility lies inside $[\nu - \theta, \nu + \theta] = [-0.0816, -0.0540]$, which is why it is not traded; the budget multiplier is $\nu = -0.0678$ | N, N, C, W |
| 53 | `lsn.opt-apps.execution` Optimal execution as a quadratic programme | Write Almgren and Chriss's discrete-time problem (expected cost plus $\lambda$ times the variance of a liquidation schedule) as a QP, and read its solution $x_j = X\frac{\sinh(\kappa(T - t_j))}{\sinh(\kappa T)}$ | Sell 1,000,000 shares over 5 days. A risk-neutral trader's daily sales? (200,000 each day). With urgency $\kappa T = 2$? (345,007; 238,803; 171,320; 131,616; 113,253: front-loaded) | `execution-lab` (frontier), `plot-2d`, `numeric-input` | Trap: trading evenly is optimal whatever the risk (`mc.optim.twap-optimal`). Edge: every schedule on the efficient frontier solves the QP for some $\lambda$; 3.1's market-making course extends the idea to quoting | N, N, C, P |

Then the Level review and homework `set.opt-apps.portfolios`.

(the weights grow without bound and the loss falls towards 0 without reaching it: there is no finite minimiser) | `classifier-plane` (logistic), `descent-lab` (newton), `choice` | Trap: a convex loss always has a minimiser (`mc.optim.convex-attains`). Edge: a ridge penalty restores a unique finite minimiser; scikit-learn's default $C = 1$ is that penalty | C, N, M, W |

(lands at $s = -1.44$, $\sigma = 0.24$, near the optimum 0.2) | `likelihood-lab`, `descent-lab`, `choice` | Trap: a constraint like $\sigma > 0$ looks after itself (`mc.optim.positivity-free`). Ship the weights? ---

## 3. Homework blueprint per level

### 3.0 Rules for every set

- **Issue, due and marking** follow `ASSESSMENT.md` 2.5: issued when the level's last lesson is done, due 7 days later, no feedback before submission; code items run their visible examples freely and their hidden tests at submission; long-form items are self-marked blind against the rubric, then Bertie's suggested mark and evidence quote appear beside the learner's (`AI-TUTOR.md` 2.7).
- **Size and mix.** Levels of 4 or 5 lessons get **8 items: 3 at difficulty 3, 4 at difficulty 4, 1 at difficulty 5**, about 3 hours. Levels of 6 or 7 lessons get **10 items: 4 at difficulty 3, 5 at difficulty 4, 1 at difficulty 5**, about 3 hours 30 minutes. Estimates by difficulty (d3 10 minutes, d4 20, d5 40, long-form 30, code drill 40). Inside those counts: exactly one long-form item (a derivation, proof or written explanation with a 10-point rubric, difficulty 4) and exactly one code drill (difficulty 4 unless stated). At least half the items are generator slots, so a redo is a fresh variant. At most two choice items.
- **Interleaving** (`ASSESSMENT.md` 2.3, as revised): 25 to 30% of each set's items come from earlier levels and earlier topics, unlabelled; at least two items per set are "which tool?" items whose stem names no method (for example "a solver returns a residual of $10^{-15}$; is the answer right?" with no hint that conditioning is the topic). Every long-form item has a sibling in a different story (`CONTENT-FORMAT.md` 3.8 `sibling`).
- **Long-form marking.** Every rubric has 4 to 10 points totalling 10 marks, each naming the model-answer lines that evidence it (`CONTENT-FORMAT.md` 3.4), with a `consequence` line where an error carries forward (`ASSESSMENT.md` 3.1 as revised). 7 of 10 is the bar wherever an outcome cites a rubric.
- **Code drills** list their hidden tests here so CI can check that the reference passes and each known-wrong solution fails the test it names (`CONTENT-FORMAT.md` 3.9). Every drill runs in Pyodide on the web app; in the Artifact it hands off and returns by result code (decision 8). Floating-point answers compare with a stated relative tolerance; tests on a simulation fix the seed and compare with the exact value within 4 standard errors, so they are deterministic. Drills use NumPy and, where named, SciPy (`scipy-statsmodels` group) or cvxpy (`optimisation` group); the reference solutions are CPython 3.12 and Pyodide clean.
- **Problems held back.** None of these sets reproduces an MIT 18.335J problem or a Boyd and Vandenberghe exercise; items "after" one are recreated with new numbers and wording (`CONTENT-FORMAT.md` 9.4). 2.0b's problem source has no public solutions (canon-gap, "Where the solutions are"), so every 2.0b solution here was written and verified by the app.

Below, each level gives its set id, item mix, long-form item, code drill with its hidden tests and a known-wrong solution, and two example problems in full: **example A** is the long-form item with its model answer (numbered lines) and rubric; **example B** is the difficulty-5 item with its worked answer and decision point.

### 3.1 The 17 sets of 2.0a

**EA1 Floating point as a number system** (`set.approx.floating`). 10 items (4 d3, 5 d4, 1 d5). Generators `gen.numerics.ulp-at`, `gen.numerics.format-range`, `gen.numerics.absorption-point`. Long-form: the relative rounding bound. Code drill `drl.numerics.ulp-tools` (d4): `ulp(x)` from `math.frexp`, `ulps_between(a, b)` from the ordered integer images of the bit patterns, and `correct_digits(approx, exact)`. Hidden tests: `ulp(1.0) == 2**-52`; `ulp(1.5) == 2**-52`; `ulp(1024.0) == 2**-42`; `ulps_between(0.1 + 0.2, 0.3) == 1`; `ulps_between(-0.0, 0.0) == 0`; `correct_digits(3.14159, math.pi)` within 0.01 of 6.07. Known-wrong: `ulp(x) = x * 2**-52` returns $1.5 \times 2^{-52}$ for 1.5 and fails the second test.

- *Example A (long-form, d4).* Prove that for every $x$ in the normal range of binary64, rounding to nearest satisfies $\operatorname{fl}(x) = x(1 + \delta)$ with $\lvert\delta\rvert \le 2^{-53}$, and say what changes for subnormal numbers.
  *Model answer.* (1) Take $x > 0$ with $2^e \le x < 2^{e+1}$, where $e$ is in the normal exponent range. (2) The doubles in $[2^e, 2^{e+1})$ are $2^e(1 + k2^{-52})$ for $k = 0, \dots, 2^{52} - 1$, so neighbouring doubles are $2^{e-52}$ apart. (3) Rounding to nearest moves $x$ by at most half that spacing: $\lvert\operatorname{fl}(x) - x\rvert \le 2^{e-53}$. (4) Dividing by $x \ge 2^e$: $\frac{\lvert\operatorname{fl}(x) - x\rvert}{x} \le 2^{-53}$; set $\delta = \frac{\operatorname{fl}(x) - x}x$. (5) The bound is approached: $x = 2^e(1 + 2^{-53})$ lies halfway between two doubles and has relative error $\frac{2^{-53}}{1 + 2^{-53}}$. (6) Below $2^{-1022}$ the spacing stays fixed at $2^{-1074}$ while $x$ shrinks, so the absolute bound $2^{-1075}$ holds but the relative error grows as $x$ shrinks: it is $\frac13$ at $x = 1.5 \times 2^{-1074}$ (a tie, rounded to $2^{-1073}$), and every $x \le 2^{-1075}$ rounds to 0, a relative error of 1. The model $\operatorname{fl}(x) = x(1 + \delta)$ with $\lvert\delta\rvert \le u$ fails there.
  *Rubric (10).* 1: the binade (line 1). 3: the spacing derived from the significand (line 2). 2: half the spacing (line 3). 2: the relative bound (line 4). 1: the near-attaining case (line 5). 1: subnormals (line 6).
- *Example B (d5).* A trading system stores event times as float32 seconds since 1 January 1970. (a) What is the resolution today, at about $1.79 \times 10^9$ s? (b) On which day did float32 seconds first become coarser than one second? (c) Propose a fix that keeps 4-byte storage.
  *Answer.* (a) $2^{30} \le 1.79 \times 10^9 < 2^{31}$, and float32 has a 24-bit significand, so the spacing is $2^{30-23} = 128$ s. (b) The spacing exceeds 1 s once $t \ge 2^{24} = 16{,}777{,}216$ s, about 194.2 days after the epoch: 14 July 1970. (c) Store integer milliseconds since a recent reference (a 4-byte signed integer covers about 24.8 days), or 8-byte integer nanoseconds. Decision point: the instinct is to blame rounding in arithmetic; here no arithmetic happens at all, and the loss is in storage, set by the size of the number.

**EA2 Conditioning and stability** (`set.approx.stability`). 10 items (4 d3, 5 d4, 1 d5). Generators `gen.numerics.condition-scalar`, `gen.numerics.cancellation-rewrite`, `gen.numerics.recurrence-direction`. Long-form: Welford's update. Code drill `drl.numerics.stable-stats` (d4): `welford_var(xs)` (population variance), `logsumexp(xs)` and `compound(r, n)`. Hidden tests: `welford_var([10000, 10001, 10002])` within $10^{-15}$ relative of $\frac23$; `welford_var([1e9, 1e9 + 1, 1e9 + 2])` within $10^{-12}$ relative of $\frac23$; `logsumexp([1000, 1000])` within $10^{-12}$ of $1000 + \ln2$; `logsumexp([-1000, -1000])` within $10^{-12}$ of $-1000 + \ln2$; `compound(0.05, 10**15)` within $10^{-12}$ relative of $e^{0.05}$. Known-wrong: the one-pass formula returns 0.0 on the second test; $(1 + r/n)^n$ returns 1.0 on the last.

- *Example A (long-form, d4).* Let $m_k$ and $M_k = \sum_{i \le k}(x_i - m_k)^2$ be the mean and the sum of squared deviations of the first $k$ observations. Prove $m_k = m_{k-1} + \frac{x_k - m_{k-1}}k$ and $M_k = M_{k-1} + (x_k - m_{k-1})(x_k - m_k)$, and explain why this update keeps its accuracy when the textbook one-pass formula does not.
  *Model answer.* (1) $km_k = (k - 1)m_{k-1} + x_k$, so $m_k = m_{k-1} + \frac\delta k$ with $\delta = x_k - m_{k-1}$. (2) Split $M_k = \sum_{i < k}(x_i - m_k)^2 + (x_k - m_k)^2$. (3) For $i < k$, $x_i - m_k = (x_i - m_{k-1}) - \frac\delta k$; summing squares and using $\sum_{i<k}(x_i - m_{k-1}) = 0$ gives $M_{k-1} + (k - 1)\frac{\delta^2}{k^2}$. (4) $x_k - m_k = \delta - \frac\delta k = \frac{(k - 1)\delta}k$, so $(x_k - m_k)^2 = \frac{(k - 1)^2\delta^2}{k^2}$. (5) Adding: $M_k = M_{k-1} + \frac{(k - 1)\delta^2}{k^2}(1 + k - 1) = M_{k-1} + \frac{k - 1}k\delta^2$. (6) And $\delta(x_k - m_k) = \frac{k - 1}k\delta^2$, the same: the stated update. (7) Each update works with deviations $x_k - m_{k-1}$, of the size of the spread; the one-pass formula subtracts $\bar x^2$ from $\frac1n\sum x_i^2$, two numbers of the size of the mean squared, so it loses about $2\log_{10}\frac{\lvert\bar x\rvert}{s}$ digits (8 for prices near 10,000 moving by about 1).
  *Rubric (10).* 1: the mean update (line 1). 1: the split (line 2). 3: the cross term vanishing (line 3), the step most attempts miss. 1: line 4. 2: the assembly (lines 5 and 6). 2: the numerical argument with a digit count (line 7).
