- *Example B (d5).* Lesson 13's integrals $I_n = \int_0^1x^ne^{x-1}\,dx$ satisfy $I_n = 1 - nI_{n-1}$. Run backwards, $I_{n-1} = \frac{1 - I_n}n$. Starting from the guess $I_N = 0$, how large must $N$ be for $I_{20}$ to be correct to full double precision? Justify the bound before computing.
  *Answer.* An error $\varepsilon$ in $I_N$ becomes $\frac\varepsilon{N(N - 1)\cdots21} = \varepsilon\frac{20!}{N!}$ in $I_{20}$. The guess's error is $I_N < \frac1{N + 1}$, so the error in $I_{20}$ is below $\frac{20!}{(N + 1)!}$. Full precision needs this below about $10^{-16} \times I_{20} \approx 5 \times 10^{-18}$: $21 \times 22 \times \cdots \times (N + 1) > 2 \times 10^{17}$, first true at $N + 1 = 33$ by the bound. Running it shows $N = 31$ already gives a relative error of $1.5 \times 10^{-16}$ (one ulp), $N = 32$ gives the correctly rounded value, and $N = 28$ gives $6 \times 10^{-12}$ ($I_{20} = 0.0455448840758181$). Decision point: the forward recurrence multiplies errors by $n$ and is hopeless; reversing it divides them, so a crude starting guess is harmless, a pattern (Miller's algorithm) used for Bessel functions and some option-pricing series.

**EA3 Solving equations** (`set.approx.roots`). 10 items (4 d3, 5 d4, 1 d5). Generators `gen.numerics.order-from-errors`, `gen.numerics.bisection-steps`, `gen.numerics.newton-basin`. Long-form: Newton's quadratic convergence. Code drill `drl.numerics.implied-vol` (d4): `implied_vol(price, S, K, T, r, call=True)`, safeguarded Newton inside a bracket, raising `ValueError` outside the no-arbitrage bounds. Hidden tests: round trips $\sigma \to$ price $\to \sigma$ within $10^{-8}$ on the grid $K \in \{50, 55, \dots, 200\}$, $T \in \{0.05, 0.25, 1, 2\}$, $\sigma \in \{0.05, 0.2, 0.5, 1.0\}$ wherever the price exceeds $10^{-10}S$; the 3-month call struck at 150 on 100 priced at 0.0192329 returns 0.3 within $10^{-8}$; a call priced below intrinsic value raises `ValueError`; a call priced above $S$ raises `ValueError`; at most 50 function evaluations per call. Known-wrong: plain Newton from $\sigma_0 = 0.1$ overflows on the deep out-of-the-money test.

- *Example A (long-form, d4).* Let $f$ be twice continuously differentiable with a simple root $x^\star$ ($f'(x^\star) \ne 0$). Prove that Newton's iterates satisfy $\lim\frac{e_{n+1}}{e_n^2} = \frac{f''(x^\star)}{2f'(x^\star)}$, where $e_n = x_n - x^\star$, provided they converge, and say what fails at a double root.
  *Model answer.* (1) Taylor's theorem about $x_n$ with the Lagrange remainder: $0 = f(x^\star) = f(x_n) - f'(x_n)e_n + \frac12f''(\xi_n)e_n^2$ for some $\xi_n$ between $x_n$ and $x^\star$. (2) Divide by $f'(x_n)$, non-zero near $x^\star$ by continuity: $\frac{f(x_n)}{f'(x_n)} - e_n = -\frac{f''(\xi_n)}{2f'(x_n)}e_n^2$. (3) Newton's step gives $e_{n+1} = e_n - \frac{f(x_n)}{f'(x_n)}$, so $e_{n+1} = \frac{f''(\xi_n)}{2f'(x_n)}e_n^2$. (4) As $x_n \to x^\star$, $\xi_n \to x^\star$, and by continuity $\frac{e_{n+1}}{e_n^2} \to \frac{f''(x^\star)}{2f'(x^\star)}$. (5) At a double root $f'(x^\star) = 0$, so line 2's division fails in the limit; writing $f = (x - x^\star)^2h$ gives $e_{n+1} \approx \frac12e_n$, linear convergence at rate $\frac12$.
  *Rubric (10).* 3: the expansion about $x_n$ with the remainder (line 1), the creative step. 2: line 2 with the non-zero derivative justified. 2: the error recursion (line 3). 2: the limit (line 4). 1: the double root (line 5).
- *Example B (d5).* $f(x) = (x - 1)^2(x + 2)$. Run Newton from $x_0 = 2$ and report the error ratios $\frac{e_{n+1}}{e_n}$; then run $x_{n+1} = x_n - 2\frac{f(x_n)}{f'(x_n)}$ from the same start. Explain both behaviours.
  *Answer.* Newton's errors: 0.556, 0.298, 0.155, 0.0796, 0.0403, with ratios (from $e_0 = 1$) 0.556, 0.536, 0.522, 0.512, 0.506, tending to $\frac12$: linear convergence, since $f'(1) = 0$. The modified step's errors: 0.111, $1.95 \times 10^{-3}$, $6.3 \times 10^{-7}$, $6.7 \times 10^{-14}$: quadratic again. Near a root of multiplicity $m$, $\frac f{f'} \approx \frac{x - x^\star}m$, so Newton removes only $\frac1m$ of the error each step; multiplying the step by $m$ restores the full correction. Decision point: the multiplicity is rarely known, so in practice one either deflates the root or switches to a bracketing method when the observed ratio settles near a constant.

**EA4 Interpolation** (`set.approx.interpolation`). 8 items (3 d3, 4 d4, 1 d5). Generators `gen.numerics.lagrange-through`, `gen.numerics.node-polynomial-max`, `gen.numerics.forward-from-zeros`. Long-form: the interpolation error formula. Code drill `drl.numerics.barycentric` (d4): `cheb_nodes(n)` and `bary_interp(x_nodes, y_nodes, x)` (second barycentric form). Hidden tests: interpolating $\frac1{1 + 25x^2}$ at 11 equally spaced nodes gives a maximum error within $10^{-3}$ of 1.9157 on a grid of $2 \times 10^5$ points; at 11 Chebyshev nodes within $10^{-3}$ of 0.1092; any cubic is reproduced to $10^{-12}$ from 4 nodes; repeated nodes raise `ValueError`. A fifth test interpolates the same function at 61 Chebyshev nodes and requires a maximum error below $10^{-4}$ (the reference reaches $5.4 \times 10^{-6}$). Known-wrong: solving the Vandermonde system with `np.linalg.solve` passes the first three tests but fails the fifth: its maximum error is 1.6 with NumPy's default decreasing powers and 0.015 with increasing powers (the figure depends on the column order and the LAPACK build; both are far above $10^{-4}$), because the matrix's condition number is $4.5 \times 10^{22}$ (50-digit SVD; double-precision `np.linalg.cond` underestimates it as $3 \times 10^{18}$).

- *Example A (long-form, d4).* Prove that if $p$ interpolates $f$ at distinct nodes $x_0, \dots, x_n$ in $[a, b]$ and $f$ has $n + 1$ continuous derivatives, then for each $x \in [a, b]$ there is $\xi$ with $f(x) - p(x) = \frac{f^{(n+1)}(\xi)}{(n + 1)!}\prod_i(x - x_i)$.
  *Model answer.* (1) If $x$ is a node both sides are 0. Otherwise fix $x$ and let $w(t) = \prod_i(t - x_i)$. (2) Define $g(t) = f(t) - p(t) - \frac{f(x) - p(x)}{w(x)}w(t)$. (3) $g$ vanishes at the $n + 1$ nodes and at $t = x$: $n + 2$ distinct zeros. (4) By Rolle's theorem $g'$ has at least $n + 1$ zeros, $g''$ at least $n$, and so on: $g^{(n+1)}$ has a zero $\xi$. (5) $p^{(n+1)} = 0$ because $\deg p \le n$, and $w^{(n+1)} = (n + 1)!$ because $w$ is monic of degree $n + 1$. (6) So $0 = f^{(n+1)}(\xi) - \frac{f(x) - p(x)}{w(x)}(n + 1)!$, which rearranges to the formula.
  *Rubric (10).* 1: the node case (line 1). 3: the auxiliary function (line 2), the creative step. 1: counting zeros (line 3). 2: repeated Rolle (line 4). 2: the derivatives of $p$ and $w$ (line 5). 1: the conclusion (line 6).
- *Example B (d5).* Continuously compounded zero rates are 2% at 1 year, 2.5% at 3 years and 2.8% at 5 years. (a) Find the forward rates on $[1, 3]$ and $[3, 5]$ when $\ln P(t) = -r(t)t$ is interpolated linearly. (b) Find the instantaneous forwards just before and just after 3 years when the zero rates are interpolated linearly. (c) Which would you give a swaps desk, and why?
  *Answer.* (a) The forward on an interval is the change in $r(t)t$ over its length: $\frac{0.075 - 0.02}2 = 2.75\%$ and $\frac{0.14 - 0.075}2 = 3.25\%$. (b) $f(t) = r(t) + tr'(t)$. On $[1, 3]$, $r' = 0.25\%$ a year, so $f(3^-) = 2.5\% + 3 \times 0.25\% = 3.25\%$; on $[3, 5]$, $r' = 0.15\%$ a year, so $f(3^+) = 2.5\% + 3 \times 0.15\% = 2.95\%$: the forward drops by 0.30 points at the knot, a sawtooth. (c) Linear $\ln P$ (flat forwards) for a first curve, because the forwards are positive and price instruments consistently; a desk that hedges forward-starting risk wants continuous forwards, which needs monotone convex or tension-spline interpolation (Hagan and West 2006). Decision point: zero rates look like the natural object to interpolate, but the curve is used through forwards, so the choice must be judged by the forwards it implies.

**EA5 Numerical integration** (`set.approx.quadrature`). 10 items (4 d3, 5 d4, 1 d5). Generators `gen.numerics.rule-error-ratio`, `gen.numerics.richardson-combine`, `gen.numerics.gauss-nodes`. Long-form: the trapezoid error and Richardson's step to Simpson. Code drill `drl.numerics.romberg` (d4): `romberg(f, a, b, levels)` returning the table. Hidden tests: $\int_0^1e^x\,dx$ with 5 levels within $10^{-12}$ of $e - 1$ (the reference reaches $3.3 \times 10^{-14}$); the table's first column halves the step each row; on $\int_0^1\sqrt x\,dx$ the last-column errors from the third level on fall by a factor between 2.7 and 3.0 per level, not 16 (the reference's ratios are 2.91, 2.85 and 2.83). Known-wrong: the combination $2T(h/2) - T(h)$ fails the first test.

- *Example A (long-form, d4).* Show that the composite trapezoid rule $T(h)$ on $[a, b]$ has error $-\frac{(b - a)h^2}{12}f''(\xi)$ for twice continuously differentiable $f$, and that if the error is $c_1h^2 + c_2h^4 + \cdots$, then $\frac{4T(h/2) - T(h)}3$ has error $O(h^4)$ and equals Simpson's rule.
  *Model answer.* (1) On one panel $[x, x + h]$, expand about the midpoint $m$: the exact integral is $hf(m) + \frac{h^3}{24}f''(m) + O(h^5)$. (2) The trapezoid value is $\frac h2[f(m - \frac h2) + f(m + \frac h2)] = hf(m) + \frac{h^3}8f''(m) + O(h^5)$. (3) Panel error: exact minus trapezoid $= -\frac{h^3}{12}f''(m) + O(h^5)$. (4) Summing over $\frac{b - a}h$ panels and using the intermediate value theorem on the average of $f''$: $-\frac{(b - a)h^2}{12}f''(\xi)$. (5) With $T(h) = I + c_1h^2 + c_2h^4 + \cdots$ and $T(\frac h2) = I + \frac{c_1h^2}4 + \frac{c_2h^4}{16} + \cdots$, the combination $\frac{4T(h/2) - T(h)}3 = I - \frac{c_2h^4}4 + \cdots$. (6) On one pair of panels with nodes $x_0, x_1, x_2$: $\frac43 \cdot \frac h2\cdot\frac{f_0 + 2f_1 + f_2}2 - \frac13 \cdot h\frac{f_0 + f_2}2 = \frac h6(f_0 + 4f_1 + f_2)$, Simpson's rule with spacing $\frac h2$.
  *Rubric (10).* 2: the midpoint expansion of the integral (line 1). 2: the trapezoid expansion (line 2). 1: the panel error (line 3). 2: the sum with the intermediate value theorem (line 4). 2: Richardson (line 5). 1: Simpson recovered (line 6).
- *Example B (d5).* Simpson's rule on $\int_0^1\sqrt x\,dx$ converges only like $h^{1.5}$. Explain why from example A's assumptions, and restore fourth-order (or better) convergence with one change of variable.
  *Answer.* The error expansion assumes bounded derivatives on $[0, 1]$; $\frac{d}{dx}\sqrt x$ is unbounded at 0, and the first panel's error dominates at $O(h^{1.5})$, which is why halving $h$ divides the error by $2^{1.5} = 2.83$. Substitute $x = t^2$: $\int_0^1\sqrt x\,dx = \int_0^12t^2\,dt$, a quadratic, which Simpson's rule integrates exactly with a single pair of panels (error 0). Decision point: refining the grid treats the symptom at great cost; moving the singularity out of the integrand by a substitution removes it, the same move as splitting at a payoff's kink (lesson 28).

**NL1 Solving $A\mathbf x = \mathbf b$ well** (`set.numlinalg.solving`). 8 items (3 d3, 4 d4, 1 d5). Generators `gen.numerics.residual-error-pair`, `gen.numerics.digits-lost`, `gen.numerics.growth-matrix`. Long-form: the residual bound. Code drill `drl.numerics.refine` (d4): `refine(A, b, steps)` factorises in float32 with `scipy.linalg.lu_factor`, computes residuals in float64, and returns the iterates and a `converged` flag. Hidden tests: on a seeded $100 \times 100$ matrix with $\kappa = 10^3$ the relative error is below $10^{-12}$ within 3 steps (the reference reaches $1.9 \times 10^{-14}$ in 2); on $\kappa = 10^9$ it returns `converged = False` within 10 steps and never returns `nan`; the factorisation is computed once. Known-wrong: computing the residual in float32 stalls near $10^{-5}$ and fails the first test.

- *Example A (long-form, d4).* For invertible $A$, $\mathbf b \ne \mathbf 0$, exact solution $\mathbf x$ and any $\hat{\mathbf x}$ with residual $\mathbf r = \mathbf b - A\hat{\mathbf x}$, prove $\frac{\lVert\mathbf x - \hat{\mathbf x}\rVert}{\lVert\mathbf x\rVert} \le \kappa(A)\frac{\lVert\mathbf r\rVert}{\lVert\mathbf b\rVert}$, show the bound can be attained, and explain what a small residual does and does not tell you.
  *Model answer.* (1) $A(\mathbf x - \hat{\mathbf x}) = \mathbf b - A\hat{\mathbf x} = \mathbf r$, so $\mathbf x - \hat{\mathbf x} = A^{-1}\mathbf r$ and $\lVert\mathbf x - \hat{\mathbf x}\rVert \le \lVert A^{-1}\rVert\,\lVert\mathbf r\rVert$. (2) $\lVert\mathbf b\rVert = \lVert A\mathbf x\rVert \le \lVert A\rVert\,\lVert\mathbf x\rVert$, so $\frac1{\lVert\mathbf x\rVert} \le \frac{\lVert A\rVert}{\lVert\mathbf b\rVert}$. (3) Multiply: $\frac{\lVert\mathbf x - \hat{\mathbf x}\rVert}{\lVert\mathbf x\rVert} \le \lVert A\rVert\,\lVert A^{-1}\rVert\frac{\lVert\mathbf r\rVert}{\lVert\mathbf b\rVert}$. (4) In the 2-norm, equality holds when $\mathbf b$ points along the top left singular vector $\mathbf u_1$ (so line 2 is tight) and $\mathbf r$ along $\mathbf u_n$ (so line 1 is tight). (5) A small residual says $\hat{\mathbf x}$ exactly solves a nearby system $(A + \delta A)\hat{\mathbf x} = \mathbf b$ with $\frac{\lVert\delta A\rVert}{\lVert A\rVert}$ about $\frac{\lVert\mathbf r\rVert}{\lVert A\rVert\,\lVert\hat{\mathbf x}\rVert}$: a small backward error. It says the forward error is small only when $\kappa$ is moderate: for the $10 \times 10$ Hilbert matrix a residual of $10^{-16}$ came with an error of $2.3 \times 10^{-5}$.
  *Rubric (10).* 2: the error equation (line 1). 2: the bound on $\frac1{\lVert\mathbf x\rVert}$ (line 2). 1: assembly (line 3). 2: attainment with the singular vectors named (line 4). 3: the interpretation as backward error and its limit (line 5).
- *Example B (d5).* For the $4 \times 4$ matrix with 1 on the diagonal, $-1$ below it and 1 in the last column, carry out elimination with partial pivoting by hand and find the growth factor. Generalise to $n \times n$ and say what the backward error bound then promises for $n = 60$.
  *Answer.* No row exchanges occur (every pivot candidate has magnitude 1, and the diagonal entry is chosen on ties). Each step adds the pivot row to every row below it, doubling the last column below the pivot: $U$'s last column is $(1, 2, 4, 8)$, so the growth factor is $\frac{8}1 = 2^{n-1}$ with $n = 4$. For $n = 60$ the growth is $2^{59} \approx 5.8 \times 10^{17}$, and the bound $n\rho u \approx 60 \times 5.8 \times 10^{17} \times 1.1 \times 10^{-16} \approx 3.8 \times 10^3$ promises nothing; for $\mathbf b = A\mathbf 1$ LAPACK's answer has relative error 0.32 (0.19 to 0.73 over ten seeded random right-hand sides, against exact rational solutions), although $\kappa_2 \approx 27$. Decision point: a well-conditioned matrix does not protect you when the algorithm amplifies rounding; the fix is an algorithm with bounded growth, QR (growth at most $\sqrt n$ by orthogonality) or complete pivoting.

**NL2 Structure: symmetric, banded and sparse** (`set.numlinalg.structure`). 8 items (3 d3, 4 d4, 1 d5). Generators `gen.numerics.corr-bounds`, `gen.numerics.tridiag-system`, `gen.numerics.fill-pattern`. Long-form: the valid range of a correlation. Code drill `drl.numerics.thomas` (d3): `thomas(a, b, c, d)` for a tridiagonal system. Hidden tests: agrees with `np.linalg.solve` to $10^{-12}$ on a seeded diagonally dominant system of size 1,000; solves $n = 200{,}000$ within the drill's time limit (a dense solve cannot); rejects mismatched lengths with `ValueError`. Known-wrong: forgetting to update the right-hand side in the forward sweep gives errors of 0.22 and fails the first test.

- *Example A (long-form, d4).* Show that the matrix with unit diagonal and off-diagonal entries $\rho_{12}$, $\rho_{13}$, $\rho_{23}$ is positive semidefinite exactly when $\rho_{23}$ lies in $\left[\rho_{12}\rho_{13} - \sqrt{(1 - \rho_{12}^2)(1 - \rho_{13}^2)}, \rho_{12}\rho_{13} + \sqrt{(1 - \rho_{12}^2)(1 - \rho_{13}^2)}\right]$ (given $\lvert\rho_{12}\rvert, \lvert\rho_{13}\rvert < 1$), and evaluate it for $\rho_{12} = \rho_{13} = 0.8$.
  *Model answer.* (1) A symmetric matrix is positive semidefinite exactly when its Cholesky-type elimination produces non-negative pivots; with unit diagonal the first pivot is 1. (2) Eliminating the first row leaves the $2 \times 2$ Schur complement $\begin{pmatrix}1 - \rho_{12}^2 & \rho_{23} - \rho_{12}\rho_{13}\\\rho_{23} - \rho_{12}\rho_{13} & 1 - \rho_{13}^2\end{pmatrix}$. (3) Its diagonal is positive, so it is positive semidefinite exactly when its determinant is non-negative: $(\rho_{23} - \rho_{12}\rho_{13})^2 \le (1 - \rho_{12}^2)(1 - \rho_{13}^2)$. (4) Taking square roots gives the interval. (5) In words: the conditional correlation of assets 2 and 3 given asset 1 must lie in $[-1, 1]$. (6) For $\rho_{12} = \rho_{13} = 0.8$: $0.64 \pm 0.36$, so $\rho_{23} \in [0.28, 1]$; a desk's 0.2 is invalid.
  *Rubric (10).* 2: the elimination criterion (line 1). 3: the Schur complement (line 2), the creative step. 2: the determinant condition (line 3). 1: the interval (line 4). 1: the interpretation (line 5). 1: the number (line 6).
- *Example B (d5).* The desk's matrix with $\rho_{12} = \rho_{13} = 0.8$ and $\rho_{23} = 0.2$ has eigenvalues $-0.036$, $0.8$ and $2.236$. Repair it two ways, by clipping the negative eigenvalue and rescaling to a unit diagonal, and by Higham's alternating projections, and compare the results' distances from the input.
  *Answer.* Clip and rescale: $(0.7767, 0.7767, 0.2065)$ at Frobenius distance 0.0475. Alternating projections: $(0.7789, 0.7789, 0.2135)$ at distance 0.0462, with smallest eigenvalue 0 to rounding. Both are valid; the second is the nearest, because it projects onto the intersection of the positive semidefinite cone and the unit-diagonal matrices instead of projecting onto one and then rescaling, which moves off the nearest point. Decision point: rescaling after clipping feels like a projection but is not one; uniqueness of the nearest point (the set is convex, 2.0b lesson 20) is what lets an algorithm be judged against an answer.

**NL3 Eigenvalues and the SVD in floating point** (`set.numlinalg.eigen`). 8 items (3 d3, 4 d4, 1 d5). Generators `gen.numerics.shift-rate`, `gen.numerics.weyl-bound`, `gen.numerics.jordan-perturb`. Long-form: eigenvector sensitivity at a small gap. Code drill `drl.numerics.rqi` (d4): `rayleigh_quotient_iteration(S, x0, mu0, tol)` returning the eigenpair and the residual history. Hidden tests: on a seeded symmetric $50 \times 50$ matrix with eigenvalues $1, 2, \dots, 50$, from a start vector within 0.1 of the eigenvector for 11 and shift 10.9, it returns 11 to residual $10^{-12}$ within 4 iterations (the reference's residuals are $8.9 \times 10^{-3}$, $5.8 \times 10^{-9}$, $7.1 \times 10^{-15}$); the returned vector has unit norm; from a random start it returns some eigenpair with residual below $10^{-12}$. Known-wrong: keeping the shift fixed at 10.9 (plain inverse iteration) needs 10 iterations and fails the iteration-count test.

- *Example A (long-form, d4).* Let $A = \begin{pmatrix}1 + \varepsilon & \delta\\\delta & 1 - \varepsilon\end{pmatrix}$ with $\varepsilon > 0$. Find its eigenvalues and the angle of its eigenvectors from the coordinate axes, compare the eigenvalue change with Weyl's bound $\lVert E\rVert_2$ for $E = \begin{pmatrix}0 & \delta\\\delta & 0\end{pmatrix}$, and explain what this means for principal components with close variances.
  *Model answer.* (1) $A = I + \begin{pmatrix}\varepsilon & \delta\\\delta & -\varepsilon\end{pmatrix}$, and the second matrix has eigenvalues $\pm\sqrt{\varepsilon^2 + \delta^2}$. (2) So $A$'s eigenvalues are $1 \pm \sqrt{\varepsilon^2 + \delta^2}$, moved from $1 \pm \varepsilon$ by $\sqrt{\varepsilon^2 + \delta^2} - \varepsilon \le \delta = \lVert E\rVert_2$, as Weyl's inequality requires. (3) The traceless part is $\sqrt{\varepsilon^2 + \delta^2}$ times a reflection whose axis makes angle $\theta$ with $\tan 2\theta = \frac\delta\varepsilon$; the eigenvectors are that axis and its perpendicular. (4) With $\varepsilon = \delta = 10^{-4}$: the eigenvalues move by $4.1 \times 10^{-5}$, but $\theta = 22.5^\circ$: the eigenvectors turn through a large angle. (5) The rotation is of order $\frac{\lVert E\rVert}{\text{gap}} = \frac\delta{2\varepsilon}$, unbounded as the gap closes, while eigenvalue changes stay bounded by $\lVert E\rVert$. (6) Two principal components of returns with nearly equal variances can therefore swap or rotate between windows; the plane they span is stable, the individual directions are not.
  *Rubric (10).* 2: the decomposition (line 1). 2: the eigenvalues and the Weyl comparison (line 2). 2: the eigenvector angle (line 3). 1: the numbers (line 4). 2: the gap argument (line 5). 1: the PCA consequence (line 6).
- *Example B (d5).* For Läuchli's matrix with rows $(1, 1)$, $(\delta, 0)$, $(0, \delta)$, compute the smallest singular value by the eigenvalues of $A^\top A$ and by `svd` for $\delta = 10^{-7}$, $10^{-8}$ and $10^{-9}$, and explain the threshold.
  *Answer.* $A^\top A = \begin{pmatrix}1 + \delta^2 & 1\\1 & 1 + \delta^2\end{pmatrix}$, with eigenvalues $2 + \delta^2$ and $\delta^2$. In floating point $1 + \delta^2$ rounds to 1 when $\delta^2 < u$, that is $\delta \lesssim 1.05 \times 10^{-8}$; then $A^\top A$ is exactly singular. Results: $\delta = 10^{-7}$ gives $9.996 \times 10^{-8}$ from $A^\top A$ (0.04% off) and $10^{-7}$ from `svd`; $\delta = 10^{-8}$ and $10^{-9}$ give 0 from $A^\top A$ and the exact $\delta$ from `svd`. Decision point: forming $A^\top A$ squares the condition number, so singular values below $\sqrt u\,\sigma_1$ are destroyed before any algorithm runs; the SVD works on $A$ directly.

**NL4 Iterative methods** (`set.numlinalg.iterative`). 8 items (3 d3, 4 d4, 1 d5). Generators `gen.numerics.spectral-radius`, `gen.numerics.iterations-for-digits`, `gen.numerics.cg-bound`. Long-form: Jacobi under diagonal dominance. Code drill `drl.numerics.cg` (d4): `conjugate_gradient(A_mul, b, tol, maxiter)` taking a function that multiplies by $A$. Hidden tests: the 1-D model problem with 100 unknowns and $\mathbf b = \mathbf 1$ converges to relative residual $10^{-10}$ within 100 iterations (the reference needs 50, because the symmetric right-hand side excites half the eigenvectors); a seeded $3 \times 3$ matrix with eigenvalues $\{1, 1, 100\}$ converges in 2 iterations to $10^{-12}$; the function never forms $A$. Known-wrong: the step $\alpha = \frac{\mathbf r^\top\mathbf r}{\mathbf p^\top\mathbf p}$ (steepest descent's form with the wrong denominator) fails the iteration-count tests.

- *Example A (long-form, d4).* Prove that the Jacobi iteration converges for every starting vector when $A$ is strictly diagonally dominant by rows, $\lvert a_{ii}\rvert > \sum_{j \ne i}\lvert a_{ij}\rvert$.
  *Model answer.* (1) Jacobi is $\mathbf x_{k+1} = D^{-1}(\mathbf b - (A - D)\mathbf x_k)$ with $D$ the diagonal of $A$; the exact solution is a fixed point. (2) The error obeys $\mathbf e_{k+1} = G\mathbf e_k$ with $G = -D^{-1}(A - D)$, so $\mathbf e_k = G^k\mathbf e_0$. (3) Row $i$ of $G$ has entries $-\frac{a_{ij}}{a_{ii}}$ for $j \ne i$ and 0 on the diagonal. (4) The $\infty$-norm is the largest absolute row sum: $\lVert G\rVert_\infty = \max_i\frac{\sum_{j \ne i}\lvert a_{ij}\rvert}{\lvert a_{ii}\rvert} < 1$ by diagonal dominance. (5) Hence $\lVert\mathbf e_k\rVert_\infty \le \lVert G\rVert_\infty^k\lVert\mathbf e_0\rVert_\infty \to 0$. (6) The condition is sufficient, not necessary: lesson 44's swapped system fails it and diverges, but other matrices that fail it converge, because the exact criterion is the spectral radius of $G$.
  *Rubric (10).* 1: the fixed point (line 1). 2: the error recursion (line 2). 2: the entries of $G$ (line 3). 3: the norm bound from dominance (line 4). 1: convergence (line 5). 1: sufficiency only (line 6).
- *Example B (d5).* A symmetric positive definite $3 \times 3$ matrix has eigenvalues $1$, $1$ and $100$. Explain why conjugate gradients solves $A\mathbf x = \mathbf b$ in at most 2 iterations in exact arithmetic, for any $\mathbf b$, although $\kappa = 100$.
  *Answer.* After $k$ iterations CG's error is $p_k(A)\mathbf e_0$ for the polynomial $p_k$ of degree $k$ with $p_k(0) = 1$ that minimises the $A$-norm of the error. The polynomial $p_2(t) = \frac{(1 - t)(100 - t)}{100}$ has degree 2, satisfies $p_2(0) = 1$ and vanishes at both distinct eigenvalues, so $p_2(A) = 0$ and the error is zero after 2 steps. A seeded run gives residuals 1.41, 1.44 and $1.5 \times 10^{-14}$. Decision point: the $\sqrt\kappa$ bound is a worst case over spread-out spectra; clustered eigenvalues are why preconditioners aim to cluster, not merely to shrink $\kappa$.

**PD1 From trees to the heat equation** (`set.pde.heat`). 8 items (3 d3, 4 d4, 1 d5). Generators `gen.numerics.mode-decay`, `gen.numerics.bs-transform-coeffs`, `gen.numerics.boundary-values`. Long-form: the Black-Scholes equation from the tree. Code drill `drl.numerics.heat-explicit` (d3): `explicit_heat(u0, dx, r, steps)` on $u_t = u_{xx}$ with zero boundary values. Hidden tests: from $\sin x + \frac12\sin3x$ on $[0, \pi]$ with 50 intervals and $r = 0.4$, the value at $\frac\pi2$ when $t$ first reaches 0.5 is within $10^{-3}$ of the exact $e^{-t} - \frac12e^{-9t}$ (0.6010 at $t = 0.5$); $r > \frac12$ raises `ValueError`; the boundary values stay exactly 0. Known-wrong: updating the boundary nodes with the interior formula makes them drift and fails the last test.

- *Example A (long-form, d4).* In a binomial tree over $[t, t + \Delta t]$ the share moves from $S$ to $Se^{\pm\sigma\sqrt{\Delta t}}$ with risk-neutral probability $q$ chosen so that $E[S_{t+\Delta t}] = Se^{r\Delta t}$, and $V(S, t) = e^{-r\Delta t}[qV(Su, t + \Delta t) + (1 - q)V(Sd, t + \Delta t)]$. Assuming $V$ smooth, derive the Black-Scholes equation as $\Delta t \to 0$.
  *Model answer.* (1) Write $V = V(S, t + \Delta t)$ and expand in the price move $\Delta S = S(e^{\pm\sigma\sqrt{\Delta t}} - 1)$: $V(S + \Delta S) = V + V_S\Delta S + \frac12V_{SS}\Delta S^2 + o(\Delta t)$. (2) The risk-neutral mean move is $E[\Delta S] = S(e^{r\Delta t} - 1) = rS\Delta t + o(\Delta t)$. (3) The mean squared move is $E[\Delta S^2] = \sigma^2S^2\Delta t + o(\Delta t)$, because each move has size $S\sigma\sqrt{\Delta t}(1 + O(\sqrt{\Delta t}))$. (4) So $qV(Su) + (1 - q)V(Sd) = V + rSV_S\Delta t + \frac12\sigma^2S^2V_{SS}\Delta t + o(\Delta t)$. (5) Discounting: $V(S, t) = (1 - r\Delta t)\left[V + \left(rSV_S + \frac12\sigma^2S^2V_{SS}\right)\Delta t\right] + o(\Delta t)$. (6) Write $V(S, t + \Delta t) = V(S, t) + V_t\Delta t + o(\Delta t)$, cancel $V(S, t)$, divide by $\Delta t$ and let it go to 0: $V_t + \frac12\sigma^2S^2V_{SS} + rSV_S - rV = 0$. (7) The real drift never appears: only the risk-neutral mean of line 2 does.
  *Rubric (10).* 2: the expansion (line 1). 2: the mean move (line 2). 2: the mean squared move (line 3), the step most attempts get wrong. 1: line 4. 1: discounting (line 5). 1: the limit (line 6). 1: no real drift (line 7).
- *Example B (d5).* Solve $u_t = u_{xx}$ on $[0, \pi]$ with $u(0, t) = u(\pi, t) = 0$ and $u(x, 0) = \sin x + \frac12\sin3x$, and find $u\left(\frac\pi2, 0.5\right)$. Then say what happens if you try to recover $u(x, 0)$ from noisy measurements of $u(x, 0.5)$.
  *Answer.* Each mode $\sin kx$ satisfies the equation with factor $e^{-k^2t}$, so $u = e^{-t}\sin x + \frac12e^{-9t}\sin3x$. At $x = \frac\pi2$, $\sin\frac{3\pi}2 = -1$: $u = e^{-0.5} - \frac12e^{-4.5} = 0.6065 - 0.0056 = 0.6010$. Running backwards multiplies mode $k$ by $e^{k^2t}$: measurement noise of $10^{-6}$ in mode 10 becomes $10^{-6}e^{50} \approx 5 \times 10^{15}$. Decision point: the forward problem smooths and is stable; the backward problem amplifies high frequencies without bound, which is why the pricing equation is always solved from the payoff in the direction of time to expiry.

**PD2 Time stepping and stability** (`set.pde.stability`). 10 items (4 d3, 5 d4, 1 d5). Generators `gen.numerics.amplification`, `gen.numerics.stable-step`, `gen.numerics.stiff-euler`. Long-form: von Neumann analysis of the θ-scheme. Code drill `drl.numerics.theta-scheme` (d4): `theta_heat(N, M, theta, T)` solving $u_t = u_{xx}$ from $\sin x$ on $[0, \pi]$ with the Thomas algorithm. Hidden tests: with $M = N$, the error at $\frac\pi2$ against $e^{-T}$ falls by a ratio in $[3.8, 4.2]$ from $N = 80$ to 160 for $\theta = \frac12$ (reference 4.00) and in $[1.9, 2.2]$ for $\theta = 1$ (reference 2.02); $\theta = 0$ with $r > \frac12$ raises `ValueError`; $N = 10^4$ runs within the time limit (no dense solve). Known-wrong: a dense `np.linalg.solve` each step fails the time limit.

- *Example A (long-form, d4).* For $u_t = u_{xx}$ with the θ-scheme $\frac{u_j^{n+1} - u_j^n}{\Delta t} = \theta\delta^2u_j^{n+1} + (1 - \theta)\delta^2u_j^n$, where $\delta^2u_j = \frac{u_{j+1} - 2u_j + u_{j-1}}{\Delta x^2}$, derive the amplification factor and the stability condition for every $\theta \in [0, 1]$.
  *Model answer.* (1) Substitute $u_j^n = g^ne^{ikj\Delta x}$. (2) Then $\delta^2u_j = -\frac{4s^2}{\Delta x^2}u_j$ with $s = \sin\frac{k\Delta x}2$. (3) The scheme becomes $g - 1 = -4rs^2(\theta g + 1 - \theta)$ with $r = \frac{\Delta t}{\Delta x^2}$. (4) Solving: $g = \frac{1 - 4(1 - \theta)rs^2}{1 + 4\theta rs^2}$. (5) $g \le 1$ always; stability needs $g \ge -1$ for every $s^2 \in [0, 1]$, the worst case being $s^2 = 1$: $1 - 4(1 - \theta)r \ge -1 - 4\theta r$, that is $4r(1 - 2\theta) \le 2$. (6) For $\theta \ge \frac12$ this holds for every $r$ (unconditional stability); for $\theta < \frac12$ it needs $r \le \frac1{2(1 - 2\theta)}$, which is $r \le \frac12$ for the explicit scheme. (7) At $\theta = \frac12$, $g \to -1$ as $r \to \infty$ for high modes: stable but barely damped, the source of Crank-Nicolson's oscillations.
  *Rubric (10).* 1: the trial solution (line 1). 2: the symbol of $\delta^2$ (line 2). 2: the amplification factor (lines 3 and 4). 2: the worst case and the inequality (line 5). 2: the two regimes (line 6). 1: the Crank-Nicolson remark (line 7).
- *Example B (d5).* Crank-Nicolson with $r = 50$ on a payoff with a kink. (a) How much of the highest mode survives 10 steps? (b) How much survives 2 implicit Euler steps followed by 8 Crank-Nicolson steps? (c) What does this do to gamma near the strike?
  *Answer.* (a) $g = \frac{1 - 100}{1 + 100} = -0.980$, and $0.980^{10} = 0.82$, alternating in sign. (b) Implicit Euler's factor for the highest mode is $\frac1{1 + 200} = 0.0050$, so two steps leave $2.5 \times 10^{-5}$, and the following Crank-Nicolson steps cannot amplify it: about $2.1 \times 10^{-5}$ remains. (c) The kink puts weight on every mode; undamped high modes show as a sawtooth in second differences, so gamma oscillates node to node near the strike; after the Rannacher steps it is smooth. Decision point: second order in time is a statement about smooth solutions; with a non-smooth start the damping of high modes matters more than the order, and two first-order steps buy it at almost no cost.

**PD3 Pricing on a grid** (`set.pde.pricing`). 10 items (4 d3, 5 d4, 1 d5). Generators `gen.numerics.grid-greeks`, `gen.numerics.strike-position`, `gen.numerics.barrier-shift`. Long-form: Crank-Nicolson's gamma and Rannacher's fix. Code drill `drl.numerics.cn-pricer` (d4): `cn_price(S0, K, r, sigma, T, Smax, NS, NT, kind, american=False, rannacher=2)`. Hidden tests: the 1-year at-the-money call ($r = 5\%$, $\sigma = 20\%$, $S_{\max} = 400$) with $N_S = N_T = 400$ is within $3 \times 10^{-3}$ of 10.4506 (reference $-0.0025$); the error ratio from 200 to 400 lies in $[3.8, 4.2]$; put-call parity holds on the grid to $10^{-3}$; the American put with projection is within 0.01 of 6.090 (reference 6.086); a hidden variant prices at $S_0 = 100.5$, between nodes, within $3 \times 10^{-3}$ (reference error $-0.00016$). Known-wrong: reading the nearest node instead of interpolating is off by 0.32 there and fails it.

- *Example A (long-form, d4).* A Crank-Nicolson grid prices a European call accurately (error 0.0025), but its gamma at the nodes nearest the strike alternates in sign from node to node in the first days after the start of the backward run. Explain the cause using the amplification factor, and describe and justify the standard fix.
  *Model answer.* (1) The payoff $\max(S - K, 0)$ has a kink at $K$, so its expansion in grid modes has non-negligible weight on the highest frequencies. (2) Crank-Nicolson's amplification factor is $g = \frac{1 - 2rs^2}{1 + 2rs^2}$; for the highest modes ($s^2 \approx 1$) and the large $r$ typical of pricing grids, $g$ is close to $-1$. (3) Those modes are therefore barely damped and flip sign every step, while the exact solution damps them like $e^{-k^2\tau}$. (4) The price at a node is a smooth average and hides them, but gamma is a second difference, which amplifies high modes by about $\frac4{\Delta S^2}$: the sawtooth appears there first. (5) The fix: take the first two (to four) steps with implicit Euler (Rannacher smoothing), whose factor $\frac1{1 + 4rs^2}$ is near 0 for high modes. (6) Two first-order steps add an error of order $\Delta\tau^2$ in total, not per step, so the global order stays 2. (7) Alternatives: smaller first steps, or averaging the payoff over each cell (lesson 61).
  *Rubric (10).* 1: the kink's spectrum (line 1). 2: the amplification factor near $-1$ (line 2). 1: undamped alternation (line 3). 2: why gamma shows it (line 4). 2: Rannacher's fix with its factor (line 5). 1: order preserved (line 6). 1: an alternative (line 7).
- *Example B (d5).* A down-and-out call has barrier 90, monitored daily (252 times a year), $\sigma = 20\%$. (a) Which continuously monitored barrier gives approximately the same price? (b) For an up-and-out call with barrier 120, in which direction does the correction move the barrier? (c) Why can a grid not use the discrete barrier on its own nodes?
  *Answer.* (a) Broadie, Glasserman and Kou's correction moves the barrier away from the share price by the factor $e^{0.5826\sigma\sqrt{\Delta t}}$: $90e^{-0.5826 \times 0.2/\sqrt{252}} = 89.34$. (b) Away from the price again, upwards: $120e^{+0.00734} = 120.88$. (c) A grid can apply the knock-out only at monitoring dates, setting $V = 0$ below 90 on those dates and solving freely between them; that is exact, but it needs the time grid to contain every monitoring date and costs accuracy near the barrier. The shifted continuous barrier gives a closed form or a boundary condition at every step. Decision point: discrete monitoring makes knock-out less likely, so the equivalent continuous barrier sits further from the price, never closer.

**MO1 Random numbers** (`set.montecarlo.rng`). 8 items (3 d3, 4 d4, 1 d5). Generators `gen.numerics.lcg-period`, `gen.numerics.seed-collision`, `gen.numerics.rejection-rate`. Long-form: RANDU's planes. Code drill `drl.numerics.inverse-transform` (d3): `exponential(u, lam)`, `cauchy(u)` and `discrete(u, probs)` (the last by bisection on the cumulative probabilities). Hidden tests: the Kolmogorov-Smirnov distance of $10^5$ seeded exponential draws from the exact CDF is below 0.0052, the 1% critical value; `exponential(0.0, 1.0)` is finite (the draw uses $1 - u$ on $[0, 1)$); `discrete` returns each index with frequency within 4 standard errors on $10^5$ draws; probabilities not summing to 1 raise `ValueError`. Known-wrong: $-\frac{\ln u}\lambda$ returns `inf` at $u = 0$ and fails the second test.

- *Example A (long-form, d4).* RANDU is $x_{k+1} = ax_k \bmod 2^{31}$ with $a = 2^{16} + 3$. Prove $x_{k+2} = 6x_{k+1} - 9x_k \pmod{2^{31}}$, and deduce that the points $(u_k, u_{k+1}, u_{k+2})$ with $u = \frac x{2^{31}}$ lie on at most 15 parallel planes.
  *Model answer.* (1) $x_{k+2} \equiv a^2x_k$. (2) $a^2 = 2^{32} + 6 \times 2^{16} + 9 \equiv 6 \times 2^{16} + 9 \pmod{2^{31}}$, since $2^{32}$ is a multiple of $2^{31}$. (3) $6 \times 2^{16} + 9 = 6(a - 3) + 9 = 6a - 9$. (4) So $x_{k+2} \equiv (6a - 9)x_k = 6x_{k+1} - 9x_k$, using $ax_k \equiv x_{k+1}$. (5) Dividing by $2^{31}$: $u_{k+2} - 6u_{k+1} + 9u_k$ is an integer. (6) With each $u$ in $(0, 1)$, that integer lies strictly between $-6$ and 10, so it takes one of the 15 values $-5, \dots, 9$: every triple lies on one of 15 planes $u_{k+2} - 6u_{k+1} + 9u_k = c$.
  *Rubric (10).* 1: line 1. 2: reducing $a^2$ (line 2). 2: rewriting in terms of $a$ (line 3), the creative step. 1: line 4. 2: the integer relation (line 5). 2: counting the planes (line 6).
- *Example B (d5).* Sample the half-normal density $\sqrt{\frac2\pi}e^{-x^2/2}$ on $x \ge 0$ by acceptance-rejection with an $\mathrm{Expo}(1)$ proposal. Find the smallest valid bound $c$, the acceptance rate, and the acceptance test.
  *Answer.* The ratio $\frac{f(x)}{g(x)} = \sqrt{\frac2\pi}e^{x - x^2/2}$ is largest at $x = 1$, where it equals $\sqrt{\frac{2e}\pi} = 1.3155 = c$. Acceptance rate $\frac1c = 76.0\%$. Accept a proposal $X$ when $U \le \frac{f(X)}{cg(X)} = e^{-(X - 1)^2/2}$. A random sign then gives a standard normal. Decision point: the best $c$ comes from maximising the ratio, which here is a one-line calculus problem; a looser $c$ is still correct but wastes draws in proportion.

**MO2 Estimators, error and bias** (`set.montecarlo.error`). 8 items (3 d3, 4 d4, 1 d5). Generators `gen.numerics.paths-for-error`, `gen.numerics.efficiency-compare`, `gen.numerics.bias-variance-split`. Long-form: the optimal split between steps and paths. Code drill `drl.numerics.mc-price` (d3): `mc_call(S0, K, r, sigma, T, n, seed)` returning the estimate and its standard error by exact simulation of $S_T$. Hidden tests: with $n = 10^5$ and seed 1 the estimate lies within 4 standard errors of the Black-Scholes value; the standard error at $4n$ is between 0.45 and 0.55 of that at $n$; the same seed gives the same result twice; $n < 2$ raises `ValueError`. Known-wrong: a standard error computed as $\frac{\hat\sigma}n$ fails the ratio test.

- *Example A (long-form, d4).* A Monte Carlo estimator with time step $\Delta t$ and $N$ paths has bias $c\Delta t$ and variance $\frac{\sigma^2}N$, and costs $\frac N{\Delta t}$ path-steps. For a target root-mean-square error $\varepsilon$, find the cheapest $\Delta t$ and $N$ and the cost's order in $\varepsilon$, and say how a scheme with bias $c\Delta t^2$ changes the answer.
  *Model answer.* (1) $\text{MSE} = c^2\Delta t^2 + \frac{\sigma^2}N$. (2) Minimise the cost $\frac N{\Delta t}$ subject to $\text{MSE} = \varepsilon^2$. (3) Write the bias share as $c^2\Delta t^2 = \alpha\varepsilon^2$ and the variance share as $\frac{\sigma^2}N = (1 - \alpha)\varepsilon^2$: $\Delta t = \frac{\sqrt\alpha\,\varepsilon}c$ and $N = \frac{\sigma^2}{(1 - \alpha)\varepsilon^2}$. (4) Cost $= \frac{c\sigma^2}{\sqrt\alpha(1 - \alpha)\varepsilon^3}$, smallest at $\alpha = \frac13$ (maximise $\sqrt\alpha(1 - \alpha)$). (5) So $\Delta t \propto \varepsilon$, $N \propto \varepsilon^{-2}$ and cost $\propto \varepsilon^{-3}$: a tenfold accuracy gain costs a thousandfold. (6) With bias $c\Delta t^2$: $\Delta t \propto \varepsilon^{1/2}$ and cost $\propto \varepsilon^{-5/2}$, which is why higher weak order (or multilevel Monte Carlo, about $\varepsilon^{-2}$) pays.
  *Rubric (10).* 1: the MSE (line 1). 1: the problem (line 2). 2: the parametrisation (line 3). 3: the optimum $\alpha = \frac13$ (line 4). 2: the orders (line 5). 1: the second-order case (line 6).
- *Example B (d5).* Estimate $P(S_T > 200)$ for a share at 100 ($\sigma = 20\%$, $r = 5\%$, $T = 1$) to a 10% relative standard error. How many paths does plain Monte Carlo need, and how many does importance sampling with the mean of $Z$ shifted to the barrier's $z$-value, 3.32, need?
  *Answer.* $p = 4.57 \times 10^{-4}$. Plain: $N = \frac{1 - p}{p \times 0.01} = 2.19 \times 10^5$. Shifted: the weighted indicator's variance is $e^{\mu^2}\Phi(-2\mu) - p^2 = 7.8 \times 10^{-7}$ with $\mu = 3.32$, against $p(1 - p) = 4.6 \times 10^{-4}$ plain: a ratio of 585, so $N = 374$ paths. Decision point: the plain estimator spends almost every path on outcomes that contribute nothing; moving the sampling mass to the event is worth a factor of hundreds, and the exact second moment proves it rather than a lucky run.

**MO3 Variance reduction** (`set.montecarlo.variance-reduction`). 10 items (4 d3, 5 d4, 1 d5). Generators `gen.numerics.control-factor`, `gen.numerics.antithetic-sign`, `gen.numerics.is-shift`. Long-form: the optimal control coefficient. Code drill `drl.numerics.variance-reduction` (d4): `antithetic(f, n, seed)` and `control_variate(y, x, ex)`. Hidden tests: for the at-the-money call the antithetic pair's variance divided by half a single path's lies in $[0.4, 0.6]$ (reference 0.50); for the straddle it exceeds 1 (reference 1.65); the control variate with $e^{-rT}S_T$ cuts the variance by a factor above 5 (reference 6.9) and its estimate lies within 4 standard errors of Black-Scholes. Known-wrong: a control coefficient fixed at 1 fails the variance-factor test.

- *Example A (long-form, d4).* For an estimator $Y$ and a control $X$ with known mean $E[X]$, show that $Y - b(X - E[X])$ is unbiased for every $b$, find the $b$ that minimises its variance, and show the minimal variance is $(1 - \rho^2)\operatorname{Var}(Y)$ with $\rho = \operatorname{Corr}(X, Y)$.
  *Model answer.* (1) $E[Y - b(X - E[X])] = E[Y] - b \cdot 0 = E[Y]$. (2) $v(b) = \operatorname{Var}(Y) - 2b\operatorname{Cov}(X, Y) + b^2\operatorname{Var}(X)$. (3) $v$ is a convex quadratic in $b$; $v'(b) = 0$ gives $b^\star = \frac{\operatorname{Cov}(X, Y)}{\operatorname{Var}(X)}$. (4) Substituting: $v(b^\star) = \operatorname{Var}(Y) - \frac{\operatorname{Cov}(X, Y)^2}{\operatorname{Var}(X)} = \operatorname{Var}(Y)(1 - \rho^2)$. (5) $b^\star$ is the slope of the regression of $Y$ on $X$, and the controlled estimator is the regression's intercept at $X = E[X]$. (6) For the at-the-money call with $X = e^{-rT}S_T$, $\rho = 0.924$ gives $1 - \rho^2 = 0.145$: 6.9 times fewer paths for the same error.
  *Rubric (10).* 1: unbiasedness (line 1). 2: the variance (line 2). 2: the optimal $b$ (line 3). 2: the minimal variance (line 4). 2: the regression view (line 5). 1: the number (line 6).
- *Example B (d5).* Split any payoff of a standard normal $Z$ into even and odd parts, $f = f_e + f_o$. Show that the antithetic estimator $\frac{f(Z) + f(-Z)}2$ has variance $\operatorname{Var}(f_e(Z))$, compare it with two independent draws, and use this to predict the results for a call and a straddle.
  *Answer.* $\frac{f(Z) + f(-Z)}2 = f_e(Z)$ exactly. Two independent draws have variance $\frac12\operatorname{Var}(f(Z)) = \frac12[\operatorname{Var}(f_e) + \operatorname{Var}(f_o)]$, because $f_e(Z)$ and $f_o(Z)$ are uncorrelated when $Z$ is symmetric. So antithetics win exactly when $\operatorname{Var}(f_e) < \operatorname{Var}(f_o)$: when the payoff is "mostly odd" in $Z$. A call is close to monotone (mostly odd), giving a ratio near 0.5; a straddle is close to even, giving a ratio above 1 (1.65 in a seeded run of $2 \times 10^6$). Decision point: the rule "antithetics help monotone payoffs" becomes a test you can compute before running anything.

**MO4 Quasi-Monte Carlo** (`set.montecarlo.qmc`). 8 items (3 d3, 4 d4, 1 d5). Generators `gen.numerics.radical-inverse`, `gen.numerics.qmc-vs-mc-error`, `gen.numerics.effective-dim`. Long-form: why quasi-Monte Carlo wins, and why it needs randomising. Code drill `drl.numerics.halton` (d3): `radical_inverse(k, base)` and `halton(n, bases)`. Hidden tests: `radical_inverse(5, 2) == 0.625`; `halton` point 7 in bases 2 and 3 is $(0.875, \frac59)$; 4,096 Halton points (from index 1) estimate $\int_{[0,1]^2}xy\,dx\,dy = \frac14$ within $10^{-3}$ (the reference's error is $-3.8 \times 10^{-4}$; at 1,024 points it is $-1.2 \times 10^{-3}$, outside the tolerance); a base of 1 raises `ValueError`. Known-wrong: reversing decimal digits instead of base-$b$ digits fails the first test.

- *Example A (long-form, d4).* Explain to a colleague, in a paragraph with the relevant bounds, why scrambled Sobol points priced lesson 85's 64-date Asian option with a standard error of 0.008 against plain Monte Carlo's 0.28 at the same cost, why the Brownian-bridge construction mattered, and why the run was repeated 32 times.
  *Model answer.* (1) Plain Monte Carlo's error is $\frac\sigma{\sqrt N}$ whatever the dimension. (2) Quasi-Monte Carlo replaces random points by points that fill the cube evenly; the Koksma-Hlawka bound is $V(f)D_N^\star$ with $D_N^\star = O\left(\frac{(\log N)^d}N\right)$, close to $N^{-1}$ when the integrand effectively depends on few coordinates. (3) A 64-date path is a 64-dimensional integral, and in that nominal dimension the bound is useless; what matters is effective dimension. (4) Built step by step, every coordinate carries a similar share of the path's variance; built by the Brownian bridge, the first coordinate sets the endpoint and the next few the large-scale shape, so the first coordinates, where Sobol points are most even, carry most of the variance. (5) The measured standard errors (0.044 step by step, 0.008 with the bridge) show it. (6) Unrandomised points give one number with no error estimate; scrambling makes each replicate an unbiased estimate, so 32 independent replicates give a standard error, and the comparison with plain Monte Carlo is honest.
  *Rubric (10).* 1: Monte Carlo's rate (line 1). 2: the Koksma-Hlawka bound and its condition (line 2). 1: nominal dimension (line 3). 3: the bridge's reallocation of variance (line 4). 1: the evidence (line 5). 2: randomisation and replicates (line 6).
- *Example B (d5).* Compute the first four points of the 2-D Halton sequence in bases 2 and 3, and the 7th point. Then explain why Halton points in bases 59 and 61 look like stripes for the first few hundred points.
  *Answer.* Base 2 gives $\frac12, \frac14, \frac34, \frac18$; base 3 gives $\frac13, \frac23, \frac19, \frac49$; the points are $\left(\frac12, \frac13\right)$, $\left(\frac14, \frac23\right)$, $\left(\frac34, \frac19\right)$, $\left(\frac18, \frac49\right)$. The 7th: $7 = 111_2 \to 0.875$ and $7 = 21_3 \to 0.12_3 = \frac59$, so $(0.875, 0.5556)$. In bases 59 and 61, the first 59 points have first coordinates $\frac k{59}$ and second coordinates $\frac k{61}$, rising together almost in step, so they lie near a line; only after many cycles of both bases do the points spread. Decision point: low discrepancy is an asymptotic property; in high bases "asymptotic" starts late, which is one reason Sobol points (all base 2) are preferred in finance.

**MO5 Sensitivities, early exercise and the exit** (`set.montecarlo.greeks`). 10 items (4 d3, 5 d4, 1 d5). Generators `gen.numerics.crn-variance`, `gen.numerics.pathwise-estimator`, `gen.numerics.lr-weight`. Long-form: pathwise and likelihood-ratio deltas. Code drill `drl.numerics.mc-greeks` (d4): `pathwise_delta_call`, `lr_delta_digital` and `bump_delta(price_fn, S0, h, seed)`, a central difference $\frac{V(S_0 + h) - V(S_0 - h)}{2h}$ with common random numbers. Hidden tests: with $10^5$ paths each estimate lies within 4 standard errors of the closed form (0.6368 and 0.01876); the bump estimator's standard error with $h = 0.01$ is below 0.005; `bump_delta(price_fn, S0, 0.0, seed)` returns exactly 0, which holds only if both runs share their draws. Known-wrong: independent seeds in the bumped runs give a standard error near 3.3 (the payoff's standard deviation 14.7 over $\sqrt{10^5}$, times $\frac{\sqrt2}{2h}$) and fail the third test.

- *Example A (long-form, d4).* Derive the pathwise estimator of a European call's delta under Black-Scholes dynamics and the likelihood-ratio estimator of a digital call's delta, and explain why each works where it does and fails where it does.
  *Model answer.* (1) $S_T = S_0e^{(r - \sigma^2/2)T + \sigma\sqrt TZ}$, so $\frac{\partial S_T}{\partial S_0} = \frac{S_T}{S_0}$. (2) The call payoff's derivative is $\mathbb 1\{S_T > K\}$ except at $S_T = K$, a probability-zero event. (3) Pathwise delta: $E\left[e^{-rT}\mathbb 1\{S_T > K\}\frac{S_T}{S_0}\right]$, valid because the payoff is Lipschitz in $S_0$, so differentiation and expectation can be exchanged. (4) For the digital $\mathbb 1\{S_T > K\}$ the pathwise derivative is 0 almost surely, but the price's derivative is not: the exchange fails because the payoff jumps. (5) Likelihood ratio: write the price as $\int f(s)p(s; S_0)\,ds$ and differentiate the lognormal density: $\frac{\partial\ln p}{\partial S_0} = \frac Z{S_0\sigma\sqrt T}$, so delta $= E\left[e^{-rT}f(S_T)\frac Z{S_0\sigma\sqrt T}\right]$ for any payoff $f$. (6) It is unbiased for the digital (standard error $8.8 \times 10^{-5}$ with $10^5$ paths against the exact 0.01876) but noisier than pathwise for smooth payoffs, because the weight $Z$ multiplies the whole payoff.
  *Rubric (10).* 1: the derivative of $S_T$ (line 1). 1: the payoff's derivative (line 2). 2: the pathwise estimator with its condition (line 3). 2: the digital's failure (line 4). 3: the likelihood-ratio estimator derived (line 5). 1: the trade-off (line 6).
- *Example B (d5).* Lesson 89's Longstaff and Schwartz estimate of the 50-date American put was about 6.06 (standard error 0.02) on fresh paths, the 50-date tree value is 6.079 and the continuously exercisable value is 6.090. Explain the three gaps, which direction each estimator is biased, and how you would tighten the comparison.
  *Answer.* 6.090 against 6.079: the contract itself, since 50 exercise dates are worth less than continuous exercise. 6.079 against about 6.06: the fitted exercise rule is suboptimal, and any rule applied to fresh paths gives a lower bound on the true value, so the estimator is biased low; with a standard error of 0.02 the gap is about one standard error. In-sample pricing on the fitting paths can be biased high, because the rule is tuned to their noise. To tighten: more paths, a richer basis (adding the discounted European put as a basis function is a standard choice), and a dual upper bound (Andersen and Broadie 2004, *Management Science* 50(9)) so the true value is bracketed. Decision point: a single Monte Carlo number for an American option is not a price until it is bracketed from both sides or checked against an independent method.

### 3.2 The 10 sets of 2.0b

**LP1 Linear programmes and their geometry** (`set.linprog.geometry`). 10 items (4 d3, 5 d4, 1 d5). Generators `gen.optim.two-var-lp`, `gen.optim.basis-count`, `gen.optim.arbitrage-bonds`. Long-form: the vertex theorem. Code drill `drl.optim.lp-model` (d3): `solve_allocation(returns, capital, risk_weights, risk_budget, caps)` with `scipy.optimize.linprog` (HiGHS), returning the allocation, the expected return and the constraint multipliers as non-negative prices. Hidden tests: lesson 1's data give $(60, 40)$ within $10^{-7}$, 9.6, and prices $(0.06, 0.2, 0)$; with the risk budget at 19 the return is 9.8; an infeasible variant (capital negative) returns status "infeasible" rather than raising. Known-wrong: passing the returns to `linprog` without negating them minimises and returns $(0, 0)$.

- *Example A (long-form, d4).* Let $P$ be a bounded polyhedron with vertices $\mathbf v_1, \dots, \mathbf v_m$, and assume every point of $P$ is a convex combination of them. Prove that $\max_{\mathbf x \in P}\mathbf c^\top\mathbf x$ is attained at a vertex, and that the set of maximisers is a face of $P$ (a vertex, an edge, or larger).
  *Model answer.* (1) Write any $\mathbf x \in P$ as $\sum_i\theta_i\mathbf v_i$ with $\theta_i \ge 0$, $\sum\theta_i = 1$. (2) By linearity $\mathbf c^\top\mathbf x = \sum_i\theta_i\mathbf c^\top\mathbf v_i$. (3) A weighted average is at most its largest term: $\mathbf c^\top\mathbf x \le \max_i\mathbf c^\top\mathbf v_i = M$. (4) The vertex achieving $M$ is in $P$, so the maximum is $M$, attained there. (5) If $\mathbf x$ is a maximiser, equality in line 3 forces $\theta_i = 0$ for every vertex with $\mathbf c^\top\mathbf v_i < M$: maximisers are exactly the convex combinations of the maximising vertices. (6) That set is $P \cap \{\mathbf c^\top\mathbf x = M\}$, the intersection of $P$ with a supporting hyperplane: a face. In lesson 2's tied example it is the edge from $(30, 50)$ to $(60, 40)$.
  *Rubric (10).* 2: the representation (line 1). 1: linearity (line 2). 3: the averaging bound (line 3), the core of the proof. 1: attainment (line 4). 2: the characterisation of maximisers (line 5). 1: the face (line 6).
- *Example B (d5).* A fund tracks a target with two assets whose returns over four periods are $\mathbf a = (2\%, -1\%, 3\%, 0\%)$ and $\mathbf b = (1\%, 1\%, -2\%, 2\%)$; the target returned $(1.5\%, 0\%, 1\%, 1\%)$. Choose weights $w_1 + w_2 = 1$ to minimise the sum of absolute tracking errors. Write the linear programme and solve it.
  *Answer.* Variables $w_1, w_2$ (free) and $u_1, \dots, u_4 \ge 0$; minimise $\sum_tu_t$ subject to $u_t \ge e_t$ and $u_t \ge -e_t$ with $e_t = r_t - w_1a_t - w_2b_t$, and $w_1 + w_2 = 1$. The minimum total absolute error is 0.5%, and it is **not attained at a single point**: with $w_2 = 1 - w_1$ the objective is $\lvert0.5 - w_1\rvert + 2\lvert w_1 - 0.5\rvert + \lvert3 - 5w_1\rvert + \lvert2w_1 - 1\rvert$ (in %), whose slopes $+1 + 2 + 2 - 5$ cancel on $[0.5, 0.6]$, so every $w_1 \in [0.5, 0.6]$ is optimal (at $w_1 = 0.5$ the errors are $0, 0, 0.5\%, 0$; at $w_1 = 0.6$ they are $-0.1\%, 0.2\%, 0, 0.2\%$). HiGHS returns the vertex $w = (0.5, 0.5)$; the grader accepts any $w_1$ in the interval with value 0.5% and gives credit for naming the tie, which is example A's face of maximisers. Decision point: absolute values are not linear, but minimising them is: at the optimum each $u_t$ equals $\lvert e_t\rvert$, because a larger $u_t$ only raises the objective. The same trick linearises turnover in 2.0b lesson 51.

**LP2 The simplex method** (`set.linprog.simplex`). 8 items (3 d3, 4 d4, 1 d5). Generators `gen.optim.ratio-test`, `gen.optim.pivot-step`, `gen.optim.phase-one`. Long-form: the simplex method by hand. Code drill `drl.optim.vertex-enum` (d4): `basic_solutions(A, b)` for $\{A\mathbf x = \mathbf b, \mathbf x \ge \mathbf 0\}$, returning every basic solution with a feasibility flag. Hidden tests: the allocation problem in standard form has 9 basic solutions and 5 feasible ones; the best feasible vertex equals `linprog`'s optimum; singular bases are skipped without an exception; a degenerate vertex named by two bases is reported once. Known-wrong: a feasibility test `x >= 0` with no tolerance rejects a degenerate vertex computed as $-10^{-17}$ and fails the degenerate test; the reference uses a tolerance of $10^{-9}$.

- *Example A (long-form, d4).* Solve lesson 1's allocation problem (maximise $0.08x + 0.12y$ subject to $x + y \le 100$, $0.1x + 0.3y \le 18$, $y \le 50$, $x, y \ge 0$) by the simplex method from the origin, entering the variable with the largest reduced cost each time; give each pivot's entering and leaving variables, and read the dual values from the final row.
  *Model answer.* (1) Slacks $s_1, s_2, s_3$; start at $x = y = 0$ with $z = 0.08x + 0.12y$. (2) Pivot 1: $y$ enters (0.12). Ratios: $s_1$ 100, $s_2$ 60, $s_3$ 50; $s_3$ leaves; $y = 50 - s_3$, $z = 6 + 0.08x - 0.12s_3$. (3) Pivot 2: $x$ enters (0.08). Ratios: $s_1 = 50 - x + s_3$ gives 50, $s_2 = 3 - 0.1x + 0.3s_3$ gives 30; $s_2$ leaves; $x = 30 - 10s_2 + 3s_3$, $z = 8.4 - 0.8s_2 + 0.12s_3$. (4) Pivot 3: $s_3$ enters (0.12). From $s_1 = 20 + 10s_2 - 2s_3$ the ratio is 10, from $y = 50 - s_3$ it is 50; $x$ grows with $s_3$ and sets no limit; $s_1$ leaves; $s_3 = 10 + 5s_2 - 0.5s_1$. (5) Then $x = 60 + 5s_2 - 1.5s_1$, $y = 40 - 5s_2 + 0.5s_1$, $z = 9.6 - 0.06s_1 - 0.2s_2$: every reduced cost is negative, so $(60, 40)$ with $z = 9.6$ is optimal. (6) The negated reduced costs of the slacks are the dual values: $y_1 = 0.06$, $y_2 = 0.2$, and $y_3 = 0$ because $s_3$ is basic: complementary slackness.
  *Rubric (10).* 1: set-up (line 1). 2: pivot 1 with its ratio test (line 2). 2: pivot 2 (line 3). 2: pivot 3 with the variable that sets no limit (line 4). 2: optimality from the signs (line 5). 1: the duals (line 6).
- *Example B (d5).* Solve by branch and bound: maximise $y$ subject to $-x + y \le 1$, $3x + 2y \le 12$, $2x + 3y \le 12$, with $x, y \ge 0$ whole numbers. Draw the tree.
  *Answer.* Root: the LP relaxation gives $(1.8, 2.8)$, $y = 2.8$, an upper bound. Branch on $y$. Branch $y \ge 3$: then $-x + 3 \le 1$ forces $x \ge 2$, but $2x + 9 \le 12$ forces $x \le 1.5$: infeasible, pruned. Branch $y \le 2$: the LP optimum is $y = 2$, attained at integer points such as $(1, 2)$ and $(2, 2)$, so the incumbent is 2. No open branch can beat 2: the integer optimum is $y = 2$. Decision point: rounding $(1.8, 2.8)$ to $(2, 3)$ violates $2x + 3y \le 12$; the relaxation's role is to bound, not to be rounded.

**LP3 Duality** (`set.linprog.duality`). 10 items (4 d3, 5 d4, 1 d5). Generators `gen.optim.dual-of`, `gen.optim.shadow-range`, `gen.optim.state-prices`. Long-form: the dual of an inequality-form programme and its certificate. Code drill `drl.optim.dual-check` (d4): `dual_of(c, A, b)` for $\max\{\mathbf c^\top\mathbf x : A\mathbf x \le \mathbf b, \mathbf x \ge \mathbf 0\}$, solving both programmes and returning the gap and the complementary-slackness products. Hidden tests: the allocation problem's gap is below $10^{-9}$ with dual $(0.06, 0.2, 0)$; every product $y_i(b_i - (A\mathbf x)_i)$ and $x_j((A^\top\mathbf y)_j - c_j)$ is below $10^{-9}$; an unbounded primal is reported with an infeasible dual. Known-wrong: a dual with $A$ instead of $A^\top$ fails on a non-square hidden instance.

- *Example A (long-form, d4).* For the primal $\max\{\mathbf c^\top\mathbf x : A\mathbf x \le \mathbf b, \mathbf x \ge \mathbf 0\}$, derive the dual by bounding the objective with multipliers, prove weak duality, and verify strong duality and complementary slackness on the allocation problem.
  *Model answer.* (1) For $\mathbf y \ge \mathbf 0$ and feasible $\mathbf x$: $\mathbf y^\top A\mathbf x \le \mathbf y^\top\mathbf b$. (2) If also $A^\top\mathbf y \ge \mathbf c$, then $\mathbf c^\top\mathbf x \le (A^\top\mathbf y)^\top\mathbf x$ because $\mathbf x \ge \mathbf 0$. (3) Together: $\mathbf c^\top\mathbf x \le \mathbf b^\top\mathbf y$, weak duality. (4) The best bound is the dual: $\min\{\mathbf b^\top\mathbf y : A^\top\mathbf y \ge \mathbf c, \mathbf y \ge \mathbf 0\}$. (5) Allocation: $\mathbf y = (0.06, 0.2, 0)$ satisfies $0.06 + 0.1 \times 0.2 = 0.08 \ge 0.08$ and $0.06 + 0.3 \times 0.2 + 0 = 0.12 \ge 0.12$, with value $6 + 3.6 = 9.6$, equal to the primal's 9.6: strong duality. (6) Complementary slackness: $y_3 = 0$ where $y \le 50$ is slack (40); both primal variables are positive and both dual constraints hold with equality.
  *Rubric (10).* 2: line 1 with the sign of $\mathbf y$ used. 2: line 2 with the sign of $\mathbf x$ used. 1: weak duality (line 3). 2: the dual (line 4). 2: the numerical check (line 5). 1: complementary slackness (line 6).
- *Example B (d5).* In lesson 17's market (bond 0.95 paying 1 and 1; share 100 paying 120 or 90; call 10 paying 20 or 0), build the arbitrage explicitly and state the Farkas certificate it corresponds to.
  *Answer.* State prices from the bond and share solve $\psi_u + \psi_d = 0.95$ and $120\psi_u + 90\psi_d = 100$: $\psi = (0.48333, 0.46667)$, so the call is worth $20\psi_u = 9.667$. Replicate the call with $\Delta = \frac{20 - 0}{120 - 90} = \frac23$ share and a bond position $B$ with $\frac23 \times 90 + B = 0$, so $B = -60$ at maturity, costing $-57$ today: the replica costs $66.667 - 57 = 9.667$. Sell the call at 10, buy the replica: 0.333 today, zero in both states. The certificate is the portfolio $\mathbf h = (-60, \frac23, -1)$ in (bond, share, call): payoff $D\mathbf h = \mathbf 0$ and cost $\mathbf p^\top\mathbf h = -0.333 < 0$, the alternative that Farkas' lemma says exists exactly when no positive $\boldsymbol\psi$ solves $D^\top\boldsymbol\psi = \mathbf p$. Decision point: the arbitrage and the missing state prices are the same fact seen from the primal and the dual.

**CX1 Convex sets** (`set.convex.sets`). 8 items (3 d3, 4 d4, 1 d5). Generators `gen.optim.convex-or-not`, `gen.optim.simplex-projection`, `gen.optim.separating-line`. Long-form: convexity of the valid correlation matrices, and a non-convex portfolio set. Code drill `drl.optim.project-simplex` (d3): `project_simplex(v)` by sorting. Hidden tests: $(0.5, 0.4, 0.3, -0.2) \mapsto (0.4333, 0.3333, 0.2333, 0)$ to $10^{-12}$; a point already in the simplex maps to itself; on 1,000 seeded vectors the output sums to 1, is non-negative, and matches a QP solution from SciPy to $10^{-8}$. Known-wrong: clipping at 0 and renormalising returns $(0.4167, 0.3333, 0.25, 0)$ and fails the first test.

- *Example A (long-form, d4).* (a) Prove that the set of $n \times n$ correlation matrices (symmetric, positive semidefinite, unit diagonal) is convex. (b) Prove that the set of fully invested portfolios of three assets with at most two non-zero weights is not convex. (c) Say what (a) implies for repairing an invalid matrix.
  *Model answer.* (1) Take correlation matrices $A$, $B$ and $\theta \in [0, 1]$; let $C = \theta A + (1 - \theta)B$. (2) $C$ is symmetric as a sum of symmetric matrices. (3) For every $\mathbf v$: $\mathbf v^\top C\mathbf v = \theta\mathbf v^\top A\mathbf v + (1 - \theta)\mathbf v^\top B\mathbf v \ge 0$. (4) $C_{ii} = \theta + (1 - \theta) = 1$. So $C$ is a correlation matrix. (5) For (b): $(1, 0, 0)$ and $(0, \frac12, \frac12)$ each have at most two non-zero weights, but their midpoint $(\frac12, \frac14, \frac14)$ has three, so the set is not convex. (6) For (c): the nearest correlation matrix in the Frobenius norm is the projection onto a closed convex set, hence unique, and alternating projections converge to it (2.0a lesson 37).
  *Rubric (10).* 1: set-up (line 1). 1: symmetry (line 2). 3: the quadratic form (line 3). 1: the diagonal (line 4). 3: a valid counterexample (line 5); a pair whose midpoint stays in the set, such as $(1, 0, 0)$ and $(0, 1, 0)$, earns nothing for this point. 1: uniqueness (line 6).
- *Example B (d5).* Derive the projection of $\mathbf v \in \mathbb R^n$ onto the simplex $\{\mathbf w : \mathbf w \ge \mathbf 0, \mathbf 1^\top\mathbf w = 1\}$ from the KKT conditions, and apply it to $\mathbf v = (0.5, 0.4, 0.3, -0.2)$.
  *Answer.* Minimise $\frac12\lVert\mathbf w - \mathbf v\rVert^2$ subject to the constraints. Stationarity: $w_i - v_i - \nu - \lambda_i = 0$ with $\lambda_i \ge 0$ and $\lambda_iw_i = 0$. Where $w_i > 0$, $\lambda_i = 0$ and $w_i = v_i + \nu$; where $w_i = 0$, $v_i + \nu = -\lambda_i \le 0$. So $w_i = \max(v_i - \tau, 0)$ with $\tau = -\nu$ chosen to make the weights sum to 1. Sorting $\mathbf v$ in decreasing order and keeping the largest $k$ with $v_{(k)} > \frac{\sum_{j \le k}v_{(j)} - 1}k$: here $k = 3$ and $\tau = \frac{1.2 - 1}3 = \frac1{15}$, giving $(0.4333, 0.3333, 0.2333, 0)$. Decision point: the KKT conditions turn a constrained problem into "shift and clip", with one scalar to find; renormalising instead changes the relative weights.

**CX2 Convex functions** (`set.convex.functions`). 10 items (4 d3, 5 d4, 1 d5). Generators `gen.optim.hessian-psd`, `gen.optim.composition-rule`, `gen.optim.strong-convexity`. Long-form: three convexity proofs, the exit's style. Code drill `drl.optim.convexity-check` (d3): `hessian_psd(f, points)` by central differences and `midpoint_counterexample(f, domain, seed)`. Hidden tests: flags $xy$ as non-convex with a counterexample; finds none for $\frac{x^2}y$ on $y > 0$, $\log(e^x + e^y)$ and $\lVert A\mathbf x - \mathbf b\rVert_2$; finds one for $e^{-x^2}$; never samples outside the stated domain. Known-wrong: checking only the Hessian's diagonal passes $xy$ (zero diagonal) and fails the first test.

- *Example A (long-form, d4).* Decide and prove the convexity of (a) $f(\mathbf w) = \sqrt{\mathbf w^\top\Sigma\mathbf w}$ for positive definite $\Sigma$; (b) $g(\mathbf x) = \log\sum_ie^{x_i}$; (c) $h(x, y) = \frac{x^2}y$ on $y > 0$.
  *Model answer.* (1) (a) Factor $\Sigma = LL^\top$ (Cholesky). Then $f(\mathbf w) = \lVert L^\top\mathbf w\rVert_2$. (2) A norm is convex by the triangle inequality and homogeneity: $\lVert\theta\mathbf u + (1 - \theta)\mathbf v\rVert \le \theta\lVert\mathbf u\rVert + (1 - \theta)\lVert\mathbf v\rVert$; composing with the linear map $L^\top$ keeps convexity. (3) (b) With $\mathbf p = \operatorname{softmax}(\mathbf x)$: $\nabla g = \mathbf p$ and $\nabla^2g = \operatorname{diag}(\mathbf p) - \mathbf p\mathbf p^\top$. (4) For any $\mathbf v$: $\mathbf v^\top\nabla^2g\,\mathbf v = \sum_ip_iv_i^2 - \left(\sum_ip_iv_i\right)^2$, the variance of $v_I$ when $I$ has distribution $\mathbf p$: non-negative. (5) (c) $\nabla^2h = \frac2{y^3}\begin{pmatrix}y^2 & -xy\\-xy & x^2\end{pmatrix} = \frac2{y^3}\begin{pmatrix}y\\-x\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}y & -x\end{pmatrix}$, a non-negative multiple of an outer product on $y > 0$: positive semidefinite. (6) Each domain is convex ($\mathbb R^n$, $\mathbb R^n$, the half-plane $y > 0$), so the second-order or direct arguments apply.
  *Rubric (10).* 2: the norm form (line 1). 2: convexity of a norm composed with a linear map (line 2). 1: the gradient and Hessian (line 3). 2: the variance argument (line 4), the creative step. 2: the outer product (line 5). 1: domains (line 6).
- *Example B (d5).* Prove that $f(X) = -\log\det X$ is convex on positive definite matrices by restricting it to lines.
  *Answer.* Take $X \succ 0$ and symmetric $V$, and let $\phi(t) = f(X + tV)$ for $t$ with $X + tV \succ 0$. Write $X + tV = X^{1/2}(I + tM)X^{1/2}$ with $M = X^{-1/2}VX^{-1/2}$, symmetric with eigenvalues $\lambda_i$. Then $\phi(t) = -\log\det X - \sum_i\log(1 + t\lambda_i)$, so $\phi''(t) = \sum_i\frac{\lambda_i^2}{(1 + t\lambda_i)^2} \ge 0$. A function convex on every line through its convex domain is convex. Decision point: the Hessian of $-\log\det$ is a fourth-order object; restricting to a line and diagonalising turns it into a sum of one-variable logarithms, which is why the line test is the standard tool for matrix functions (Boyd and Vandenberghe §3.1.5 [check]).

**CX3 Duality and the KKT conditions** (`set.convex.kkt`). 10 items (4 d3, 5 d4, 1 d5). Generators `gen.optim.kkt-solve`, `gen.optim.dual-function`, `gen.optim.active-set`. Long-form: the long-only minimum-variance portfolio by KKT. Code drill `drl.optim.cvxpy-minvar` (d4, `optimisation` group): `min_var_long_only(Sigma)` in cvxpy, returning the weights and the multipliers of $\mathbf w \ge \mathbf 0$. Hidden tests: lesson 33's assets give $(0.8947, 0.1053, 0)$ to $10^{-6}$ and a multiplier of 0.0329 on $w_3 \ge 0$ (to $10^{-4}$) with zeros on the others; the KKT residual is below $10^{-7}$; a matrix that is not positive semidefinite is rejected before solving. Known-wrong: minimising $\sqrt{\mathbf w^\top\Sigma\mathbf w}$ written as `cp.sqrt(cp.quad_form(...))` is rejected by DCP and fails every test. Fallback if cvxpy does not run in Pyodide (section 0.5, item 5): the same drill through `highspy`'s QP solver.

- *Example A (long-form, d4).* For three assets with volatilities 10%, 20%, 30% and correlations $\rho_{12} = 0.3$, $\rho_{13} = 0.9$, $\rho_{23} = 0.3$, find the long-only minimum-variance portfolio from the KKT conditions and interpret every multiplier.
  *Model answer.* (1) Problem: minimise $\mathbf w^\top\Sigma\mathbf w$ subject to $\mathbf 1^\top\mathbf w = 1$ and $\mathbf w \ge \mathbf 0$. KKT: $2\Sigma\mathbf w = \nu\mathbf 1 + \boldsymbol\lambda$, $\boldsymbol\lambda \ge \mathbf 0$, $\lambda_iw_i = 0$. (2) The unconstrained solution $(1.306, 0.057, -0.363)$ shorts asset 3, so guess the active set $\{w_3 = 0\}$. (3) Solve on assets 1 and 2: $\mathbf w_{12} \propto \Sigma_{12}^{-1}\mathbf 1$, giving $(0.8947, 0.1053, 0)$, variance $0.00958$ (volatility 9.8%). (4) Check: $2\Sigma\mathbf w = (0.01916, 0.01916, 0.05211)$, so $\nu = 0.01916$ and $\lambda_3 = 0.05211 - 0.01916 = 0.0329 > 0$: dual feasible, so the guess is right. (5) $\nu$ is the marginal variance of every held asset (twice the covariance of each with the portfolio): at the optimum it is equal across held assets. (6) $\lambda_3 > 0$ says asset 3's marginal variance exceeds the held assets' by 0.0329: adding a small long position raises variance, and the constraint stops the optimiser shorting it, which it would do (line 2) because asset 3 hedges asset 1.
  *Rubric (10).* 2: the KKT system (line 1). 1: the active-set guess with its reason (line 2). 2: the reduced solve (line 3). 3: the check of dual feasibility (line 4), without which the answer is a guess. 1: $\nu$ interpreted (line 5). 1: $\lambda_3$ interpreted (line 6).
- *Example B (d5).* Explain why the problem "minimise $2 + e^{-3a}$ subject to $\frac{a^2}b \le 0$, with domain $b > 0$" has optimal value 3 but dual optimal value 2, and say which hypothesis of strong duality fails.
  *Answer.* The constraint forces $a = 0$ (with $b > 0$ free), so $p^\star = 2 + e^0 = 3$. The Lagrangian is $2 + e^{-3a} + \lambda\frac{a^2}b$, always above 2; for any $\lambda \ge 0$, taking $a \to \infty$ with $b = a^3$ makes it tend to 2, so $g(\lambda) = 2$ and $d^\star = 2$: a duality gap of 1. The problem is convex (a convex objective, and $\frac{a^2}b$ is convex on $b > 0$), but no point satisfies $\frac{a^2}b < 0$, so Slater's condition fails. The item is recreated after Boyd and Vandenberghe's exercise 5.21 [recalled] with new numbers and wording, as section 3.0 requires. Decision point: convexity alone is not enough; the constraint qualification is what lets multipliers exist.

**DA1 Unconstrained methods and their rates** (`set.descent.rates`). 10 items (4 d3, 5 d4, 1 d5). Generators `gen.optim.gd-iterations`, `gen.optim.armijo-step`, `gen.optim.order-from-sequence`. Long-form: gradient descent's linear rate. Code drill `drl.optim.gd-newton` (d4): `gradient_descent(f, grad, x0, backtracking=True)` and `newton(f, grad, hess, x0, damped=True)`, each returning the iterate history, run on a seeded logistic regression. Hidden tests: Newton reaches gradient norm $10^{-10}$ within 8 iterations; in gradient descent's last 20 iterations the ratios $\frac{e_{k+1}}{e_k}$ vary by less than 0.05 (linear convergence); Newton's $\frac{e_{k+1}}{e_k^2}$ stays below 10 once $e_k < 0.1$ (quadratic); undamped Newton on $\log\cosh x$ from 1.1 diverges and the damped version converges. Known-wrong: a fixed step of 1 for gradient descent diverges on the hidden ill-conditioned instance.

- *Example A (long-form, d4).* For $f(\mathbf x) = \frac12\mathbf x^\top A\mathbf x - \mathbf b^\top\mathbf x$ with $\mu I \preceq A \preceq LI$, prove that gradient descent with step $\eta$ satisfies $\lVert\mathbf x_k - \mathbf x^\star\rVert \le \max_i\lvert1 - \eta\lambda_i\rvert^k\lVert\mathbf x_0 - \mathbf x^\star\rVert$, and find the step that minimises the rate.
  *Model answer.* (1) $\nabla f = A\mathbf x - \mathbf b = A(\mathbf x - \mathbf x^\star)$, since $A\mathbf x^\star = \mathbf b$. (2) So $\mathbf e_{k+1} = \mathbf e_k - \eta A\mathbf e_k = (I - \eta A)\mathbf e_k$. (3) $I - \eta A$ is symmetric with eigenvalues $1 - \eta\lambda_i$, so $\lVert I - \eta A\rVert_2 = \max_i\lvert1 - \eta\lambda_i\rvert$ and $\lVert\mathbf e_k\rVert \le \max_i\lvert1 - \eta\lambda_i\rvert^k\lVert\mathbf e_0\rVert$. (4) With $\eta = \frac1L$ the rate is $1 - \frac\mu L = 1 - \frac1\kappa$. (5) The worst eigenvalues are $\mu$ and $L$; the rate $\max(\lvert1 - \eta\mu\rvert, \lvert1 - \eta L\rvert)$ is smallest when they balance, $1 - \eta\mu = \eta L - 1$, so $\eta = \frac2{\mu + L}$ and the rate is $\frac{\kappa - 1}{\kappa + 1}$. (6) Iterations for a factor $\varepsilon$: about $\frac\kappa2\ln\frac1\varepsilon$; for $\kappa = 100$ and $\varepsilon = 10^{-6}$, about 690 with the best step against 1,375 with $\frac1L$.
  *Rubric (10).* 2: the gradient in error form (line 1). 1: the error recursion (line 2). 2: the norm via eigenvalues (line 3). 1: the $\frac1L$ rate (line 4). 3: the optimal step (line 5). 1: the iteration count (line 6).
- *Example B (d5).* Newton's method on $f(x) = \log\cosh x$ (minimum at 0). Write the iteration, run it from $x_0 = 1.0$ and from $x_0 = 1.1$, find the threshold between convergence and divergence, and show that a backtracking line search fixes it.
  *Answer.* $f' = \tanh x$, $f'' = \operatorname{sech}^2x$, so $x_{k+1} = x_k - \sinh x_k\cosh x_k = x_k - \frac12\sinh2x_k$. From 1.0: $-0.813, 0.409, -0.0473, 7.1 \times 10^{-5}, -2.3 \times 10^{-13}$: converges. From 1.1: $-1.129, 1.234, -1.695, 5.715$: diverges. The threshold solves $\frac12\sinh2x = 2x$ (a step that lands on $-x$): $x \approx 1.0887$. With backtracking, a step is halved until $f$ decreases enough; since the Newton direction is a descent direction ($f'' > 0$), some step always works, and near 0 the full step is accepted, so quadratic convergence returns. Decision point: Newton's fast local rate says nothing about its global behaviour; on a function that flattens (curvature $\to 0$) the full step overshoots.

**DA2 Constraints, momentum and noise** (`set.descent.scale`). 10 items (4 d3, 5 d4, 1 d5). Generators `gen.optim.sgd-noise`, `gen.optim.prox-step`, `gen.optim.accel-iterations`. Long-form: constant-step SGD's noise. Code drill `drl.optim.ista` (d4): `ista(X, y, lam, eta, iters)` for $\frac1{2n}\lVert\mathbf y - X\boldsymbol\beta\rVert^2 + \lambda\lVert\boldsymbol\beta\rVert_1$. Hidden tests: on a seeded problem the KKT residual (each zero coefficient has $\frac1n\lvert\mathbf x_j^\top\mathbf r\rvert \le \lambda + 10^{-6}$, each non-zero one has $\frac1n\mathbf x_j^\top\mathbf r = \lambda\operatorname{sign}\beta_j$ within $10^{-6}$) holds after the run; every coefficient is exactly 0 for $\lambda \ge \lambda_{\max}$; the objective never increases with $\eta = \frac1L$. Known-wrong: a gradient step on the non-smooth objective (using $\operatorname{sign}$ as its gradient) leaves coefficients oscillating around 0 and fails the KKT test.

- *Example A (long-form, d4).* SGD on $f(x) = \frac12E[(x - \xi)^2]$ with $\xi$ independent, mean 0 and variance $\sigma^2$, uses $x_{k+1} = x_k - \eta(x_k - \xi_k)$. Find the stationary mean and variance of $x_k$ for constant $\eta \in (0, 2)$, explain what this means for convergence, and say what the Robbins-Monro conditions buy.
  *Model answer.* (1) $x_{k+1} = (1 - \eta)x_k + \eta\xi_k$. (2) Taking expectations: $m_{k+1} = (1 - \eta)m_k \to 0$, the minimiser. (3) Variances: $v_{k+1} = (1 - \eta)^2v_k + \eta^2\sigma^2$, since $\xi_k$ is independent of $x_k$. (4) The fixed point is $v = \frac{\eta^2\sigma^2}{1 - (1 - \eta)^2} = \frac{\eta\sigma^2}{2 - \eta}$, reached geometrically because $(1 - \eta)^2 < 1$. (5) So with a constant step the iterates do not converge: they hover in a band of variance about $\frac\eta2\sigma^2$ for small $\eta$ (0.053 for $\eta = 0.1$ and $\sigma = 1$). (6) Steps with $\sum\eta_k = \infty$ (to keep moving far enough) and $\sum\eta_k^2 < \infty$ (to let the injected noise sum to something finite), such as $\eta_k = \frac1k$, give $x_k \to 0$ almost surely; with $\eta_k = \frac1k$ here $x_k$ is exactly the running mean of the $\xi$'s.
  *Rubric (10).* 1: the recursion (line 1). 1: the mean (line 2). 2: the variance recursion with independence used (line 3). 2: the fixed point (line 4). 2: the interpretation (line 5). 2: Robbins-Monro, with the running-mean example (line 6).
- *Example B (d5).* Minimise $\frac12(x - 3)^2 + 2\lvert x\rvert$ by proximal gradient (ISTA) from $x_0 = 0$ with steps $\eta = 1$ and $\eta = 0.5$. Give the iterates and the solution, and explain the rate.
  *Answer.* The proximal step is $x_{k+1} = S_{2\eta}(x_k - \eta(x_k - 3))$ with $S_t(z) = \operatorname{sign}(z)\max(\lvert z\rvert - t, 0)$. With $\eta = 1$: $x_1 = S_2(3) = 1$, and 1 is a fixed point, the solution. With $\eta = 0.5$: $x_1 = S_1(1.5) = 0.5$, $x_2 = S_1(1.75) = 0.75$, $x_3 = S_1(1.875) = 0.875$: $x_{k+1} - 1 = \frac12(x_k - 1)$, linear at rate $1 - \eta = 0.5$. The solution is $S_2(3) = 1$ by the optimality condition $0 \in x - 3 + 2\partial\lvert x\rvert$. Decision point: the smooth part sets the rate exactly as in gradient descent; the non-smooth part costs nothing because its proximal map is in closed form.

**AP1 Portfolio construction** (`set.opt-apps.portfolios`). 10 items (4 d3, 5 d4, 1 d5). Generators `gen.optim.mv-two-asset`, `gen.optim.turnover-trades`, `gen.optim.ac-schedule`. Long-form: a turnover-limited portfolio's dual values, the exit's rehearsal. Code drill `drl.optim.frontier` (d4): `long_only_frontier(mu, Sigma, targets)` returning weights and volatilities, by cvxpy (or SciPy's SLSQP if the `optimisation` group is unavailable). Hidden tests: on a seeded five-asset universe the volatility is non-decreasing in the target above the minimum-variance return; every portfolio is long-only and fully invested to $10^{-8}$; targets above the largest expected return are reported infeasible; five targets match a reference solver to $10^{-6}$. Known-wrong: solving without the long-only constraint and clipping afterwards fails the reference comparison.

- *Example A (long-form, d4).* Three assets have expected returns 9%, 8% and 6%, volatilities 20% and correlations $\rho_{12} = 0.9$, $\rho_{13} = 0.8$, $\rho_{23} = 0.85$; the current weights are $(0.2, 0.3, 0.5)$, risk aversion $\gamma = 4$, and turnover $\sum\lvert w_i - w_i^0\rvert$ is limited to 40%. The optimiser returns $(0.4, 0.3, 0.3)$, with marginal utilities $g_i = \mu_i - \gamma(\Sigma\mathbf w)_i$ of $-0.0556$, $-0.0664$ and $-0.0800$. Explain every constraint's dual value, and predict what happens if the turnover limit is removed.
  *Model answer.* (1) KKT: $g_i = \nu + \alpha_i - \beta_i - \lambda_i$, where $\nu$ prices the budget, $\alpha_i$ and $\beta_i$ the two sides $u_i \ge \pm(w_i - w_i^0)$ of the turnover linearisation, $\lambda_i$ the long-only bound, and $\alpha_i + \beta_i = \theta$, the turnover multiplier. (2) Asset 1 is bought ($\alpha_1 = \theta$, $\beta_1 = 0$) and asset 3 sold ($\beta_3 = \theta$, $\alpha_3 = 0$); every weight is positive, so every $\lambda_i = 0$. (3) Hence $g_1 = \nu + \theta$ and $g_3 = \nu - \theta$: $\theta = \frac{-0.0556 + 0.0800}2 = 0.0122$ and $\nu = -0.0678$. (4) $\theta$ is the utility gained per extra unit of turnover: one unit moves half a unit from asset 3 to asset 1, gaining $\frac12(g_1 - g_3) = \theta$. Raising the limit to 41% adds about $1.2 \times 10^{-4}$ of utility. (5) Asset 2 is untouched because $g_2 = -0.0664$ lies inside $[\nu - \theta, \nu + \theta] = [-0.0800, -0.0556]$: trading it in either direction gains less than the turnover it uses. (6) $\nu$ is the utility of one more unit of capital in the best use available; it is negative because extra invested capital adds more risk penalty than return at $\gamma = 4$. (7) Without the limit the optimum is $(0.8125, 0.1875, 0)$: asset 3's long-only bound binds, with $\lambda_3 = 0.0025$, the amount by which its marginal utility ($-0.0695$) falls short of the held assets' ($-0.0670$).
  *Rubric (10).* 2: the KKT system with every multiplier named (line 1). 1: which constraints bind (line 2). 2: $\theta$ and $\nu$ computed (line 3). 2: $\theta$ interpreted as a price (line 4). 1: the untouched asset (line 5). 1: $\nu$ (line 6). 1: the unconstrained case (line 7).
- *Example B (d5).* Sell 1,000,000 shares over 5 days under Almgren and Chriss's model, whose optimal holdings are $x_j = X\frac{\sinh(\kappa(T - t_j))}{\sinh(\kappa T)}$. Compute the daily sales for $\kappa T = 1$ and for $\kappa T \to 0$, and explain what $\kappa$ measures.
  *Answer.* With $\kappa = 0.2$ a day: holdings $x_j = 10^6\frac{\sinh(0.2(5 - j))}{\sinh1}$, so the sales $x_{j-1} - x_j$ are 244,295, 213,965, 192,223, 178,196 and 171,320.
