As $\kappa T \to 0$, $\sinh$ becomes linear and the sales are 200,000 a day. $\kappa^2$ is proportional to $\frac{\lambda\sigma^2}\eta$: risk aversion times variance over the temporary-impact cost; a trader who fears price risk more than impact sells sooner. Decision point: the even schedule is optimal only for a risk-neutral trader; any aversion to variance front-loads the sales, and $\kappa T$ alone sets how much.

**AP2 Learning as optimisation** (`set.opt-apps.learning`). 8 items (3 d3, 4 d4, 1 d5). Generators `gen.optim.lasso-lambda-max`, `gen.optim.support-vectors`, `gen.optim.reparam-step`. Long-form: the SVM dual. Code drill `drl.optim.svm-dual` (d4): `svm_dual(X, y)` for the hard-margin SVM, returning $\boldsymbol\alpha$, $\mathbf w$ and $b$. Hidden tests: lesson 56's six points give $\boldsymbol\alpha = \left(\frac49, 0, 0, 0, \frac29, \frac29\right)$, $\mathbf w = \left(\frac23, \frac23\right)$ and $b = -\frac53$ to $10^{-6}$; the dual and primal objectives agree ($\frac49$); a non-separable set is reported rather than returning a margin. Known-wrong: omitting $\sum_i\alpha_iy_i = 0$ returns a solution with the wrong $b$.

- *Example A (long-form, d4).* Derive the dual of the hard-margin SVM, $\min\frac12\lVert\mathbf w\rVert^2$ subject to $y_i(\mathbf w^\top\mathbf x_i + b) \ge 1$, and use complementary slackness to find the support vectors and $(\mathbf w, b)$ for lesson 56's six points.
  *Model answer.* (1) $L = \frac12\lVert\mathbf w\rVert^2 - \sum_i\alpha_i[y_i(\mathbf w^\top\mathbf x_i + b) - 1]$ with $\alpha_i \ge 0$. (2) $\nabla_{\mathbf w}L = 0$ gives $\mathbf w = \sum_i\alpha_iy_i\mathbf x_i$; $\frac{\partial L}{\partial b} = 0$ gives $\sum_i\alpha_iy_i = 0$. (3) Substituting: $g(\boldsymbol\alpha) = \sum_i\alpha_i - \frac12\sum_{i,j}\alpha_i\alpha_jy_iy_j\mathbf x_i^\top\mathbf x_j$, maximised over $\boldsymbol\alpha \ge \mathbf 0$ with $\sum\alpha_iy_i = 0$. (4) Complementary slackness: $\alpha_i > 0$ only where $y_i(\mathbf w^\top\mathbf x_i + b) = 1$, the support vectors. (5) The closest pair of classes lies along the diagonal: $(2, 2)$ on one side, $(1, 0)$ and $(0, 1)$ on the other. With $\mathbf w = (a, a)$: $4a + b = 1$ and $a + b = -1$, so $a = \frac23$, $b = -\frac53$. (6) $\mathbf w = \alpha_+(2, 2) - \alpha_a(1, 0) - \alpha_b(0, 1)$ with $\alpha_+ = \alpha_a + \alpha_b$ and symmetry $\alpha_a = \alpha_b$ gives $\alpha_a = \alpha_b = \frac29$, $\alpha_+ = \frac49$; the dual value $\sum\alpha_i - \frac12\lVert\mathbf w\rVert^2 = \frac89 - \frac49 = \frac49$ equals the primal $\frac12\lVert\mathbf w\rVert^2 = \frac49$. Margin width $\frac2{\lVert\mathbf w\rVert} = \frac3{\sqrt2}$.
  *Rubric (10).* 1: the Lagrangian (line 1). 2: the stationarity conditions (line 2). 2: the dual (line 3). 1: complementary slackness (line 4). 2: the support vectors and $(\mathbf w, b)$ (line 5). 2: the $\alpha$'s and the zero gap (line 6).
- *Example B (d5).* With orthonormal-scaled features ($\frac1nX^\top X = I$) and $\frac1nX^\top\mathbf y = (0.2, -0.6, 0.35)$, find the lasso solution (scikit-learn's scaling) at $\lambda = 0.3$, the active set, and verify the KKT conditions.
  *Answer.* With an orthonormal design the problem separates: $\hat\beta_j = S_\lambda(z_j)$ with $z_j = \frac1n\mathbf x_j^\top\mathbf y$. At $\lambda = 0.3$: $(0, -0.3, 0.05)$; active set $\{2, 3\}$. KKT: for $j = 2, 3$, $\frac1n\mathbf x_j^\top(\mathbf y - X\hat{\boldsymbol\beta}) = z_j - \hat\beta_j = -0.3, 0.3$, equal to $\lambda\operatorname{sign}\hat\beta_j$; for $j = 1$, $\lvert z_1\rvert = 0.2 \le 0.3$. $\lambda_{\max} = 0.6$. Decision point: in general designs no closed form exists, but the KKT conditions are the same, and checking them is how any lasso solution is verified.

### 3.3 The Numerics Lab (`set.montecarlo.lab`, 3.5 hours)

Seven drills of 30 minutes, kind `practice`, issued beside the levels named, all on the web app with hidden tests, ungated. They are the code companions of the lessons whose quizzes carry no code (decision 8).

| Drill | After level | Task | Hidden tests (in brief) |
|---|---|---|---|
| `drl.numerics.lab-kahan` | EA2 | Kahan and pairwise summation of a seeded stream of $10^6$ float32 values | Kahan's error below $4u_{32}\sum\lvert x_i\rvert$; pairwise below $\lceil\log_2n\rceil u_{32}\sum\lvert x_i\rvert$; naive exceeds both on the seeded stream |
| `drl.numerics.lab-streaming-vol` | EA2 | A streaming exponentially weighted volatility with Welford-style updates on prices near 10,000 | Matches a two-pass float64 reference to $10^{-10}$ relative; no catastrophic loss in float32 mode on the hidden stream |
| `drl.numerics.lab-brent` | EA3 | Brent's method from a bracket, with evaluation counting | Matches `scipy.optimize.brentq` to $10^{-12}$ on six functions, including a flat root and a function with a pole outside the bracket; never more than twice bisection's evaluations plus 10 |
| `drl.numerics.lab-curve` | EA4 | A discount curve from zero rates by linear $\ln P$, with forwards | Lesson 24's and homework EA4's forwards; positive forwards on a hidden curve; discount factors decreasing |
| `drl.numerics.lab-sparse` | NL2 | Assemble the 2-D Laplacian on a $200 \times 200$ grid in CSR form and solve with `splu` and with preconditioned CG | Both agree to $10^{-8}$; the CSR matrix has $5n - 4\sqrt n$ non-zeros; no dense matrix is formed |
| `drl.numerics.lab-gauss-legendre` | EA5 | Price calls across strikes by Gauss-Legendre above the strike | Within $10^{-8}$ of Black-Scholes at 20 nodes for strikes 50 to 200 |
| `drl.numerics.lab-sobol-asian` | MO4 | Price lesson 85's Asian option by scrambled Sobol with the Brownian bridge, 32 replicates | Standard error below 0.015 at 1,024 points; the step-by-step construction's standard error is at least 3 times larger |

## 4. Topic exam blueprints and the exit tests

### 4.1 The 2.0a paper (`exm.numerics`)

One paper is both the 2.0a topic exam and the in-app gate of `2.0a.exit`. It is of `ASSESSMENT.md`'s `topic` kind as revised (long-form at least 30% of marks, target 40%; at least 30% of marks on applied items with a named trap).

| Property | Value |
|---|---|
| Duration | 90 minutes on one clock |
| Items | 16 items, 80 marks, marked per part |
| Difficulty mix, by marks | 16 at d2, 40 at d3, 16 at d4, 8 at d5 (20/50/20/10), checked per form by CI within 5 percentage points |
| Format mix, by marks | Long-form 32 (four items of 8: 40%); numeric 30; choice 8 (at most 2 marks a part); sort or match 8; expression 2 |
| Applied with a named trap | Q3, Q5, Q8, Q12, Q13, Q15, Q16: 40 marks (50%) |
| Aids | The in-app scientific calculator; no code runs; Q4 and Q8 may show code to read |
| Pass mark | **56 of 80 (70%)**, with **Q3 at 70%** for the exit (`slotMin`) |
| Forms | At least 3 parallel forms; 44 of 80 marks from generator slots; long-form pools of at least 12 items built across methods (a Q15 item may be answered by a control variate, importance sampling or conditioning); slot order shuffled per sitting; no stem names its method; two slots per form replaced by unlabelled slots of the same marks drawn from the whole topic at d3 to d4 (`ASSESSMENT.md` 3.2) |
| Practice | One practice paper of the same shape from the practice pool, never the exam reserve |
| Resit | 3-day cool-down and a remediation set on the failed skills, as for every `topic` paper |
| Help | None: Bertie is away app-wide while the paper runs (`AI-TUTOR.md` 2.12) |

**Slots.**

| Slot | Levels | Content | Format | Difficulty | Marks |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| Q1 | EA1 | The grid: a spacing, a representable integer, or a format's range | N, N | d2 | 4 |
| Q2 | EA1, EA2 | The rounding model: an absorption point, a non-associative sum, or a code fragment's output | C, N | d2 | 4 |
| Q3 | EA2 | **Cancellation, written out** (pool: $1 - \cos x$; one-pass variance; $(1 + r/n)^n$; finite-difference gamma; $\sqrt{x + 1} - \sqrt x$; the small root of a quadratic; $\log(1 + x)$): locate the rounding the subtraction exposes, count the digits lost, give and justify a stable rewrite | Long-form | d3 | 8 |
| Q4 | EA2, NL1 | Conditioning against stability: match four scenarios to "ill-conditioned problem", "unstable algorithm", "both", "neither" | S | d3 | 4 |
| Q5 | EA3 | Root finding: an order from three errors, and the safeguard decision for a proposed Newton step | N, C | d3 | 4 |
| Q6 | EA4 | Interpolation: Runge or Chebyshev, or the forward rates of a curve scheme | C, N | d2 | 4 |
| Q7 | EA5 | Quadrature: an error ratio on halving, or a Richardson combination | N, N | d3 | 4 |
| Q8 | NL1 | Residual against error, or digits kept for a given $\kappa$ | N, N | d3 | 4 |
| Q9 | NL2, NL3 | A valid correlation range, a structure-to-solver match, or an eigenvalue's sensitivity | S | d3 | 4 |
| Q10 | NL4 | Iteration counts for Jacobi, SOR or CG from a spectral radius or $\kappa$ | N | d3 | 4 |
| Q11 | PD1 | **Derivation, written out** (pool: the heat equation from a random walk; the Black-Scholes equation from a tree; the change of variables to the heat equation; a separation-of-variables solution) | Long-form | d4 | 8 |
| Q12 | PD2 | The largest stable explicit step, or the implicit scheme's amplification factor | N, X | d2 | 4 |
| Q13 | PD2, PD3 | **Stability and its artefacts on a pricing grid, written out** (pool: von Neumann for the θ-scheme applied to a pricing grid's step; Crank-Nicolson's high modes, gamma and Rannacher; why an unstable explicit pricing run looks fine for 400 steps; American options by PSOR against projection); every pool item combines a stability argument with a pricing grid | Long-form | d4 | 8 |
| Q14 | MO1, MO2 | Paths for a target error, an efficiency comparison, or a rejection rate | N, N | d3 | 4 |
| Q15 | MO3 | **Variance reduction, designed and justified** (pool: a control variate and its factor; antithetics by the even and odd parts; an importance-sampling shift with its weight; stratification of the terminal value) | Long-form | d5 | 8 |
| Q16 | MO4, MO5 | Quasi-Monte Carlo against Monte Carlo, or a Greek estimator's validity and noise | C, N | d3 | 4 |

**Totals.** Marks 80. By difficulty: d2 $4 + 4 + 4 + 4 = 16$; d3 $8 + 4 \times 8 = 40$ (Q3, Q4, Q5, Q7, Q8, Q9, Q10, Q14, Q16); d4 $8 + 8 = 16$; d5 8. Long-form 32. Generator slots (Q1, Q2, Q5 to Q10, Q12, Q14, Q16) 44.

**Coverage.** Every form tests at least 14 of the 17 levels (82%, above `ASSESSMENT.md`'s 80% rule): EA1 to EA5; NL1, NL4 and one of NL2 and NL3; PD1 to PD3; one of MO1 and MO2; MO3; and one of MO4 and MO5. Across forms every level appears. By course, marks run EA 32, NL 12, PD 20, MO 16, against lesson-hour shares of 33%, 20%, 19% and 29%: EA is weighted for the exit's cancellation slot, and MO is under-weighted because the exit's four-pricer drill tests it at length.

**Worked example of one slot (Q13, d4).** "For $u_\tau = u_{xx}$ on $[0, 1]$ with zero boundary values, $\Delta x = 0.01$ and $\Delta\tau = 0.005$, find $r$, the Crank-Nicolson amplification factors of the lowest and highest grid modes, and the exact decay factors over one step. Explain what a learner pricing a call on such a grid would see in gamma near the strike, and how to fix it." Model answer: $r = \frac{0.005}{0.0001} = 50$. Lowest mode $k = \pi$: $s = \sin(0.005\pi)$, $s^2 = 2.467 \times 10^{-4}$, $g = \frac{1 - 0.02467}{1 + 0.02467} = 0.95184$, against the exact $e^{-\pi^2 \times 0.005} = 0.95185$: excellent. Highest mode $k = 99\pi$: $s^2 = 0.99975$, $g = \frac{1 - 99.975}{1 + 99.975} = -0.9802$, against the exact $e^{-(99\pi)^2 \times 0.005} \approx 10^{-210}$: the mode survives and flips sign each step. A payoff's kink loads the high modes, gamma (a second difference) amplifies them, so gamma alternates from node to node near the strike in the first steps. Fix: two to four implicit Euler steps at the start ($g = \frac1{1 + 4rs^2} \approx 0.005$ for the highest mode), which keeps second order overall. Rubric (8): 1 for $r$; 2 for the lowest mode with its comparison; 2 for the highest mode with its comparison; 2 for the gamma explanation; 1 for the fix with its factor. CI recomputes every number and checks each line (section 6).

### 4.2 The 2.0a exit, rebuilt in the app

Canon-gap G1's exit line: "Price a European call four ways (closed form, binomial tree, Crank-Nicolson, Monte Carlo with a control variate) and plot error against work on log-log axes, reading off the orders: $O(n^{-1})$ for the tree, $O(\Delta t^2 + \Delta S^2)$ for Crank-Nicolson, $O(N^{-1/2})$ for Monte Carlo. Compute implied volatility across a strike grid with a safeguarded Newton method and no failures. Explain one cancellation failure and its fix."

| Part | Exit line | Gate (in the app) | Confirm | Taught in | Runs on |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| `2.0a.exit` | The three tasks above | `exm.numerics` at 70% with Q3 (cancellation, written out) at 70%; `drl.numerics.exit-four-pricers` and `drl.numerics.exit-iv-grid`, every hidden test passing | None outside the app: the drills are the learner's own code against hidden tests, and canon-gap names no external paper for this item. The MIT 18.335J problem sets (public solutions) are practice, not a confirm | EA2, EA3, PD2, PD3, MO2, MO3, MO5 | Paper: both targets. Drills: the web app (owner decision 5) |

**`drl.numerics.exit-four-pricers`** (5 h). The learner writes `price_tree(N)`, `price_cn(N)` (with $N$ price steps and $N$ time steps, $S_{\max} = 4K$, the strike on a node, Rannacher start), `price_mc(N, seed)` (exact terminal simulation with $e^{-rT}S_T$ as control) and `error_vs_work()`, for a call whose parameters the hidden tests draw from a fixed reserve (the rehearsal uses $S = K = 100$, $r = 5\%$, $\sigma = 20\%$, $T = 1$). Hidden tests: each pricer within its stated error of the closed form at three sizes; the fitted log-log slope of $\lvert$error$\rvert$ against the method's own parameter lies in $[-1.15, -0.85]$ for the tree (after averaging $N$ and $N + 1$ to remove the odd-even wobble), $[-2.2, -1.8]$ for Crank-Nicolson, and $[-0.6, -0.4]$ for Monte Carlo's standard error; the slopes against work (nodes or paths) lie in $[-0.6, -0.4]$, $[-1.1, -0.9]$ and $[-0.6, -0.4]$; the plot file is produced with labelled log-log axes; a one-paragraph note names which method wins at an error of $10^{-4}$ and why. Rehearsed in lessons 59, 65 and 90.

**`drl.numerics.exit-iv-grid`** (2 h). `implied_vol_surface(prices, S, strikes, expiries, r)` on a hidden grid of 31 strikes from 50% to 200% of spot and 6 expiries from one week to two years, including deep out-of-the-money quotes, a quote below intrinsic value and a quote above the upper bound. Hidden tests: every valid quote's round trip within $10^{-8}$ in volatility wherever the price exceeds $10^{-10}S$; invalid quotes flagged, never assigned a number; no `nan`, no exception, no more than 50 evaluations per quote. Rehearsed in lesson 18 and homework EA3.

```ts
// exit data for 2.0a, using ASSESSMENT.md 2.10's ExitPart
{ id: '2.0a.exit',
  text: 'Price a European call four ways with error against work and orders read off; implied volatility across a strike grid with no failures; explain one cancellation failure and its fix',
  gate: [ { kind: 'exam', exam: 'exm.numerics', pass: 0.7, slotMin: { q3: 0.7 } },
          { kind: 'drill', drills: ['drl.numerics.exit-four-pricers', 'drl.numerics.exit-iv-grid'] } ],
  confirm: [],
  estHours: 8 }   // drills 7 h, review of the paper 1 h; the paper's 1.5 h is its own estimate
```

**Order and test-out.** A learner may sit `exm.numerics` and the two drills before the lessons to test out (`ASSESSMENT.md` 7.3); a pass credits the topic's lesson hours as tested out. The drills can be resubmitted after a fix; the paper follows the `topic` kind's resit rule.

**Totals.** d2 $4 + 4 + 4 + 4 = 16$ (Q1, Q2, Q6, Q8); d3 $8 + 4 \times 8 = 40$ (Q3, Q4, Q5, Q9, Q10, Q11, Q13, Q15, Q16); d4 16 (Q7, Q12); d5 8 (Q14). Every form covers all 10 levels. By course: LP 24, CX 24, DA 16, AP 16 (Q16 counted with AP).

**Worked example of one slot (Q3, d3).** "Maximise $3x + 2y$ subject to $x + y \le 4$, $x + 3y \le 6$, $x \le 3.5$, $x, y \ge 0$. Derive the dual, prove weak duality for this pair, and verify that $(3.5, 0.5)$ and a dual solution you find certify each other." Model answer: dual $\min 4u_1 + 6u_2 + 3.5u_3$ subject to $u_1 + u_2 + u_3 \ge 3$, $u_1 + 3u_2 \ge 2$, $\mathbf u \ge \mathbf 0$. Weak duality: for feasible $(x, y)$ and $\mathbf u$, $3x + 2y \le (u_1 + u_2 + u_3)x + (u_1 + 3u_2)y = u_1(x + y) + u_2(x + 3y) + u_3x \le 4u_1 + 6u_2 + 3.5u_3$. At $(3.5, 0.5)$ the second constraint is slack ($3.5 + 1.5 = 5 < 6$), so $u_2 = 0$; both primal variables are positive, so both dual constraints bind: $u_1 + u_3 = 3$, $u_1 = 2$, giving $\mathbf u = (2, 0, 1)$. Values: primal $10.5 + 1 = 11.5$; dual $8 + 3.5 = 11.5$. Equal values certify both. Rubric (8): 2 for the dual; 2 for weak duality with the sign conditions used; 2 for complementary slackness giving $\mathbf u$; 1 for both values; 1 for the certificate statement.

### 4.4 The 2.0b exit, rebuilt in the app

Canon-gap G2's exit line: "Derive the dual of a given LP and confirm strong duality numerically; decide and prove the convexity of three functions; implement gradient descent and Newton's method and show linear against quadratic convergence; build a long-only mean-variance portfolio with a turnover limit and explain each constraint's dual value."

| Part | Exit line | Gate (in the app) | Confirm | Taught in | Runs on |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| `2.0b.exit` | The four tasks above | `exm.optim` at 70% with Q3 (duality) and Q7 (convexity) at 70%; three convexity proofs from the exit pool at 7 of 10 each; `drl.optim.exit-duality`, `drl.optim.exit-convergence` and `drl.optim.exit-portfolio` passing; the portfolio's dual-value note at 7 of 10 | None outside the app (no public solutions exist for the canonical problem source) | LP3, CX2, DA1, AP1 | Paper and proofs: both targets. Drills: the web app |

**The three convexity proofs** (`itm.convex.exit-*`, a pool of 18 functions disjoint from the paper's Q7 pool, three drawn per sitting with at least one non-convex): long-form, rubric of 10, marked by the gate rule $\min(\text{learner}, \text{Bertie})$ per point (`ASSESSMENT.md` decision 6).

**`drl.optim.exit-duality`** (1.5 h). Given a hidden LP in inequality form with 4 to 6 variables, the learner writes its dual by hand in a structured answer (checked symbolically against the reference dual), then solves both with `linprog` and reports the gap and the complementary-slackness products. Hidden tests: the dual's data match; the gap is below $10^{-8}$; every slackness product is below $10^{-8}$.

**`drl.optim.exit-convergence`** (1.5 h). Gradient descent with backtracking and damped Newton on a hidden strongly convex problem (logistic regression with a small ridge term). Hidden tests: Newton reaches gradient norm $10^{-10}$ within 10 iterations; the ratio $\frac{e_{k+1}}{e_k}$ of gradient descent is within 0.05 of constant over its last 20 iterations; Newton's $\frac{e_{k+1}}{e_k^2}$ is bounded; a semilog plot of both histories is produced; the learner's sentence names the observed rate of each.

**`drl.optim.exit-portfolio`** (3 h, `optimisation` group). A long-only, fully invested mean-variance portfolio of a hidden 8-asset universe, with risk aversion $\gamma$, current weights and a turnover limit, in cvxpy. Hidden tests: weights match a reference to $10^{-6}$; every constraint's dual value matches to $10^{-5}$; the turnover multiplier equals half the gap between the marginal utilities of a bought and a sold asset to $10^{-6}$ (the KKT identity of lesson 52); re-solving with the limit raised by 1% changes the utility by the multiplier times 0.01 to within $10^{-6}$. Then a long-form note (`itm.opt-apps.exit-duals-note`, 10-point rubric) explains each constraint's dual value in words, as homework AP1's example A did.

```ts
// exit data for 2.0b, using ASSESSMENT.md 2.10's ExitPart
{ id: '2.0b.exit',
  text: 'Derive an LP dual and confirm strong duality; prove the convexity of three functions; show linear against quadratic convergence; build a long-only mean-variance portfolio with a turnover limit and explain every dual value',
  gate: [ { kind: 'exam', exam: 'exm.optim', pass: 0.7, slotMin: { q3: 0.7, q7: 0.7 } },
          { kind: 'rubric', items: ['itm.convex.exit-convexity'], minPoints: 7, count: 3 },        // a pool id; three drawn per sitting
          { kind: 'drill', drills: ['drl.optim.exit-duality', 'drl.optim.exit-convergence', 'drl.optim.exit-portfolio'] },
          { kind: 'rubric', items: ['itm.opt-apps.exit-duals-note'], minPoints: 7 } ],
  confirm: [],
  estHours: 8.5 }   // proofs 1.5 h, drills 6 h (the note inside the portfolio's 3 h), review of the paper 1 h
```

The `rubric` instrument names items, and the exit pool is 18 items; this file writes it as one pool id. `ASSESSMENT.md` 2.10 should accept a pool reference with `count` (section 0.5, request 7).

## 5. Reading and resource map per level

### 5.0 How to use this map

- **One spine per course, plus up to three resources in fixed roles** (canon-gap decision 1): lectures (`parallel-reading`), problems (`problem-inspired`), stretch (`further`). Spines (decision 5): Süli and Mayers (`src.suli-mayers`) for course EA and the stability parts of PD; Trefethen and Bau (`src.trefethen-bau`) for NL and for conditioning and stability in EA2; Wilmott, Howison and Dewynne (`src.wilmott-howison-dewynne`) for PD; Glasserman (`src.glasserman-mc`) for MO; Vanderbei (`src.vanderbei-lp-5e`, role changed to `basis`, request 1) for LP; Boyd and Vandenberghe (`src.boyd-cvx`) for CX, DA and AP.
- **Lectures and problems.** MIT 18.335J Introduction to Numerical Methods (Spring 2019, `src.mit-18335j`) is 2.0a's lecture and problem parallel: notes, problem sets and exams with solutions on OpenCourseWare [searched by canon-gap]; it follows Trefethen and Bau, so its problem sets sit with course NL and level EA2. Stanford EE364A (`src.stanford-ee364a`) is 2.0b's: Boyd's recorded lectures follow the book chapter by chapter [recalled]; they are **pull-based** (watched when a level review falls below 70%, time taken from the Redo budget). Boyd and Vandenberghe's *Additional Exercises* (`src.boyd-cvx-exercises`) have no public solutions [unverified], so every 2.0b solution in this file was written and checked by the app (canon-gap decision 7).
- **Problems over reading** (curriculum rule 2). Every row says what to do. Reading is budgeted at about 2 minutes a page for the spines' dense sections; external problems at 15 minutes each.
- **Editions** as canon-gap pins them; chapter titles are [recalled] and chapter numbers are marked [check] where I am not certain of them. Section numbers inside chapters are cited only where I am confident; otherwise a row names the chapter and the topic.
- **Held back.** None: no 2.0a or 2.0b resource is reserved for an exit, because the exits are the learner's own code and the in-app papers.

**Links** (free unless stated). Boyd and Vandenberghe, *Convex Optimization* (CUP 2004), free PDF and the *Additional Exercises*: https://web.stanford.edu/~boyd/cvxbook/. Stanford EE364A: https://web.stanford.edu/class/ee364a/ [searched: the path serves the course's lecture slides]. MIT 18.335J (Spring 2019): https://ocw.mit.edu/courses/18-335j-introduction-to-numerical-methods-spring-2019/ [searched: four problem sets with solutions, a take-home midterm and a final project]. Goldberg (1991), *ACM Computing Surveys* 23(1), 5 to 48, stable address https://doi.org/10.1145/103162.103163 [recalled]; free copies on several sites. Shewchuk, "An Introduction to the Conjugate Gradient Method Without the Agonizing Pain" (Carnegie Mellon technical report CMU-CS-94-125, 1994), free PDF at https://www.cs.cmu.edu/~quake-papers/painless-conjugate-gradient.pdf [recalled; not reachable from this environment]. Owen, *Monte Carlo Theory, Methods and Examples* (online book in progress), https://artowen.su.domains/mc/ [check]. Bottou, Curtis and Nocedal, "Optimization Methods for Large-Scale Machine Learning", *SIAM Review* 60(2), 2018, also arXiv:1606.04838. Paid books: Süli and Mayers (CUP 2003); Trefethen and Bau (SIAM 1997); Wilmott, Howison and Dewynne (CUP 1995); Glasserman (Springer 2003); Vanderbei, 5th ed (Springer 2020); Nocedal and Wright, 2nd ed (Springer 2006); Cornuéjols, Peña and Tütüncü, 2nd ed (CUP 2018); Higham, *Accuracy and Stability of Numerical Algorithms*, 2nd ed (SIAM 2002); Trefethen, *Approximation Theory and Approximation Practice*, extended edition (SIAM 2019, ISBN 978-1-611975-93-2) [searched].

## 6. Verification approach

Every auto-marked item has two independent implementations of its answer (`CONTENT-FORMAT.md` 6.2): the TypeScript generator (or the author's declared answer) that builds it, and a Python verifier written from the generator's plain-language `semantics` without reading the TypeScript, run in CI under `tools/zqverify` on CPython 3.12 with the versions pinned in `tools/py/uv.lock` (NumPy, SciPy, SymPy, mpmath; cvxpy, Clarabel and highspy for 2.0b). CI also replays every listed distractor through the runtime grader and requires it to be marked wrong with its intended diagnosis. Numerics and optimisation add five rules of their own [our choice]:

1. **Reference values in exact or high precision.** Every "true value" against which an error is stated is computed in exact rational arithmetic (`fractions`, SymPy) or with mpmath at 50 digits, never by the method under study. A hook that says "error $2.3 \times 10^{-9}$" is checked by recomputing both the method's output and the reference.
2. **IEEE results are checked in two engines.** Every floating-point output an item shows or grades (a sum, a cancellation, an absorption point, a float32 or float16 rounding) is computed in CPython or NumPy and in the app's JavaScript (`@zq/mathcore`, with `Math.fround` for float32 and an integer-bit implementation for float16 and bfloat16), and the two must agree bit for bit. Results that depend on a transcendental function's last bit fail CI unless both engines and mpmath's correctly rounded value agree (decision 9).
3. **Orders are measured, not asserted.** Every claimed order of convergence (a quadrature rule, a scheme, a tree, a Monte Carlo estimator, an optimiser) is checked by a least-squares slope over at least four refinements, within a stated band ($\pm0.15$ for orders up to 2); odd-even effects are removed by averaging neighbouring sizes before fitting, and the band is stated on the item.
4. **Two methods where two exist.** A price on a grid is checked against the closed form and a tree; an LP's optimum against its dual and against vertex enumeration at small sizes; a QP's solution against its KKT system solved on the active set and against a second solver (Clarabel and HiGHS); a convexity claim against a randomised midpoint and Hessian search (no counterexample in $10^5$ trials for "convex"; a stored counterexample for "not convex").
5. **Simulations never grade.** Monte Carlo screens grade predictions or exact quantities (decision 10). CI runs each displayed simulation with its fixed seed and requires every displayed statistic to lie within 4 standard errors of its exact value, so no seed shows a misleading run; variance ratios quoted as numbers (for example 0.50 and 1.65 for antithetics) are labelled as estimates from a stated number of seeded draws.

| Family | Items | CI: second implementation | Runtime check | Traps the check must catch |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Floating-point grid and rounding | EA1 | Bit patterns from `struct` against `@zq/mathcore`; spacings from `math.ulp` and `numpy.spacing`; float16 from NumPy; bfloat16 by round-to-nearest-even on float32 bits | `numeric` (exact powers of 2 accepted in any form), `choice` | Spacing taken as constant; $u$ confused with machine epsilon; range confused with precision |
| Conditioning, backward error, cancellation | EA2 | Condition numbers symbolically (SymPy) and by finite perturbation in mpmath; cancellation outputs in both engines; digit counts from mpmath references | `numeric` with stated tolerance on digit counts (±0.5) | Blaming the subtraction; algebraically equal rewrites assumed equal numerically |
| Root finding | EA3 | Iterates replayed in both engines; orders fitted from the sequence; roots by mpmath `findroot` at 50 digits; basins by a fine sweep of starting points | `numeric`, `point` | A stopping rule below one ulp; Newton assumed global; price tolerance taken for volatility tolerance |
| Interpolation | EA4 | Interpolants by SciPy (barycentric, `CubicSpline`) against an mpmath Lagrange evaluation; maximum errors on grids of $2 \times 10^5$ points; forward rates by exact rational arithmetic on the zero rates | `numeric`, `curve` | Monomial coefficients trusted; linear zeros assumed to give smooth forwards |
| Quadrature | EA5 | Each rule against mpmath `quad` at 50 digits; error ratios from four refinements; Gauss nodes from `numpy.polynomial` against SymPy roots of the Legendre polynomial | `numeric` | Orders assumed for non-smooth integrands; an adaptive routine's estimate taken as a guarantee |
| Dense linear systems | NL1 | Solutions in exact rationals for small $n$ (SymPy) and in mpmath at 50 digits for $n \le 60$; growth factors from the computed $U$; refinement replayed with float32 factors | `numeric`, `matrix` | Residual taken for error; pivoting assumed to guarantee stability |
| Structured and sparse | NL2 | Correlation ranges by exact determinant conditions (SymPy); nearest correlation by Higham's method against an SDP solve in cvxpy; fill-in counts from `splu` with fixed orderings | `numeric`, `interval`, `matrix` | Entrywise-valid matrices taken as valid; clipping taken as projection |
| Eigenvalues and the SVD | NL3 | Eigenvalues by `eigh` against mpmath at 50 digits; sensitivity by perturbation; iteration counts from the stated rates and from replay | `numeric` | Eigenvectors assumed stable at a small gap; the SVD from $A^\top A$ |
| Iterative solvers | NL4 | Spectral radii exactly for the model problem ($\cos\frac\pi{n+1}$) and numerically otherwise; iteration counts from the rate formulas against replayed runs; CG in exact rational arithmetic for the distinct-eigenvalue claims | `numeric` | Convergence assumed for any matrix; CG taken as an $n$-step direct method in floating point |
| PDE derivations | PD1 | Every derivation line checked by SymPy (the Taylor expansion of the tree's recursion, the change of variables to the heat equation, separation of variables) | Long-form self-marking with Bertie's suggestion | The real drift kept; the time direction reversed |
| Time stepping and stability | PD2 | Amplification factors symbolically; stability bounds by SymPy inequalities; growth replayed in both engines from a fixed rounding seed for the "fails late" claims | `numeric`, `expression` | Instability expected at once; stability taken for accuracy |
| Grid pricing | PD3 | Crank-Nicolson and implicit grids against Black-Scholes (mpmath) for Europeans, against a 4,000-step tree with odd-even averaging for Americans, and against a fine-grid reference for barriers; strike placement fixed by construction | `numeric` | Nearest-node reading; a moving strike position breaking the order |
| Random numbers | MO1 | LCG relations proved symbolically (SymPy) and checked on $10^6$ outputs; inverse-transform limits from `scipy.special.ndtri` against mpmath; acceptance rates exactly | `numeric`, `choice` | Looks random taken as random; sequential seeds taken as independent |
| Estimators, bias and sizing | MO2 | Sample sizes and efficiency ratios by formula; biases of Euler schemes against $10^7$-path seeded runs with stated standard errors; strong errors from paired paths | `numeric` | More paths assumed to remove bias; variance compared per path |
| Variance reduction | MO3 | Optimal coefficients and variance factors by exact integrals where they exist (the control-variate correlation of a call with $S_T$ by mpmath quadrature), otherwise by $10^7$ seeded draws with stated error; importance-sampling second moments in closed form | `numeric` | Any proposal assumed safe; a simulated control mean |
| Quasi-Monte Carlo | MO4 | Sequences by SciPy's `qmc` against an independent implementation (radical inverse, Joe and Kuo direction numbers); randomised standard errors from 32 or more replicates | `numeric` | QMC treated as random; one run given an error bar |
| Sensitivities and early exercise | MO5 | Greeks against closed forms (mpmath); Longstaff and Schwartz against a 5,000-step Bermudan tree, priced on fresh paths | `numeric` | Pathwise used on discontinuous payoffs; in-sample exercise rules |
| Linear programmes | LP1 to LP3 | HiGHS (simplex and interior point) against vertex enumeration in exact rationals for $n \le 6$; duals from the solver against duals from complementary slackness solved exactly; shadow-price ranges by re-solving at the range ends | `numeric`, `interval`, `matrix`, `region` | Rounding an LP solution; duals with the wrong sign; ranges assumed unbounded |
| Convex sets and functions | CX1, CX2 | Convexity by the randomised search of rule 4; Hessians symbolically; projections against a QP solve | `choice`, `multi`, long-form | Separate convexity taken as joint; compositions taken as convex |
| KKT and duality | CX3 | KKT systems solved exactly on the guessed active set (SymPy), checked for dual feasibility, and against cvxpy and highspy; dual functions symbolically | `numeric`, `matrix`, `expression` | A zero gradient on a constrained problem; clipping weights; a gap assumed zero without Slater |
| Descent methods | DA1, DA2 | Rates from formulas against replayed runs in both engines; iteration counts from fitted slopes; SGD noise variance by formula against $10^6$ seeded steps | `numeric`, `point` | Linear rates claimed on non-strongly-convex functions; constant-step SGD claimed to converge |
| Portfolios and execution | AP1 | Weights and every dual against two solvers and against the KKT identities of lesson 52; Almgren and Chriss schedules from the closed form against the QP solved directly | `numeric`, `matrix` | Scaled trades; zero-dual constraints declared removable |
| Learning problems | AP2 | Lasso solutions by coordinate descent against cvxpy with KKT residuals below $10^{-8}$; SVM duals in exact rationals for the six-point example | `numeric`, `set` | Lasso zeros attributed to rounding; every point treated as a support vector |
| Long-form items | every set and both papers | CI checks each model-answer line with SymPy (each equation equivalent to the last under the stated assumptions), recomputes every number, and checks that each rubric point names lines that exist (`CONTENT-FORMAT.md` 6.8) | Blind self-marking against the rubric, then Bertie's suggested mark and evidence quote (`AI-TUTOR.md` 2.7); gates count $\min(\text{learner}, \text{Bertie})$ | Rubric points no line evidences; a line valid only for smooth data used at a kink |
| Code drills | every set, the Lab, both exits | `zqtest` suites run against `reference.py` on CPython 3.12 and Pyodide; every `wrong/*.py` must fail the test it names; timing tests have a 3-fold margin on the CI proxy | Visible examples on Run; hidden tests at submission; the first failing test mapped to a diagnosis | Unseeded randomness; float equality; tolerances tighter than the method's order allows |
| The papers | `exm.numerics`, `exm.optim` | `ASSESSMENT.md` 3.2's rules on every form: marks, the 20/50/20/10 mix within 5 points, at least 80% of levels covered, at least 50% of marks from generators, 40% long-form, reserve capacity for six fresh sittings | Exam engine (`ASSESSMENT.md` 3) | A form that drifts off the mix |

**What verification cannot prove** (`CONTENT-FORMAT.md` 6.13): that a hook is engaging, that a trap catches the misconception it targets, that a rubric rewards understanding rather than vocabulary, or that a stated "practice" (desks interpolate log discount factors; banks run quasi-Monte Carlo overnight) is current at a given firm. Practice claims are worded as typical, cite a source where one exists, and are reviewed by the owner's outside reviewer (`docs/PLAN.md` 12 decision 10); the rest is judged by item statistics after release (a trap fewer than 30% of learners fall into, or a distractor nobody picks, is flagged for rewriting).

---

## 7. Sample lesson in full: "Catastrophic cancellation"

**Why this lesson.** Decision 15: cancellation is the third task of the 2.0a exit and the failure behind most of the item's later rescues (stable rewrites in lesson 10, Welford in lesson 12, the normal equations in 1.4 lesson 48 seen again in lesson 43, finite-difference Greeks in lessons 62 and 86). It is also the first place in the path where a learner's formula is right and its number is wrong, so it carries the item's central distinction: the error was made earlier than where it shows. 1.3 lesson 27 met the trade-off between truncation and round-off for a first derivative; 0.1 lesson 42 met $0.1 + 0.2 \ne 0.3$. This lesson explains why a subtraction can turn a rounding error of $10^{-17}$ into a wrong answer, and how to stop it.

| Field | Value |
|---|---|
| Id | `lsn.approx.cancellation` (course EA, level EA2, lesson 9 of 2.0a) |
| Length | 10 screens (1 title, 1 summary, 8 tasks) and a quiz of 5 items; about 11 minutes of screens and 4 of quiz |
| Requires | 2.0a lessons 1, 2, 6, 7 and 8 (the grid, the rounding model, measuring error, condition numbers, backward error); 0.3 level T4 (compound-angle identities); 1.3 lesson 27 (finite differences) and lesson 99 (Taylor polynomials) |
| Introduces | `skl.numerics.cancellation-diagnosis`, `skl.numerics.stable-rewrite` (level grain, at most 2); step tags `sterbenz-exact-subtraction`, `digits-lost-estimate`, `representation-error-exposed` |
| Frame (why it exists) | Every quant formula with a difference in it, from a variance to a bumped Greek to a log return, will one day subtract two nearly equal numbers. Without a way to see where the digits went, a correct formula that prints garbage looks like a bug in the computer, and the fix people reach for (more precision, a smaller step) usually makes it worse |
| Insight (the aha line) | Subtraction does not create error; it reveals the error its inputs already carried. Rounding $\cos x$ to 16 digits costs almost nothing, until you subtract it from 1 and the leading digits cancel, leaving the rounding error as the answer. So remove the subtraction, not the rounding |
| Misconceptions | `mc.numerics.computer-exact` (the bait of screen 2), `mc.numerics.last-digit-only`, `mc.numerics.nan-on-small`, `mc.numerics.double-means-16`, `mc.numerics.halve-digits`, `mc.numerics.subtraction-inexact` (the trap of screen 5), `mc.numerics.division-loses`, `mc.numerics.squaring-loses`, `mc.numerics.more-precision-fixes`, `mc.numerics.smaller-step-better`, `mc.numerics.exact-inputs-lose`, `mc.numerics.decimal-literal-exact`, `mc.numerics.precision-reduction-fix` |
| Worked example with a decision point | Screen 7: three candidate fixes (more precision for the subtraction, a different $x$, an identity); the first two fail and point to the third, $1 - \cos x = 2\sin^2\frac x2$ |
| Trap and edge cases | Trap: screen 5 (bait: "the subtraction is where the error is made"). Edges: screen 8 (subtracting exact inputs is harmless; a decimal literal is not an exact input), screen 6 (the digit-loss rule is an estimate, off by up to a digit) |
| Transfer | Screen 9: gamma by bumping, where the best bump is about $10^{-2}$ and $10^{-6}$ gives a 52% error |
| Sources | Goldberg (1991), the section on cancellation; Trefethen and Bau, lecture 13; Higham, *Accuracy and Stability*, chapter 1, the section on cancellation [check section number]; Sterbenz, *Floating-Point Computation* (Prentice-Hall 1974), for the exact-subtraction lemma [recalled] |
| Lesson-wide tutor guidance | Do not say "cancellation", "Sterbenz" or "rewrite" before screen 3's Continue; never state 0, 8 or $2\sin^2\frac x2$ before the screen that asks for them; prefer "which number on this screen was rounded first?" and "how many leading digits do $1$ and $\cos x$ share?" to statements |

Throughout, numbers are shown with their exact decimal expansions where the point depends on them (`float-inspector` renders the double nearest a value as a full decimal), and digits that agree between two numbers are underlined as well as coloured, so the lesson does not depend on colour vision (design research 11).

---

**Screen 1. Title** (`title`)

- **Heading:** When subtraction destroys digits
- **Prose:** "A formula can be exactly right and still print a wrong number. Today we find out where the digits go."
- **Art:** Bertie at a desk calculator whose display reads 0, his fuzzy hair tufts lifted in surprise.
- **Action:** Continue.

---

**Screen 2. The hook** (`problem`, id `hook`)

- **Prose:** "A risk library computes $f(x) = \frac{1 - \cos x}{x^2}$. By Taylor's theorem (1.3 lesson 99), $\cos x = 1 - \frac{x^2}2 + \frac{x^4}{24} - \cdots$, so $f(x) \to \frac12$ as $x \to 0$. The library is asked for $f(10^{-8})$."
- **Widget:** `predict-output` showing three lines of Python (`import math`, `x = 1e-8`, `print((1 - math.cos(x)) / x**2)`), with the output recorded at build time and hidden until the learner commits.
- **Question:** "What does it print?" (`choice`)

| Option | Correct? | Misconception | Feedback |
|---|---|---|---|
| A. 0.5, the limit (the bait) | no | `mc.numerics.computer-exact` | "That is what the mathematics says. Run it and see what the arithmetic says." |
| B. 0.49999999999999994, one step below 0.5 | no | `mc.numerics.last-digit-only` | "A sensible guess if rounding only ever touches the last digit. Here it touched all of them." |
| C. 0.0 | yes | | "Yes. Every digit is wrong. The rest of the lesson finds out why, and how to fix it." |
| D. An error, because $x$ is so small | no | `mc.numerics.nan-on-small` | "$10^{-8}$ is far inside the range of doubles (lesson 3), and nothing here divides by zero." |

- **Hints.** Nudge: "What is $\cos(10^{-8})$, to 20 decimal places?" Point: highlights `math.cos(x)` in the code. Step: "$\cos(10^{-8}) = 0.99999999999999995\ldots$. The doubles nearest 1 are 1 and $1 - 1.1 \times 10^{-16}$. Which is closer?"
- **Why?** Shown: "`math.cos(1e-8)` returns exactly 1.0, because the true value lies within half a spacing of 1. Then $1 - 1 = 0$." Then: "The cosine was correct to 16 digits. The subtraction kept none of them."
- **Tutor.** Focus: commit to a prediction before the reveal. Notes: most learners choose A or B. Avoid: explaining before Check.

---

**Screen 3. Slide $x$ down** (`explore`, id `slide`)

- **Prose:** "Slide $x$ from $10^{-1}$ down to $10^{-8}$. Watch the stored value of $\cos x$, the computed $1 - \cos x$, and how many digits of $f(x)$ survive."
- **Widget:** `float-inspector` in cancellation mode: a `slider` over $x \in \{10^{-1}, \dots, 10^{-8}\}$; a panel showing $\operatorname{fl}(\cos x)$ in full decimal with the digits it shares with 1 underlined; the computed and true $1 - \cos x$ (the true value precomputed at build time in 50-digit arithmetic); the computed $f(x)$ against $\frac12 - \frac{x^2}{24}$; a "correct digits" readout and a small bar chart of digits kept against $x$, filled in as the learner visits values.
- **Continue when:** at least four values of $x$ visited, including $10^{-4}$ (predicate `visited(x, 4)` and `visited(x, 1e-4)`).
- **Tutor.** Focus: the pattern, about two digits lost per decade of $x$. Notes: some learners slide straight to $10^{-8}$; ask them what happened on the way.

---

**Screen 4. Count the damage** (`ladder`, id `count`, constraint "Read off the digits at $x = 10^{-4}$")

- **Widget:** the same canvas, slider locked at $10^{-4}$ (`from: slide`). Shown: computed $f = 0.4999999969612645$; true $f = 0.49999999958333333$.
- **Question:** "How many significant digits of the computed $f(10^{-4})$ are correct, to the nearest whole number?"
- **Answer input:** `numeric-input`.
- **Correct:** 8 (the relative error is $5.2 \times 10^{-9}$; $-\log_{10}$ of it is 8.3).

| Wrong answer | Misconception | Feedback |
|---|---|---|
| 16 | `mc.numerics.double-means-16` | "A double can hold 16 digits, but compare: 0.49999999**69**... against 0.49999999**58**... They agree to 8 digits." |
| 4 or 5 | `mc.numerics.halve-digits` | "Count the agreeing digits from the left: 4999999 and then the 9 before they part. That is 8." |
| 9 or 10 | slip | "The ninth significant digit already differs: 6 against 5." |
| anything else | (otherwise) | "Line the two numbers up and count the leading digits that agree." |

- **Hints.** Nudge: "Line up the two values digit by digit." Point: underlines the agreeing digits. Step: "0.4999999969 and 0.4999999958 agree in their first 8 significant digits. The relative error is $\frac{0.0000000026}{0.5} = 5.2 \times 10^{-9}$, so $-\log_{10}$ gives 8.3."
- **Why?** Yours: "8 correct digits: half of what a double holds." Then: "At $x = 10^{-6}$ only 4 survive, and at $10^{-8}$ none."

---

**Screen 5. Which step lost them?** (`trap`, id `blame`, trap `mc.numerics.subtraction-inexact`, bait option A)

- **Prose:** "The computation has four steps: round $x$ to a double, compute $\cos x$ and round it, subtract from 1, divide by $x^2$."
- **Widget:** `float-inspector` showing each step's exact result, the double it is stored as, and the absolute and relative error of that step alone, at $x = 10^{-4}$. Hidden until Check: the row for the subtraction shows "exact: 0 error".
- **Question:** "Which step made the error that ruined $f(10^{-4})$?" (`choice`)

| Option | Correct? | Misconception | Feedback |
|---|---|---|---|
| A. The subtraction $1 - \cos x$ (the bait) | no | `mc.numerics.subtraction-inexact` | "Look at the subtraction's own error on the canvas: zero. Two doubles within a factor of 2 of each other subtract exactly (Sterbenz's lemma). The subtraction is where the damage shows, not where it was done." |
| B. Rounding $\cos x$ to the nearest double | yes | | "Yes. That rounding moved $\cos x$ by $2.6 \times 10^{-17}$: a relative error of $2.6 \times 10^{-17}$, harmless. After the subtraction the same $2.6 \times 10^{-17}$ sits on a result of $5 \times 10^{-9}$: a relative error of $5.2 \times 10^{-9}$." |
| C. Dividing by $x^2$ | no | `mc.numerics.division-loses` | "Division is correctly rounded: it adds at most one rounding, $1.1 \times 10^{-16}$ relative. It cannot lose 8 digits." |
| D. Squaring $x$ | no | `mc.numerics.squaring-loses` | "Squaring adds one rounding of at most $1.1 \times 10^{-16}$ relative, like any other operation." |

- **Hints.** Nudge: "Which step's relative error on the canvas is largest before the subtraction?" Point: highlights the rounding of $\cos x$. Step: "Every step but the subtraction adds a relative error below $1.1 \times 10^{-16}$; the subtraction adds none. So the 8 lost digits must already have been there before it, hidden."
- **Why?** Shown: "The rounding of $\cos x$ made an absolute error of $2.6 \times 10^{-17}$. Subtracting from 1 removed the 8 leading digits that 1 and $\cos x$ share, and left that error sitting just 8 digits below the result's leading digit." Then: "This is catastrophic cancellation: the subtraction is exact, and that is exactly the problem."
- **Tutor.** Notes: most learners choose A; the screen exists to be failed once. Avoid: "Sterbenz" before Check.

---

**Screen 6. The rule** (`generalise`, id `rule`)

- **Prose:** "You found that a subtraction keeps the absolute errors of its inputs but divides by a much smaller result. If $a$ and $b$ each carry a relative error up to $u$, the difference carries up to $u\frac{\lvert a\rvert + \lvert b\rvert}{\lvert a - b\rvert}$. When $a \approx b$, about $\log_{10}\frac{\lvert a\rvert}{\lvert a - b\rvert}$ digits are lost."
- **Rule (displayed):** digits lost $\approx \log_{10}\dfrac{\lvert a\rvert}{\lvert a - b\rvert}$; digits left $\approx 16 - $ digits lost.
- **Widget:** `float-inspector` (cancellation) with $x = 10^{-6}$ selected and the outputs hidden.
- **Question:** "At $x = 10^{-6}$, $1 - \cos x \approx 5 \times 10^{-13}$. Predict the number of correct digits of the computed $f$."
- **Answer input:** `numeric-input`.
- **Correct:** 4 (lost $\log_{10}\frac1{5 \times 10^{-13}} = 12.3$; left about 3.7; the canvas then shows 4.1). Accepted: 3 to 5.

| Wrong answer | Misconception | Feedback |
|---|---|---|
| 12 | `mc.numerics.lost-for-left` | "12 is the number of digits lost. How many does that leave from about 16?" |
| 0 | `mc.numerics.all-or-nothing` | "Not all of them yet: that happens near $10^{-8}$, where $1 - \cos x$ falls below the spacing of doubles near 1." |
| 8 | slip | "8 was the answer at $10^{-4}$. At $10^{-6}$ the two numbers share 4 more digits." |
| anything else | (otherwise) | "Compute $\log_{10}\frac1{5 \times 10^{-13}}$, then subtract it from 16." |

- **Hints.** Nudge: "How many leading digits do 1 and $\cos(10^{-6}) = 0.9999999999995$ share?" Point: highlights the shared nines. Step: "$\log_{10}(2 \times 10^{12}) = 12.3$ digits lost; $16 - 12.3 \approx 4$ left."
- **Why?** Yours: "About 4 digits left, and the canvas shows 4.1." Then: "The rule is an estimate: it can be a digit off either way, because the actual rounding error can be anywhere up to $u$. Its job is to say when to worry."

---

**Screen 7. Fixing it** (`derive`, id `fix`; the lesson's worked example with a decision point)

- **Leap.** Naive: keep the formula and fight the rounding, by doing the subtraction in higher precision or by choosing a larger $x$. Fails: the subtraction was already exact (screen 5), so more precision there changes nothing; the error entered when $\cos x$ was rounded, and a larger $x$ answers a different question. Points to: rewrite $1 - \cos x$ as an expression that never subtracts nearly equal rounded numbers.
- **Prose:** "Three proposed fixes for $f(10^{-8})$. Decide which can work, then complete the one that does."
- **Widget:** `derivation-steps` in fill mode, each line with its reason in the margin:
  1. Proposal 1: compute $1 - \cos x$ in quadruple precision after `math.cos` returns. **[slot A: works or fails]**
  2. Proposal 2: use $x = 10^{-4}$ instead. **[fails: a different question]** (shown)
  3. Proposal 3: use the identity $\cos x = \cos^2\frac x2 - \sin^2\frac x2$ and $1 = \cos^2\frac x2 + \sin^2\frac x2$.
  4. Subtracting: $1 - \cos x = $ **[slot B]**.
  5. So $f(x) = \frac{2\sin^2(x/2)}{x^2}$, and at $x = 10^{-8}$ it prints 0.5; at $10^{-4}$, 0.4999999995833334, correct to all 16 digits.
- **Question:** "Decide proposal 1, and complete line 4."
- **Answer input:** slot A is a `choice` ("works", "fails"); slot B is an `expression-input`.
- **Correct:** slot A, fails; slot B, $2\sin^2\frac x2$ (accepted forms: `2*sin(x/2)^2` and equivalents, checked by the CAS at probe points).

| Wrong answer | Misconception | Feedback |
|---|---|---|
| Slot A: works | `mc.numerics.more-precision-fixes` | "`math.cos` has already returned exactly 1.0 for $x = 10^{-8}$. Subtracting in any precision gives 0: the information is gone." |
| Slot B: $\sin^2\frac x2$ | slip | "Subtracting $\cos^2 - \sin^2$ from $\cos^2 + \sin^2$ leaves two copies of $\sin^2$." |
| Slot B: $1 - \cos^2\frac x2 + \sin^2\frac x2$ | `mc.numerics.rewrite-keeps-subtraction` | "That is correct algebra but still subtracts from 1 numbers close to 1. Use the identity for 1 as well." |
| Slot B: $\frac{x^2}2$ | `mc.numerics.truncate-not-rewrite` | "That is the first Taylor term: good for tiny $x$, wrong by $\frac{x^4}{24}$ for larger $x$. Find an exact identity." |
| anything else | (otherwise) | "Subtract line 3's two identities from each other." |

- **Hints.** Nudge: "Write 1 and $\cos x$ in terms of $\frac x2$." Point: highlights line 3. Step: "$(\cos^2 + \sin^2) - (\cos^2 - \sin^2) = 2\sin^2\frac x2$, with no subtraction of nearly equal numbers left."
- **Why?** Yours: "$f(x) = \frac{2\sin^2(x/2)}{x^2}$." Then: "Notice what just happened: the fix was not more care with the arithmetic but a formula whose arithmetic never cancels. `log1p`, `expm1` and Welford's update (lessons 10 and 12) are the same move."
- **Tutor.** Notes: if the learner chooses "works", ask what `math.cos(1e-8)` returned on screen 2. Avoid: giving the identity before slot A is answered.

---

**Screen 8. When subtraction is harmless** (`problem`, id `harmless`; the edge case)

- **Prose:** "Not every subtraction of close numbers is a disaster. Which of these lose accuracy against the value the programmer meant?"
- **Widget:** `choice` in multi-select mode, four cards, each with `float-inspector`'s exact expansion of its inputs available on tap.
- **Question:** "Select every subtraction that loses significant digits."
- **Correct:** (b) and (c).

| Option | Correct? | Misconception | Feedback |
|---|---|---|---|
| (a) `1.5 - 1.25` | no | `mc.numerics.exact-inputs-lose` | "Both inputs are exact doubles and the difference, 0.25, is exact. Subtracting exact inputs loses nothing." |
| (b) `1.0000001 - 1`, meant as $10^{-7}$ | yes | | "The literal 1.0000001 is stored with an error of about $10^{-16}$; after subtracting 1 that error is $5.8 \times 10^{-10}$ of the result: about 7 digits lost, 9 left." |
| (c) `1 - math.cos(1e-4)` | yes | | "Screen 4: 8 digits lost." |
| (d) `100.25 - 100.125` | no | `mc.numerics.exact-inputs-lose` | "Both are binary fractions with short expansions, stored exactly: the result 0.125 is exact." |
| Missing (b) | | `mc.numerics.decimal-literal-exact` | "A decimal literal like 1.0000001 is not exactly representable in binary (0.1 lesson 42), so it is an input with a rounding error." |

- **Hints.** Nudge: "For each card, ask: were the inputs exact before the subtraction?" Point: taps open card (b)'s exact expansion, 1.00000010000000005838672... Step: "(a) and (d) have exact inputs; (b) and (c) have inputs that were rounded, and the subtraction exposes the rounding."
- **Why?** Shown: "Cancellation is harmless when the inputs are exact and catastrophic when they carry earlier error." Then: "That is why a difference of two prices on a tick grid (stored as integers) is safe, and a difference of two computed prices is not."

---

**Screen 9. Transfer: gamma by bumping** (`problem`, id `gamma`, context quant)

- **Prose:** "A desk computes gamma by bumping the share price: $\Gamma \approx \frac{V(S + h) - 2V(S) + V(S - h)}{h^2}$, for a 1-year at-the-money call ($S = K = 100$, $r = 5\%$, $\sigma = 20\%$). The true gamma is 0.018762."
- **Widget:** `value-table` with $h = 1, 0.1, 0.01, 10^{-3}, 10^{-4}, 10^{-5}, 10^{-6}$ and the computed gamma for each, the error column hidden until Check; a `plot-2d` of $\lvert$error$\rvert$ against $h$ on log-log axes appears after Check.
- **Question:** "Which bump gives the most accurate gamma?" (`choice` among the seven values of $h$)
- **Correct:** $h = 0.01$ (error $-2.7 \times 10^{-10}$).

| Wrong answer | Misconception | Feedback |
|---|---|---|
| $h = 10^{-6}$ | `mc.numerics.smaller-step-better` | "At $10^{-6}$ the second difference of the three prices is about $1.9 \times 10^{-14}$, only about ten spacings of the doubles near the price 10.45 ($1.8 \times 10^{-15}$), so rounding in the prices swamps it: gamma comes out as 0.0284, 52% too high." |
| $h = 1$ | `mc.numerics.larger-step-safer` | "A large bump avoids cancellation but pays truncation error, $\frac{h^2}{12}V''''$: here $-2.3 \times 10^{-6}$." |
| $h = 10^{-3}$ or $10^{-4}$ | slip | "Close: the error is already rising again from rounding ($-1.6 \times 10^{-8}$ and $-1.4 \times 10^{-7}$)." |

- **Hints.** Nudge: "Two errors pull in opposite directions as $h$ shrinks. Which grows?" Point: highlights the bottom rows. Step: "Truncation falls like $h^2$; the cancellation error grows like $\frac{u\,V}{h^2}$. They balance near $h \approx u^{1/4} \times 100 \approx 0.01$."
- **Why?** Yours: "$h = 0.01$ gives gamma to 10 digits." Then: "1.3 lesson 27 found the same balance for a first derivative at $h \approx \sqrt u$; a second difference cancels more, so its best $h$ is larger. 2.0a lesson 86 meets the noisy Monte Carlo version."

---

**Screen 10. Summary** (`summary`)

- **Three ideas.**
  1. Subtracting nearly equal numbers is exact, but it exposes the rounding errors its inputs already carried: about $\log_{10}\frac{\lvert a\rvert}{\lvert a - b\rvert}$ digits are lost.
  2. The cure is a formula that never makes the subtraction: an identity such as $1 - \cos x = 2\sin^2\frac x2$, or a library function such as `log1p` or `expm1`. More precision in the subtraction does nothing once the inputs are rounded.
  3. Cancellation of exact inputs is harmless; cancellation of computed or decimal inputs is not. In finite differences it sets the best step size.
- **Formula card (saved to the Notebook).** Digits lost $\approx \log_{10}\frac{\lvert a\rvert}{\lvert a - b\rvert}$. Sterbenz: if $\frac b2 \le a \le 2b$, then $a - b$ is exact. $1 - \cos x = 2\sin^2\frac x2$.
- **Common trap.** "The subtraction made the error": it only revealed it.

---

**Quiz** (5 items, difficulty 2 to 3, one attempt each, no hints; "Why?" after each)

| # | Item | Type | Difficulty | Correct | Diagnosed wrong answers | Why? |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| Q1 | Which expression gives the most accurate value of $1 - \cos x$ at $x = 10^{-9}$ in double precision? | C | 2 | $2\sin^2\frac x2$ (gives $5 \times 10^{-19}$) | `1 - cos(x)` (`mc.numerics.subtraction-inexact`: 0); $\frac{1 - \cos^2x}{1 + \cos x}$ (`mc.numerics.rewrite-keeps-subtraction`: still 0); `1 - cos(x)` with `cos` rounded to float32 first (`mc.numerics.precision-reduction-fix`: 0) | "Only the identity avoids subtracting from 1 a number that rounds to 1." |
| Q2 | $a = 1.0002$ and $b = 1.0001$ each carry a relative error of about $10^{-16}$. About how many significant digits does $a - b$ keep? | N | 2 | 12 (accepted 11 to 13) | 16 (`mc.numerics.double-means-16`); 4 (`mc.numerics.lost-for-left`: 4 is the number lost); 8 (`mc.numerics.halve-digits`) | "$\log_{10}\frac{1.0002}{0.0001} \approx 4$ digits lost, so about 12 remain; the computed difference is $9.999999999998899 \times 10^{-5}$." |
| Q3 | In double precision, $\sqrt{10^{12} + 1} - \sqrt{10^{12}}$ prints $5.00003807 \times 10^{-7}$; the true value is $4.99999999999875 \times 10^{-7}$. How many significant digits are correct? | N | 3 | 5 | 16 (`mc.numerics.double-means-16`); 12 (`mc.numerics.lost-for-left`); 0 (`mc.numerics.all-or-nothing`) | "The relative error is $7.6 \times 10^{-6}$, about 5 digits. The rewrite $\frac1{\sqrt{x + 1} + \sqrt x}$ gives all 16." |
| Q4 | Select every rewrite that removes catastrophic cancellation for small $x > 0$ | M | 3 | $\log(1 + x) \to$ `log1p(x)`; $e^x - 1 \to$ `expm1(x)`; $\sqrt{1 + x} - 1 \to \frac x{\sqrt{1 + x} + 1}$ | "Compute $1 - \cos x$ after rounding $\cos x$ to float32" (`mc.numerics.precision-reduction-fix`) | "Each correct rewrite computes the small quantity directly instead of as a difference of two numbers near 1." |
| Q5 | In two sentences: why does computing $1 - \cos x$ in quadruple precision, after `math.cos` has returned a double, not fix the cancellation? | W | 3 | Rubric (3): the error is already in the rounded $\cos x$ (1); the subtraction of the rounded values is exact in any precision, so the same absolute error remains, now relative to a tiny result (1); only a more accurate $\cos x$ itself, or a rewrite, helps (1) | Bertie's suggested mark beside the learner's own (`AI-TUTOR.md` 2.7) | "Precision after the rounding cannot recover what the rounding removed." |

The quiz creates the FSRS cards for `skl.numerics.cancellation-diagnosis` and `skl.numerics.stable-rewrite` (`ASSESSMENT.md` 5.3); 4 of 5 or better on the first completion marks the lesson Passed, and any wrong item joins the Redo queue as a fresh generated variant at the same level (`gen.numerics.cancellation-rewrite`, `gen.numerics.digits-lost`).

**Verification of this lesson's numbers** (section 6, rules 1 and 2): `math.cos(1e-8) == 1.0` in CPython and in the app's `dmath`, and the correctly rounded value from mpmath is 1.0 (the true value $1 - 5 \times 10^{-17}$ lies within half a spacing of 1); the computed and true $f$ at $x = 10^{-1}$ to $10^{-8}$ (digits kept 14.0, 12.5, 10.8, 8.3, 7.1, 4.1, 3.1, 0), against 50-digit references; the stored $\cos(10^{-4})$'s error $2.6 \times 10^{-17}$ and the exactness of $1 - \operatorname{fl}(\cos 10^{-4})$ in rational arithmetic; the identity's outputs (0.5 at $10^{-8}$, 0.4999999995833334 at $10^{-4}$); $1.5 - 1.25$ and $100.25 - 100.125$ exact; `1.0000001 - 1` $= 1.0000000005838672 \times 10^{-7}$; the gamma table (errors $-2.30 \times 10^{-6}$, $-2.30 \times 10^{-8}$, $-2.68 \times 10^{-10}$, $-1.56 \times 10^{-8}$, $-1.36 \times 10^{-7}$, $-3.69 \times 10^{-6}$, $+9.66 \times 10^{-3}$); the quiz values ($5 \times 10^{-19}$, $9.999999999998899 \times 10^{-5}$, $5.00003807246685 \times 10^{-7}$ against $4.99999999999875 \times 10^{-7}$). The bump-and-revalue table uses `scipy.stats.norm.cdf`, whose last digits could differ from the app's `stats-core`; CI recomputes it in both and requires the displayed errors to agree to 2 significant figures, which the item needs.

---

## 8. New widgets this item needs

All ten are **P2**: `docs/WIDGETS.md` 1.5 sets a widget's priority by its first curriculum use, and 2.0a and 2.0b are Stage 2 items (request 4 adds them to the P2 line). All ten are **scenes** (`docs/PLAN.md` 3.2): each is a typed data description over primitives plus pure model functions in `@zq/models/numerics` or `@zq/models/optim` (section 8.3), and none meets a bespoke reason of rule 3. None takes free text or code (drills are `code-cell`, decision 8); none runs a clock-driven loop (a Monte Carlo run is a seeded batch whose replay is a function of the path count, not of time); none owns a service with a state machine (a simplex pivot, a branch-and-bound node or a grid time step is a pure function of the config and the list of choices the learner has made, as 2.1b argues for its tape); and every gesture is a `drag-point`, `slider`, `stepper` or selection. Every number shown or graded comes from `derive` (rule 2) and has a Python parity test against NumPy or SciPy; no widget runs Python, so each behaves identically in the Artifact and on the web app (owner decision 5). No widget grades a simulated estimate (decision 10). They are built in the first P2 waves, after the engine and every P0 batch (owner decision 1): 2.0a's six first, because 2.0a is the first Stage 2 item, then 2.0b's four. Proposed clusters `numerics` (2.0a's six) and `optim` (2.0b's four) [our choice]; `docs/WIDGETS.md` decides.

### 8.1 The ten widgets

| Order | Id | Purpose | Tier, kind | First use | Size | Composes | Answers |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | `backward-error` | An input pane and an output pane side by side. Conditioning mode: a relative perturbation ball of radius $\delta$ around $x$ and its image under $f$, with the stretch $\kappa$ read off and compared with $\left\lvert\frac{xf'(x)}{f(x)}\right\rvert$. Polynomial mode: a polynomial's computed values (Horner in binary64) as a noisy band around the exact curve near a multiple root, with the backward error (the smallest coefficient perturbation that makes the computed value exact) beside the forward error. Recurrence mode: forward and backward runs of a recurrence in a table with the error multiplied at each step | composite, scene | 2.0a lesson 7 (also 8, 13) | M | `plot-2d` (two panes), `marks` (`ball`, `band`, `arrow`, `scatter`), `slider`, `value-table`, `numeric-input`, `choice` | `numeric`, `choice`, `point` |
| 2 | `convergence-plot` | Error against step size, iteration count or work on semilog or log-log axes, for one or several methods; a fitted slope over a draggable window (order of convergence, or digits gained per iteration on semilog axes); reference lines of slope $-\frac12$, $-1$, $-2$ and $-4$; a work mode (error against evaluations, flops or measured time) for comparing methods at equal cost; a predict mode in which the learner drags a slope line before the data appear. Series come from `@zq/models` runs or build-time fixtures, never from a live random draw that is graded. **The base of 2.1b's `scaling-plot`**, which becomes a preset adding an irreducible-floor fit and an extrapolation band (`stage2-deep-learning.md` 8.1, item 7) | composite, scene | 2.0a lesson 11 (also 14 to 16, 19, 25, 30, 33, 40, 41, 45, 46, 57, 59, 61, 65, 73, 74, 83, 90; 2.0b lessons 37, 39, 40, 43) | M | `plot-2d` (log axes), `marks` (`curve`, `scatter`, `line`), `drag-point`, `slider`, `value-table`, `numeric-input`, `choice` | `numeric`, `point`, `choice` |
| 3 | `interp-lab` | Nodes on a function and the interpolant through them: the monomial (Vandermonde, with its condition number), Lagrange and barycentric forms; the error curve and the node polynomial $\prod(x - x_i)$; the Runge preset $\frac1{1 + 25x^2}$ on equispaced nodes; Chebyshev nodes with a degree slider; piecewise linear and cubic splines (natural, clamped, not-a-knot) with draggable data showing local against global influence; a curve preset that interpolates zero rates, discount factors or log discount factors and draws the implied forward curve | composite, scene | 2.0a lesson 20 (also 21 to 24) | M | `plot-2d`, `marks` (`curve`, `scatter`, `band`), `drag-point`, `slider`, `matrix`, `value-table`, `numeric-input`, `choice` | `numeric`, `point`, `curve`, `choice`, `expression` |
| 4 | `fd-grid` | A finite-difference grid in $(x, \tau)$ or $(S, \tau)$ with the stencil drawn at a chosen node. Domain mode: truncation at $S_{\max}$ and the boundary and terminal conditions. Method-of-lines mode: the second-difference matrix's eigenvalues on the real axis. Explicit mode with an $r$ slider, the amplification factor $g(\theta)$ and the step at which the highest mode overtakes the solution; implicit and Crank-Nicolson modes, with a gamma overlay that shows Crank-Nicolson's oscillation at the strike and the Rannacher start that removes it; a Black-Scholes mode with error against the closed form; payoff smoothing (strike on a node, midway, cell-averaged); Greeks from the grid; American mode (projected SOR, the free boundary drawn); barrier mode (barrier on a node, and the $0.5826\,\sigma\sqrt{\Delta t}$ shift for discrete monitoring) | composite, scene; if `scene.ts` passes 400 lines (`docs/PLAN.md` 3.2 rule 4), the American and barrier modes split into presets over the Black-Scholes mode | 2.0a lesson 53 (also 55 to 64) | L | `plot-2d`, `plot-3d` (the surface $V(S, \tau)$), `marks` (`cells`, `stencil`, `curve`), `slider`, `stepper`, `value-table`, `numeric-input`, `choice` | `numeric`, `choice`, `point`, `state` |
| 5 | `rng-lab` | What a generator is. LCG mode with a small modulus: successive pairs and triples as a lattice, RANDU's 15 planes found by rotating in 3-D. Streams mode: PCG64 and Philox states, spawned streams against consecutive seeds, with an overlap check. Inverse mode: $\Phi^{-1}(U)$ for $U$ on the double grid, with the tail limits it can reach. Rejection mode: the polar method's acceptance region and rate. Point-set mode: plain, stratified and Latin hypercube samples. QMC mode: van der Corput digit reversal, Halton (with the correlated pairs of large prime bases), Sobol, and a discrepancy readout | composite, scene | 2.0a lesson 66 (also 67 to 69, 80, 82 to 84) | M | `plot-2d`, `plot-3d`, `marks` (`scatter`, `cells`, `region`), `slider`, `stepper`, `value-table`, `numeric-input`, `choice` | `numeric`, `choice`, `set`, `point` |
| 6 | `mc-variance` | A Monte Carlo estimator race on common seeds: the running estimate with its 95% band against $N$, the sample variance, cost per path, and efficiency (variance times cost) in a table, with the variance ratio against plain Monte Carlo from a closed form wherever one exists. Modes: single estimator; efficiency; antithetic (the pair and its correlation); control (the scatter of payoff against control, the fitted $b$ and $\rho^2$); importance (the shifted density and a histogram of likelihood-ratio weights); stratified (allocation by stratum); conditional; RQMC (the spread of randomised replicates); Greeks (bump with and without common random numbers against $h$, pathwise, likelihood ratio); LSM (the regression of continuation values at one exercise date and the boundary it implies) | composite, scene; runs above $2 \times 10^5$ paths go to a Web Worker that calls the same pure model [our choice] | 2.0a lesson 71 (also 72, 75 to 81, 84 to 89) | L | `plot-2d`, `marks` (`curve`, `band`, `scatter`, `hist`), `sim-runner`, `slider`, `value-table`, `numeric-input`, `choice` | `numeric`, `choice`, `interval` |
| 7 | `feasible-region` | Linear programmes in two variables: constraints as draggable half-planes, the feasible polygon, the objective's level line and gradient. Basis mode: each vertex labelled with its basic and non-basic variables and slacks. Convex-combination mode: a point inside written as a mix of vertices. Simplex mode: the vertex walk beside the tableau, with the learner choosing the entering variable. Phase-one mode; Klee-Minty mode on the cube in 3-D; lattice mode: integer points and the branch-and-bound tree; central-path mode: the log-barrier minimisers as $t$ grows. All pivots in exact rational arithmetic | composite, scene | 2.0b lesson 1 (also 2 to 5, 7, 9 to 11, 15, 45) | L | `plot-2d`, `plot-3d`, `marks` (`half-plane`, `polygon`, `line`, `path`), `drag-point`, `slider`, `stepper`, `matrix` (tableau), `node-link` (branch and bound), `value-table`, `numeric-input`, `choice` | `point`, `numeric`, `choice`, `matrix`, `ordering`, `state` |
| 8 | `dual-pair` | A primal and its dual side by side. Bound mode: multiplier sliders build a bound from a combination of constraints. Gap mode: primal and dual values closing to equality. Slackness mode: each constraint's slack paired with its multiplier. Sensitivity mode: the optimal value as a piecewise-linear function of one right-hand side, each segment's slope a shadow price, each kink a basis change. Cone mode: Farkas' cone of columns and the separating certificate. State-prices mode: a one-period market, its state prices as the dual, an arbitrage when they cannot be found. Nonlinear mode: the concave dual function $g(\lambda)$ of a convex programme, its maximum and Slater's condition. Portfolio mode: every KKT multiplier of a constrained portfolio with a re-solve check. SVM mode: dual coefficients on the points, support vectors marked | composite, scene | 2.0b lesson 12 (also 13 to 17, 30, 31, 34, 52, 56) | L | `plot-2d`, `marks` (`curve`, `cone`, `half-plane`, `bars`), `slider`, `drag-point`, `matrix`, `value-table`, `numeric-input`, `choice` | `numeric`, `matrix`, `choice`, `point`, `state` |
| 9 | `convex-set` | Sets in the plane from presets (half-planes, balls, norm balls, polytopes, the long-only budget simplex, a cardinality set, a minimum-lot set, sublevel sets) with the segment test: drag two points in the set and the segment is highlighted where it leaves. Intersection mode (and affine images and sums); separation mode: a draggable hyperplane between two sets, and a supporting hyperplane at a boundary point; projection mode: drag a point and see its Euclidean projection with the obtuse-angle condition, including projection onto the simplex | composite, scene | 2.0b lesson 18 (also 19, 21, 22, 29, 42) | M | `plot-2d`, `marks` (`region`, `segment`, `line`, `point`), `drag-point`, `card-sort`, `value-table`, `numeric-input`, `choice` | `point`, `choice`, `set`, `numeric`, `state` |
| 10 | `frontier-lab` | The constrained efficient frontier. Embeds `portfolio-lab`'s $(\sigma, \mu)$ plane and adds what that P1 widget does not have: a constraint editor (budget, long-only, caps, sector limits) whose frontier kinks at corner portfolios; tangency mode (maximising the Sharpe ratio by the rescaled convex problem); a weights bar chart; sensitivity mode (one asset's expected return on a slider and the weights swinging); turnover mode (current weights, a turnover budget, the trades kept ranked by gain per unit of turnover). Every point solved by the active-set QP in `@zq/models/optim` | composite, scene | 2.0b lesson 28 (also 33, 34, 48 to 51) | M | `plot-2d`, `marks` (`curve`, `scatter`, `bars`), `slider`, `drag-point`, `value-table`, `numeric-input`, `choice`; embeds `portfolio-lab` | `point`, `numeric`, `choice`, `state` |

**Rejected: `frontier-lab` as new modes of `portfolio-lab`.** `portfolio-lab` is a P1 widget for 1.7 that already embeds `regression-lab` and `lagrange-explorer`; a constraint editor, a QP and a turnover view would carry it past rule 4's 400-line cap and put P2 code in a P1 batch. Embedding its plane (rule 5) reuses the drawing without the cost.

**AI hooks, in brief.** `backward-error`: targets `ball:input`, `ball:output`, `point:computed`, `point:exact`, `slider:delta`, `row:<n>`; describe, e.g. "f(x) = x − 1 at x = 1.000001; relative input radius 1e-10 gives relative output radius 1.0e-4; κ about 1.0e6"; secret: $\kappa$ and the backward error; tryable `slider:delta`. `convergence-plot`: targets `series:<method>`, `window`, `line:slope`, `ref:<slope>`, `marker:predict`; secret: the fitted slope while a prediction is open; tryable `window`. `interp-lab`: targets `node:<i>`, `curve:interpolant`, `curve:error`, `slider:n`, `chip:<form>`; secret: the maximum error. `fd-grid`: targets `node:<i>,<j>`, `stencil`, `slider:r`, `curve:g`, `boundary:<side>`, `curve:gamma`, `curve:exercise`; secret: the reference price and the stable limit; tryable `slider:r`. `rng-lab`: targets `plane:<k>`, `point:<i>`, `stream:<s>`, `slider:n`, `chip:<sequence>`; secret: the lattice's plane count. `mc-variance`: targets `series:<estimator>`, `band:<estimator>`, `row:<estimator>`, `slider:N`, `toggle:<method>`, `scatter:control`; secret: the closed-form variance ratio; tryable `slider:N`, `toggle:<method>`. `feasible-region`: targets `constraint:<i>`, `vertex:<i>`, `line:objective`, `arrow:gradient`, `tableau:<r>,<c>`, `node:<k>`; secret: the optimal vertex and the next pivot; tryable `slider:objective`. `dual-pair`: targets `slider:y<i>`, `value:primal`, `value:dual`, `row:<i>`, `segment:<k>`, `cone`, `curve:g`; secret: the dual optimum. `convex-set`: targets `set:<k>`, `handle:a`, `handle:b`, `segment`, `hyperplane`, `point:projection`; secret: whether the set is convex in a sorting item. `frontier-lab`: targets `curve:frontier`, `point:<portfolio>`, `bar:<asset>`, `slider:mu<i>`, `slider:turnover`, `constraint:<k>`; secret: the optimal weights while hidden; tryable `slider:mu<i>`. Every widget's table view carries every value its canvas shows, and colour is never the only carrier of meaning (`docs/WIDGETS.md` 1.4).

### 8.3 Services, and widgets whose first use moves earlier

**Services.**

- **`@zq/models/numerics`** (new). Root finders (bisection, Newton, secant, Brent, the safeguarded implied-volatility solver); interpolation (barycentric, Chebyshev nodes, natural, clamped and not-a-knot splines); quadrature (trapezoid, Simpson, Romberg, tabulated Gauss-Legendre, adaptive Simpson); dense and banded linear algebra at the sizes screens use (LU with partial pivoting and its growth factor, Cholesky, Thomas, QR, power, inverse and Rayleigh quotient iteration, Jacobi, Gauss-Seidel, SOR, CG); the θ-scheme on a uniform grid with Rannacher steps, projected SOR and barrier boundaries; LCG, PCG64 (BigInt) and the architecture's Philox4x32-10; the inverse normal; van der Corput, Halton and Sobol (Joe and Kuo direction numbers) with a random digital shift; the Monte Carlo estimators of course MO. Parity tests against NumPy and SciPy on fixed inputs: deterministic functions agree to $10^{-12}$ relative or to the stated ulps; PCG64 and Philox agree bit for bit with NumPy's bit generators from the same state [our choice; the test proves it].
- **`@zq/models/optim`** (new). An exact rational simplex (Bland's rule and largest coefficient) with Phase I, dual values and sensitivity ranges; branch and bound for two-variable integer programmes; an active-set QP for at most 10 variables returning KKT multipliers (`frontier-lab`, `dual-pair` portfolio mode); projection onto the simplex by sorting; gradient descent with backtracking, Newton, BFGS, ISTA, projected gradient, heavy ball, Nesterov and seeded SGD; the log-barrier path for small LPs; the SVM dual for at most 20 points. Parity against `scipy.optimize.linprog` (HiGHS) and, in CI on CPython, cvxpy with Clarabel; the browser never needs cvxpy for a lesson.
- **`@zq/mathcore`** additions: float16 and bfloat16 rounding from a double (round to nearest, ties to even, float16 subnormals), `nextUp`, `nextDown`, `ulp`, and the exact rational value of a double. 2.1b's `float-inspector` config uses the same functions.

**First uses that move earlier.** Each stays P2; each moves into the 2.0a or 2.0b wave.

| Widget or mode | Catalogue's first use | New first use | Note |
|---|---|---|---|
| `heat-diffusion` | 3.2 lesson 45 | 2.0a lesson 49 | Its `finite-difference` mode (3.2 lesson 88) embeds `fd-grid` instead of building a second solver |
| `path-simulator` `bridge` mode | 3.2 lesson 39 | 2.0a lesson 75 (also 85) | |
| `sde-stepper` | 2.2 lesson 40 | 2.0a lesson 73 | |
| `pricer-compare`, with its `pde` method | 2.3 lessons 160 and 164 | 2.0a lesson 65 | |
| `iv-solver` algorithm mode | 2.3 lesson 142 | 2.0a lesson 18 | Embeds `root-finder` (`brent`) |
| `descent-lab` momentum mode | 2.1 lesson 112 | 2.0b lesson 43 | |
| `descent-lab` stochastic mode | 2.1 lesson 19 | 2.0b lesson 44 | |
| `classifier-plane` | 2.1 lesson 3 | 2.0b lesson 21 (also 55, 56) | Size L; 2.0b lesson 21 needs only its scatter and boundary marks, so its first build can ship the linear models and add the rest for 2.1 |
| `loss-lab` | 2.1 lesson 11 | 2.0b lesson 41 | |
| `regularisation-path` | 2.1 lesson 31 | 2.0b lesson 54 | |
| `penalty-geometry` | 2.1 lesson 47 | 2.0b lesson 54 | |
| `execution-lab` Almgren-Chriss frontier mode | P2, no lesson named in `docs/WIDGETS.md` | 2.0b lesson 53 | The widget is P1 (1.7); only the mode moves |

**Proposed `docs/WIDGETS.md` 7.4 lines.** **2.0a** (9): `backward-error`, `convergence-plot`, `interp-lab`, `heat-diffusion`, `fd-grid`, `pricer-compare`, `rng-lab`, `mc-variance`, `sde-stepper`. **2.0b** (8): `feasible-region`, `dual-pair`, `convex-set`, `classifier-plane`, `frontier-lab`, `loss-lab`, `regularisation-path`, `penalty-geometry`. The 2.1 line falls from 34 to 30, 2.2 from 5 to 4, 2.3 from 13 to 12 and 3.2 from 6 to 5.
